Arizona Cannabis & Hemp Research — AUDITED

Last audited: 31 October 2026. This is an independent audit and corrected rewrite of the attached complete Arizona cannabis & hemp report, Parts 0–12 (research date 31 October 2026). The auditor did not write the original report. Part 1 below is the audit table. Part 2 is everything still unverified, with where to confirm it. Part 3 is the full rewritten report, with every correction from the audit applied in place — every section and all detail from the original are kept; only what the audit changed has been changed.

Federal baseline — used as given, not re-audited. Scheduling is split: under a final rule at 91 FR 22714 (effective 28 April 2026), FDA-approved cannabis products and state-licensed MEDICAL marijuana are in Schedule III; all other marijuana, including all adult-use, remains Schedule I; the broader 2024 rescheduling proposal is NOT final. IRS 280E: Treasury has said rescheduling generally removes 280E as a bar for businesses no longer dealing in Schedule I/II substances (guidance forthcoming); adult-use activity remains fully subject to 280E. Hemp: Public Law 119-37 §781 takes legal effect 12 November 2026, but Public Law 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026 to only the provisions excluding cannabinoids that are not naturally produced or are synthesized; the full total-THC definition and 0.4 mg per container cap apply after 11 December 2026. USDA has delayed enforcement of the DEA-registered-laboratory hemp testing requirement until 31 December 2026. The federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider is in force through 11 December 2026. No federal cannabis banking law has been enacted. Any statement in the original that contradicted this baseline has been corrected to match it. The rewritten report applies Schedule III and the 280E change to medical activity only, explains how dual licensees (nonprofit medical dispensary plus marijuana establishment) split their activity, and treats all adult-use activity as Schedule I.

How to read the verdicts. Confirmed = the claim matches a primary source opened in this audit (Arizona Revised Statutes / azleg.gov, Arizona Administrative Code / Administrative Register, ADHS, AZDA, ADOR, Arizona State Treasurer, Attorney General opinions, Secretary of State, Arizona courts, official tribal government sources and codes, official county/city/town codes). Corrected = the claim was wrong or incomplete and the corrected wording in the table is the wording used in the rewritten report. Removed = wrong with no correct version found — none in this audit. Unverified = only a secondary source exists, the primary source was not opened directly (including Cornell/Justia reproductions of A.A.C. text, which are treated as mirrors, not primaries), or the claim is an absence finding that cannot be proved from the sources opened. A claim marked Unverified in the audit table keeps its Verify / Unavailable flag in the rewritten report. Do not upgrade a search excerpt, a dashboard summary, or a review-site figure into a confirmed figure.

PART 1 — AUDIT TABLE

Audit scope and counts. The report was audited section by section in six parallel tracks (Federal baseline + Parts 0–1; Parts 2–3; Parts 4–6; Parts 7–8; Parts 9–10; Parts 11–12), which returned 233 rows. After grouping repeated and confirmed claim clusters for publication — notably the nine Part 8 properties into one row and multi-claim Part 9/12 rows into single rows — the published audit table below contains 192 rows, whose verdicts sum exactly: Confirmed 77, Corrected 47, Removed 0, Unverified 68 (77 + 47 + 0 + 68 = 192). No claim was removed outright: every wrong variant found (for example I24-003, the 30 June / 1 July conflict as an unresolved flag, the $2,500,000-per-year SB 1299 figure, the 8.6% current Phoenix rate, the 7-lab ADHS count, the “annual” renewal, and the 0.8 total-CBD factor) has a correct version in the table and in the rewritten report, so it is Corrected, not Removed. Two audit-track tallies needed reconciliation at assembly and are reconciled here, not hidden: (1) the Parts 11–12 track tabled 40 rows with a 22/4/14/0 tally but carried a fifth correction (PO/BM disclosure) inside an Unverified row — in the published table that correction is its own Corrected row; (2) the Parts 9–10 track left the lab count “unresolved” and the Parts 0–1 track left it “unresolved”, but the Parts 4–6 track resolved it to 8 labs as of 1 August 2026 from the ADHS July 2026 report, which is the most authoritative official source opened — the published table therefore records the lab count as Corrected/resolved, and the August 7-lab figure as secondary-only. Likewise, the Parts 11–12 track marked visiting-patient purchase as Unverified on a secondary source only, but the Parts 0–1 track confirmed it from azleg §36-2804.03(C) — the azleg opening wins and the published table records it as Confirmed.

What the audit changed most materially. (1) Phoenix TPT resolved to 9.1% current (8.6% pre-1 July 2025) and Mesa to 8.3% (8.05% superseded), with the worked $100 example corrected to $25.10 adult-use / $9.10 medical in Phoenix. (2) HB 2179: session-law wording “effective from and after June 30, 2026” (azleg summaries: 1 July 2026), three-fourths vote satisfied (House 51-3-6, Senate 28-1-1), and the $20,000 §36-2859 penalty confined to non-establishments/non-dispensaries. (3) Lab count resolved to 8 (ADHS, 1 Aug 2026); establishment renewal corrected to biennial; medical laboratory term confirmed at 2 years (§36-2804.06(A)). (4) AG Opinion I24-005 (R24-001) — I24-003 deleted. (5) ADHS succession corrected: Cunico (resignation announced 30 Apr 2025) → Sjolander (interim) → Johnston (appointed 24 Nov 2025, signed as Director 27 Mar 2026; Senate confirmation Verify). (6) Delivery live date (1 Nov 2024) and $10,000 trip cap downgraded from “confirmed in R9-18-312.01” to secondary-summary / Verify against the official A.A.C. text — the original research note claiming confirmation is not carried forward as a confirmation. (7) DUI: §36-2852(B) impairment requirement added; “no impairment proof required” deleted for marijuana; Dobson treated as a pre-Prop 207 holding. (8) Total CBD factor resolved to 0.877; 0.8 discarded. (9) Hemp: §3-314(E) two-year renewal and §3-319 penalties (≤$5,000/violation) resolved; 2-year fees completed (Transporter $200, Processor $4,000, on the 2022 AZDA form — currency vs current Table 1 still to be checked); Feb 2024 hemp roster corrected to 48 licence-type entries, not 92. (10) Local rules: Marana “Town of Maricopa” corrected to “town of Marana”; Flagstaff §6-10-001-0006 delivery attribution deleted (that section is consumption in transportation; the delivery text is from a sample ordinance); Prescott Valley §10-07-050 delivery prohibition removed (section Reserved after Ord. 2025-962, 9 Oct 2025); Prescott Valley §13-08-040 buffer list completed (a)–(f); Yuma hours conflict flagged, not presented as a single current hours rule; medical 500-foot buffer childcare/preschool limbs attributed to SB 1105 (2025), not Prop 203 alone. (11) Tribal: 22-tribe count confirmed, enumeration Verify; compact wording softened to “none found”; SRPMIC dates and Ordinance SRO-576-2024 verbatim text confirmed; GRIC permit regime (from 1 Mar 2023) replaces any flat prohibition statement; three USDA tribal hemp plans confirmed; RCH Wellness affirmed on standing only (30 Apr 2026) with the “case closed 4 June 2026” claim deleted. (12) SB 1299 corrected to $2,500,000 per distribution ($5,000,000/year), failed three-fourths vote, not law. (13) §43-108 scope narrowed to establishments, testing facilities and for-profit dual licensees; nonprofit-only dispensaries fall under §43-1201(B). (14) No statewide rural licence ceiling exists in §36-2854(A)(1)(c); the ~143 ceiling is rejected and the 13-vs-12 rural figure is downgraded to secondary-only. (15) Expungement: the official statewide 2025–2026 total remains Unavailable; Maricopa-only and secondary figures must never be summed into a statewide total.

Part 0.4 / 2.6Arizona §43-108 decoupling scope (medical only vs both channels) and post-Apr 2026 effectCorrectedhttps://www.azleg.gov/ars/43/01201.htm§43-108 as quoted covers establishments, testing facilities and for-profit dual licensees only — it does not by its terms cover a nonprofit-only dispensary, which instead falls under the §43-1201(B) exemption (and must file Form 99M per ADOR). Do not describe §43-108 as covering all medical activity. A dual licensee that elects for-profit operation does not qualify for a subtraction for the medical portion; a dual licensee that has not elected for-profit operation may subtract the medical portion and must add back a medical-portion loss included in federal AGI. Schedule DFE / live Form 140 wording remains Verify against the live ADOR form (reproduced form text only was read).

Part 2.9Worked example: $100 adult-use sale in a jurisdiction with 8.6% combined TPT = $16.00 excise + $8.60 TPT = $24.60; $100 medical sale = $8.60 TPT, no excise; 8.6% described as “a contested Phoenix figure”Correctedhttps://azdor.gov/model-city-tax-code/city-profile/phoenixOn a $100 adult-use sale in Phoenix at the current ordinary retail combined TPT rate of 9.1% (see Part 3 row), tax = $16.00 excise + $9.10 TPT = $25.10, because TPT is on the $100 price, excise excluded from the TPT base (§42-5452(D)). A $100 medical sale in Phoenix pays $9.10 TPT and no excise. The 8.6% figure was Phoenix’s pre-1 July 2025 combined rate (5.6% + 0.7% + 2.3%); do not use 8.6% as a current Phoenix rate or as a statewide rate.

Attribute the expanded buffer to SB 1105, not to Prop 203 alone, and verify SB 1105’s chapter number and House vote on azleg.gov before publication.

The report’s list stops at (d) and must be completed with (e)–(f).

Part 3.7Phoenix combined TPT: report preserves a direct conflict, 9.1% (5.6% + 0.7% + 2.8%) vs 8.6% (5.6% + 0.7% + 2.3%), plus a two-level threshold structure, and says “flag both and do not choose one”Correctedhttp://azdor.gov/tpt-update-may-2025RESOLVED in favour of 9.1% for ordinary retail: ADOR’s May 2025 TPT Update records that on 18 March 2025 the Phoenix City Council passed Ordinance G-7369 increasing multiple privilege-tax classifications and use tax from 2.3% to 2.8%, effective 1 July 2025. Current ordinary Phoenix combined retail TPT is therefore 5.6% (state) + 0.7% (Maricopa County) + 2.8% (city) = 9.1%; 8.6% is the pre-1 July 2025 rate. The two-level structure survives for single items: the threshold is $14,338 for sales transacted 1 January 2026–31 December 2027 (ADOR/Phoenix guidance), with a lower city rate on the portion above the threshold — verify the above-threshold city rate against the ADOR Phoenix rate table before quoting a Level-2 combined rate.

Part 12.4DUI: §28-1381(A)(3) is a metabolite offence with “no impairment proof required”, and the Dobson affirmative defence is the operative protection for medical patientsCorrectedhttps://codes.findlaw.com/az/title-36-public-health-and-safety/az-rev-st-sect-36-2852/Under §28-1381(A)(3) a drug/metabolite in the body is an offence, BUT §36-2852(B) provides: “a person with metabolites or components of marijuana in the person’s body is guilty of violating §28-1381(A)(3) only if the person is also impaired to the slightest degree.” For marijuana, therefore, (A)(3) guilt now requires slightest-degree impairment; the report’s “no impairment proof required” statement must be deleted for marijuana cases (it remains the bare text of (A)(3) for other drugs).
Part 12.4 / 12.11Dobson v. McClennen, 238 Ariz. 389, 361 P.3d 374 (Ariz. 2015): AMMA does not immunise patients from (A)(3), but gives an affirmative defence (authorised use + concentration insufficient to cause impairment, patient proving insufficiency by a preponderance); Dobson patients failed it and convictions were affirmedCorrectedhttps://neffsedacca.com/arizonas-highest-court-resolves-questions-regarding-dui-convictions-of-medical-marijuana-users-dobson-v-mcclennen-238-ariz-389-2015/ (secondary summary only; opinion not opened)Keep the Dobson summary as a 2015 holding, but add: “Dobson predates Prop 207. Since §36-2852(B), the State must also prove slightest-degree impairment for a marijuana (A)(3) conviction; do not present Dobson burden-on-defendant as the only route to acquittal for marijuana (A)(3) cases, and do not extend Dobson to adult-use consumers.” The report’s citation/date are corroborated by secondary summaries only — the opinion itself was not opened, so the pinpoint citation remains Verify.

Arizona Cannabis & Hemp Research — Complete

Complete file — Parts 0–12. Research date / date accessed for sources in both runs: 31 October 2026, unless a source itself is dated otherwise. Parts 0–6 are Run 1; Parts 7–12 are Run 2, appended after the Run 1 open-items section; the Research Notes covering both runs are at the end of this file. The standalone Run 1 file is preserved separately, so the Run 1 sections below are kept as delivered and Run 2 corrections are stated in the Run 2 text and in the Research Notes, not silently edited into Run 1.

Contents: Part 0 Headlines · Part 1 Laws · Part 2 Taxes · Part 3 Local Rules · Part 4 Licence Types · Part 5 Licence Data Sources · Part 6 Market Data · Run 1 open items · Part 7 Hemp Licences · Part 8 Cannabis-Zoned Properties · Part 9 Regulators and History · Part 10 Tribal and Interstate · Part 11 Operations Rules · Part 12 Consumer and Social Rules · Research Notes (both runs).

Federal baseline — given, not re-researched

Split scheduling: state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714). All adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I. The broader 2024 rescheduling proposal is not final. IRS §280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity; adult-use remains fully subject to §280E. Hemp: PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026, but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026. After 11 December 2026, the full total-THC hemp definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply. USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp is delayed to 31 December 2026. The federal medical marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026. There is no federal cannabis banking law.

Arizona application. Arizona has both medical and adult-use, and most licensees hold both a nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary registration and a marijuana establishment licence (a “dual licensee”). On the given baseline, Schedule III / §280E relief attaches to medical activity only — marijuana handled under the state medical licence/registration (Prop 203, Chapter 28.1). Adult-use activity under Chapter 28.2 remains Schedule I and fully subject to §280E. A dual licensee must therefore split its activity — medical vs adult-use inventory, sales, revenue and deductions — and may not treat adult-use sales as Schedule III merely because the same entity, premises or licence also serves patients. Verify: no Arizona-specific ADHS or Arizona Department of Revenue (ADOR) guidance document on post-28 April 2026 medical/adult-use splitting, accounting or amended-return procedure was located in this research; do not publish a splitting method until ADOR/ADHS guidance is found. Verify: whether any tribal medical programme, if one exists, counts as “state-licensed” for Schedule III purposes — no tribal medical programme was identified in official sources consulted for Parts 0–1 (tribal detail is Part 10 in Run 2).

Method and sourcing limits — read before using any figure

Official sources were preferred throughout: Arizona Revised Statutes and bill pages on azleg.gov, the Arizona Administrative Code, ADHS, the Arizona Department of Agriculture (AZDA), ADOR, the Arizona State Treasurer, the Attorney General, the Secretary of State, Arizona courts, and official city/town/county codes. Secondary sources (news, industry reports, polls) are used only for non-law facts such as market figures and events, are dated, and are labelled as secondary.

Important limitation carried forward: direct fetches of some azleg.gov/ars/36/…htm pages failed in the Parts 0–1 research session (fetch error, not a finding about the law). Statute text in Part 1 is therefore quoted from azleg viewdocument search results, azleg bill/session-law PDFs, and — where the azleg text was only available in snippet form — from mirrors of the same section (Justia/FindLaw/Cornell). Verify each quotation against the current official compilation before publication. The azleg online compilation itself states it is primarily maintained for legislative drafting purposes, the official version is published by Thomson Reuters, and it reflects law through the 57th Legislature, 2nd Regular Session. Parts 2–3 and 4–6 researchers did directly open several azleg pages (notably §§36-2854, 36-2856, 36-2857, 36-2863, 36-2864, 42-5452, 43-108, 36-2804); those openings are noted where they occur. Verify means uncertain, conflicting or not confirmed from a directly opened primary source. Unavailable means no primary source was located by the route stated. Proposed, failed or vetoed bills are never presented as current law. No personal phone numbers or emails of individuals are reproduced. Place names are Arizona places unless stated otherwise: Glendale AZ ≠ Glendale CA, Peoria AZ ≠ Peoria IL, Mesa AZ ≠ Mesa CO, Kingman AZ ≠ Kingman KS, Payson AZ ≠ Payson UT, Winslow AZ ≠ Winslow ME, Douglas AZ ≠ Douglas WY, Yuma AZ ≠ Yuma CO, Prescott AZ ≠ Prescott, Ontario, Nogales AZ ≠ Nogales, Sonora, San Luis AZ ≠ San Luis Río Colorado, and the City of Maricopa ≠ Maricopa County.

PART 0: HEADLINES

0.1 Market: adult-use dominant, medical in long decline, total roughly flat in 2026

  • 2025 taxable sales (secondary sum of official ADOR monthly data, marked as such): $1,224.0 million total — adult-use $1,058.7m, medical $165.3m — down 9.8% from 2024 ($1,357.4m: adult-use $1,106.7m, medical $250.6m); 2023 was $1,462.0m (adult-use $1,114.7m, medical $347.3m). Medical fell 34% in 2025; adult-use slipped 4.3%. January–July 2026 adult-use was 0.4% below the same months of 2025. Source: CannBus, Arizona Cannabis Market Intelligence Report — Q3 2026, data as of 30 September 2026, summing ADOR monthly taxable sales — cannbus.org/cannmir/az_cannmir.html (accessed 31 Oct 2026).
  • Tax collections, official ADOR via press reporting (secondary reporting of official data): January–July 2026 total $173,452,328, up ~2.4% ($4.1m) on $169,349,815 in January–July 2025. July 2026 alone: $23,902,742, of which ~$14.99m marijuana excise tax, ~$9.47m adult-use TPT, and medical TPT negative at ~–$556,500 on adjustments. January–July 2026 split: ~$108.2m excise, ~$59.4m adult-use TPT, ~$5.9m medical. Since adult-use launch, nearly $1.5 billion in marijuana taxes collected. Source: The Marijuana Herald, 12 Aug 2026 — themarijuanaherald.com, 12 Aug 2026.
  • First half 2026: $149,549,586, up 2.3% on $146,181,586 in H1 2025; $93.18m excise, $49.89m adult-use TPT, $6.47m medical TPT; estimated combined sales ~$658m (method: excise base + average TPT rate; local rates and prior-period adjustments affect the estimate). Source: The Marijuana Herald, 10 July 2026 — themarijuanaherald.com, 10 July 2026.
  • May 2026 was the highest monthly total since May 2023: $26,487,451 (medical TPT $1,144,824; adult-use TPT $9,295,134; excise $16,036,626; EFT fees $10,867), up 6.8% on May 2025 ($24.8m). Source: The Marijuana Herald, 14 June 2026 — themarijuanaherald.com, 14 June 2026.
  • Price pressure (secondary, dated): dried flower averaged $5.62/gram (Headset, via The Marijuana Herald, 13 May 2026), “down by more than 50% from previous levels,” even as tax collections held flat — themarijuanaherald.com, 13 May 2026. See Part 6.4 for conflicting price figures — do not use any single price as settled.
  • Medical patients — official ADHS monthly reports: December 2025: 81,221 active qualifying patients; 98,322 total cardholders — ADHS Dec 2025 report (PDF). February 2026: 78,352 / 95,197 — ADHS Feb 2026 (PDF). April 2026: 74,653 / 91,341 — ADHS Apr 2026 (PDF). June 2026: 71,605 / 87,975 — ADHS June 2026 (PDF). July 2026: 70,444 qualifying patients (442 cultivation-approved; 6,600 SNAP-eligible); 86,770 total cardholders — ADHS July 2026 (PDF). In December 2025, chronic pain accounted for 76,215 patients (93.84% of cardholders; patients may appear under more than one condition, so percentages do not sum to 100%).
  • Licence counts, August 2026 (official ADHS monthly report via secondary summary): 170 licensed adult-use establishments (169 operating as of 1 Sept 2026), 136 medical dispensaries, 8 licensed labs (down from 21 in December 2022, per CannBus), 16,061 facility agents. Sources: CannBus Q3 2026 — CannBus; Herring Bank summary of ADHS July 2026 report — Herring Bank. Lab count conflict (8 vs 7) is flagged in Parts 4–6 and must be resolved from a dated AZCareCheck extraction before a single number is published.

0.2 Social equity — 26 licences, lottery done, “fronting” controversy unresolved in official action

26 social-equity establishment licences were required by Prop 207 and awarded by live-streamed random drawing on 8 April 2022 from more than 1,500 applications filed (1,301 in the drawing — see Part 4.4 for the gap). Eligibility: at least 3 of 4 criteria (low household income; prior marijuana conviction; a relative with one; residence in a heavily affected area / qualifying ZIP). Application fee $4,000 on the contemporaneous ADHS checklist (secondary sources differ on whether the original fee was $5,000 — Verify current ADHS fee schedule). Source: CannBus Q3 2026. No new social-equity round is open in 2026 on any official source found; the programme is not a loan/grant fund. Secondary investigative reporting (AZCIR, July 2023, via CannBus) found 11 of 26 licences owned by corporate dispensaries and only 8 original winners retaining any equity share. A 2026 partisan investigation (The State 48 News) alleges a Maricopa County Superior Court ruling in 2024 declined to void/reassign a licence alleged to have been seized by an investor, and alleges ADHS/AG inaction — this is an allegation in a non-official source, not an ADHS enforcement finding; do not present it as an official enforcement outcome — thestate48news.com. No ADHS “fronting” enforcement order, revocation, or ownership-control rule change was located in official ADHS sources for 2024–2026 in this research. Flag as Verify / gap for Part 4: search ADHS enforcement/compliance pages and Maricopa County Superior Court records before stating any fronting outcome.

0.3 Delivery — adult-use delivery live since 1 November 2024

Adult-use delivery began 1 November 2024, ahead of the 1 January 2025 statutory deadline in Prop 207, under ADHS Title 9, Chapter 18 rules (notably R9-18-312.01). Medical delivery predates it. Delivery must originate from the licensed retail location; third-party couriers such as DoorDash/Uber Eats may not independently transport cannabis; only facility agents may deliver. Localities may prohibit/restrict delivery (§36-2857(A)(6)). Sources: AZ Marijuana News, 29 Oct 2024 — azmarijuana.com, 29 Oct 2024; Dutchie Arizona guide — dutchie.com. Verify the delivery-vehicle details in §1.6 (unmarked vehicle, GPS/video, locked compartment, $10,000 retail-value trip cap, no extra inventory, trip plan) against the current text of R9-18-312.01 — they appear in secondary summaries of that rule, not in an ADHS page opened in this research.

0.4 Tax and revenue — no rate change; fund split unchanged in statute

Adult-use: 16% excise tax (A.R.S. §42-5452) plus state/local TPT (state base 5.6%). Medical: TPT only, no 16% excise. No 2026 enacted change to the 16% rate was found. Smart and Safe Arizona Fund split after costs (A.R.S. §36-2856): community colleges 33%, police/fire/sheriffs 31.4%, Highway User Revenue Fund 25.4%, Justice Reinvestment Fund 10%, Attorney General 0.2%. June 2026 Treasurer distribution (official): total revenues $92,965,999.81; distributions $87,606,879.21, including community colleges $28,910,270.14, police/fire/sheriffs $27,508,560.07, highway $22,252,147.32, Justice Reinvestment $8,760,687.92, AG $175,213.76. Source: Arizona State Treasurer, Smart & Safe Arizona Fund Report — June 2026 Distributions — aztreasury.gov (PDF). Arizona §280E decoupling (Prop 207, §43-108): Arizona already decouples for state income tax in the manner described in Part 2 — Verify the current text and whether it covers both medical and adult-use, or medical only, before Part 2 is written as settled; the federal Schedule III change does not by itself rewrite §43-108.

0.5 Hemp-derived THC — the defining enforcement story of 2024–2025

AG Opinion No. I24-005 (11 March 2024): “Arizona law does not permit the sale of delta-8 and other hemp-synthesized intoxicants by entities that have not been licensed by Health Services” — irrespective of arguable federal legality under the 2018 Farm Bill. Secondary summaries: Bradley, 27 Mar 2024 — bradley.com; MJBizDaily, 14 Mar 2024 — mjbizdaily.com. Opinion number is I24-005 (R24-001) per the AG’s official opinions index — AUDIT RESOLVED; the I24-003 variant in one search result is deleted.

AG enforcement letters, 24 March 2025: AG Kris Mayes told retailers and law enforcement that all THC-infused edible/beverage products sold by unlicensed entities are illegal, including delta-9 THC, not only delta-8; enforcement to begin 24 April 2025 (retailers given until 23 April 2025 to discontinue). Source: AG letter reproduced at cannabusiness.law (AG letter); Dept. of Liquor Licences and Control Industry Advisory, 24 Apr 2025 — azliquor.gov (PDF).

No Arizona statute or rule responding to PL 119-37 / PL 119-103 was found as of 31 Oct 2026. The state hemp definition remains delta-9-based (§3-311). The interaction of the new federal total-THC definition and 0.4 mg/container cap (effective after 11 Dec 2026) with Arizona’s AG position is Unverified / open — it is the single largest hemp follow-up for Run 2.

0.6 ADHS rule changes

1 November 2024 adult-use rules (Title 9, Chapter 18): current adult-use rules took effect 1 Nov 2024, including delivery (R9-18-312.01) and the ISO 17025 lab requirement, per CannBus Q3 2026. Medical rules are Title 9, Chapter 17. Verify the exact Arizona Administrative Register citation and any 2025–2026 amendments to Chapters 17/18 — none was located in this research beyond the 2024 package. Telemedicine / certification review (secondary, 2026): MMJ.com reports it “temporarily paused Arizona appointments while the state reviews telemedicine rules for medical marijuana certifications” and directs patients to renew directly through ADHS Patient Services — mmj.com. Verify with ADHS: no ADHS telemedicine rule change was located in official sources in this research.

0.7 Major bills and court/political developments, 2025–2026

  • HB 2179 (57th Legislature, 1st Regular Session, 2025), Chapter 166 — ENACTED, effective from and after June 30, 2026 (paraphrased as 1 July 2026 in azleg summaries): rewrites §36-2859 advertising restrictions (age-gating, no cartoons/toys/consumption imagery, mandatory health warning, 1,000-foot billboard buffer from schools, playgrounds, churches, child-care and recovery facilities; AG enforcement; $20,000 civil penalty per violation). Source: session law — azleg.gov, Ch. 166. Three-quarters vote RESOLVED in this audit: §3 of Ch. 166 contains the article IV, part 1, section 1 three-fourths clause; House 51-3-6 and Senate 28-1-1, both above three-fourths (45 of 60; 23 of 30). No court ruling on HB 2179 was located.
  • SB 1641 (2026), marijuana producers; licensure — DEAD at sine die 13 June 2026: would have required ADHS to accept marijuana-producer licence applications from 1 Jan 2029. Passed Senate 23-5-2 but did not complete the House before sine die. Sources: Senate fact sheet — azleg.gov fact sheet; JLBC fiscal note, 23 Mar 2026 — azleg.gov fiscal note.
  • SB 1725 (2026), marijuana smoke; public; private nuisance — DEAD. Senate read second time 9 Feb 2026; retained on House Committee of the Whole calendar 1 June 2026 with no action; died at sine die. Source: LegiScan — legiscan.com.
  • SB 1128 (2026) strike-everything, rural opportunity initiative — DEAD at sine die on the evidence found: would have created up to 18 additional dual licensees for unserved rural communities, applications 1–14 Apr 2027. It sat in House Rules at sine die. Verify final azleg status. Source: House Caucus & COW summary, 1 Apr 2026 — azleg.gov summary.
  • SB 1476 (2026), child neglect; prenatal substance exposure — VETOED 19 June 2026. Governor’s veto message: “further criminalization will not yield safer pregnancies and births in Arizona.” Source: Senate fact sheet as vetoed — azleg.gov fact sheet.
  • 2026 session ended sine die ~04:30, 13 June 2026; “no cannabis related legislation from the 2026 session will become law” once SB 1476 was vetoed, per the wrap article — azcannabisnews.com. Treat that claim as that article’s summary, not an azleg certification.
  • Repeal initiative I-04-2026, “Sensible Marijuana Policy Act for Arizona” — ABANDONED, not on the 3 Nov 2026 ballot: filed 17 Dec 2025; would have repealed most of Prop 207’s commercial framework while leaving personal possession (1 oz) and 6-plant home grow, and medical marijuana, intact; required 255,949 valid signatures by 2–3 July 2026 (sources differ by one day — Verify the exact deadline from the Secretary of State). Campaign abandoned May 2026. Sources: MJBizDaily, 6 May 2026 — mjbizdaily.com.
  • Hemp bills dead in 2025: SB 1702 (hemp-derived products; regulation) — introduced 10 Feb 2025, Senate read second time 11 Feb 2025, died in Senate Regulatory Affairs and Government Efficiency Committee — legiscan.com; SB 1556 (adult hemp beverages; policies) — passed Senate, removed from House Appropriations agenda without a hearing, died. Neither is law. Earlier hemp bills SB 1453 (2023) and SB 1715 (2022) also did not become law — Verify their final dispositions on azleg.
  • Court note (employment, not a 2026 ruling): Whitmire v. Walmart Inc. (D. Ariz.) — judgment against Walmart for terminating a medical-marijuana patient where high metabolite levels alone did not prove on-the-job impairment under §36-2813(B). Verify citation/date before citing as precedent. Summary: legal500.com.

0.8 Key numbers at a glance (all sourced above; do not mix years)

PART 1: LAWS

1.0 How to read Arizona cannabis law — Prop 203 vs Prop 207 vs later legislation vs rules

Voter Protection Act (1998 Prop 105; Ariz. Const. art. IV, pt. 1, §1(6)–(7)): a measure approved by voters may be amended only by a three-quarters vote of each house, and only if the amendment “furthers the purposes” of the measure; it may not be repealed except by voters. Every section in Chapters 28.1 and 28.2 is therefore headed “(Caution: 1998 Prop. 105 applies)” in the azleg compilation. Every later amendment listed in §1.2 must be checked for (a) chapter/session law, (b) three-quarters vote, and (c) “furthers the purposes” clause — where that check was not completed it is marked Verify.

  • Prop 203 (2010) = Arizona Medical Marijuana Act (AMMA), Title 36, Chapter 28.1, §§36-2801–36-2819. Approved 2 Nov 2010 (by a narrow margin — Verify the certified percentage from the Secretary of State before publication; do not use the ~50.13% figure from memory).
  • Prop 207 (2020) = Smart and Safe Arizona Act, Title 36, Chapter 28.2, §§36-2850–36-2865 (plus conforming amendments to §36-2817, Title 42, Ch. 5, Art. 10 (§§42-5452–42-5453), and Title 43, §43-108). Approved 3 Nov 2020, proclaimed/effective 30 Nov 2020, with first adult-use sales 22 Jan 2021 (the “January 2021” date is universally reported; the 22 Jan date is from Herring Bank’s summary — Verify against ADHS’s first-licence announcement). Prop 207 passed with about 60% yes — MJBizDaily, 6 May 2026, states “Nearly 60% of voters approved” — mjbizdaily.com.
  • ADHS rules: Medical = Arizona Administrative Code (A.A.C.) Title 9, Chapter 17 (R9-17-101 et seq.); Adult-use = Title 9, Chapter 18 (R9-18-101 et seq.). Both chapters were adopted under exempt rulemaking for Prop 207 implementation; the current adult-use chapter is the 1 Nov 2024 version. Hemp rules = A.A.C. Title 3, Chapter 4, Article 10 (R3-4-1001 et seq., cited in secondary sources as R3-4-1002 fingerprint, R3-4-1008 sampling — Verify article/section numbers against the Secretary of State’s A.A.C. compilation).
  • Regulator: ADHS for both medical and adult-use marijuana (licensing, rules, inspections, patient/caregiver/facility-agent cards, testing-facility certification). ADOR for TPT and marijuana excise tax. Arizona Department of Agriculture (AZDA) for industrial hemp production. Attorney General for advertising enforcement (§36-2859) and THC-product enforcement (2024 opinion + 2025 letters).

1.1 Legal status — what is legal, in one paragraph

Both medical and adult-use marijuana are legal in Arizona under state law, within the limits below; everything outside those limits remains criminal under Title 13. An adult 21+ may possess, consume, purchase, process (by manual/mechanical means only), manufacture (same limit), and transport 1 oz or less of marijuana, of which ≤5 g may be concentrate; grow 6 plants (12 per residence with 2+ adults 21+) at the adult’s primary residence under lock and out of public view; and transfer 1 oz / 6 plants without remuneration and without advertising to another adult 21+ (§36-2852, quoted in §1.3). A registered qualifying patient may possess 2.5 oz of usable marijuana per 14 days and, if authorised, cultivate 12 plants in an enclosed, locked facility (§36-2801(1), §36-2816 — see §1.4). Sale outside a licensed establishment/dispensary remains illegal; public smoking is a petty offence (§36-2853(C)); driving impaired “to the slightest degree” or with a drug/metabolite in the body remains a DUI (§28-1381, see §1.3). Federal law is not changed by Arizona law: adult-use remains federally Schedule I, and medical is Schedule III only on the given federal baseline (28 Apr 2026) for state-licensed medical activity.

1.2 Every statute and regulation — inventory with verbatim operative text

Important: “Last amended” dates below are the last amendment identified in this research, not a certified legislative-history check. The azleg compilation is current through the 57th Legislature, 2nd Regular Session (2026). Where no amendment was identified, the entry says “No post-enactment amendment identified — Verify.” Do not publish a last-amended date without that check. Official entry points (azleg compilation, via viewdocument pattern): §36-2801 (viewdocument); §36-2850 (viewdocument); §36-2853 (viewdocument); §36-2857 (viewdocument). Direct /ars/ fetches failed in the Parts 0–1 session; use the viewdocument form and Verify.

A. Title 36, Chapter 28.1 — Arizona Medical Marijuana Act (Prop 203, 2010)

SectionSubject — plain summaryVerbatim operative text (key passage)Origin / last amendment identified
§36-2801Definitions“‘Allowable amount of marijuana’ (a) With respect to a qualifying patient, means: (i) Two and one-half ounces of usable marijuana. (ii) If the qualifying patient’s registry identification card states that the qualifying patient is authorized to cultivate marijuana, twelve marijuana plants contained in an enclosed, locked facility, except that the plants are not required to be in an enclosed, locked facility if the plants are being transported because the qualifying patient is moving.” And: “‘Debilitating medical condition’ means one or more of the following: (a) Cancer, glaucoma, positive status for human immunodeficiency virus, acquired immune deficiency syndrome, hepatitis C, amyotrophic lateral sclerosis, Crohn’s disease, agitation of Alzheimer’s disease or the treatment of these conditions. (b) A chronic or debilitating disease or medical condition or its treatment that produces one or more of the following: cachexia or wasting syndrome; severe and chronic pain; severe nausea; seizures, including those characteristic of epilepsy; or severe and persistent muscle spasms, including those characteristic of multiple sclerosis. (c) Any other medical condition or its treatment added by the department pursuant to section 36-2801.01.” Source: azleg via Justia 2014 text, still in substance current — Justia §36-2801.Prop 203 (2010); PTSD added by ADHS under §36-2801.01. No post-Prop 207 statutory amendment to the condition list identified — Verify.
§36-2801.01Adding conditionsADHS may add a condition on petition; petitions accepted in January and July (secondary: University of Arizona AzCANN FAQ — AzCANN FAQ). Verify current R9-17 petition procedure and the full list of ADHS-added conditions beyond PTSD.Prop 203.
§36-2803ADHS duties (rulemaking, registry, verification system)ADHS is “the licensing and issuing authority for the Arizona Medical Marijuana Program” (ADHS monthly reports, e.g. July 2026 — ADHS July 2026).Prop 203.
§36-2804, §36-2804.01, §36-2804.02Applications: dispensary registration; patient/caregiver applications; physician written certificationPatient application requires a physician’s written certification (see §1.4). The statute requires nonprofit dispensary registration for the medical channel, while the same entity may hold an adult-use establishment licence (dual licensee, §36-2850). Do not rely on secondary phrasing that “only not-for-profit can distribute” post-dual-licensing.Prop 203; amended by Prop 207 §3 only as to §36-2817 (fund), not §§2804–2804.02 in substance — Verify.
§36-2804.03Issuance of registry identification cards; visiting patients“A registry identification card, or its equivalent, that is issued under the laws of another state, district, territory, commonwealth or insular possession of the United States that allows a visiting qualifying patient to possess or use marijuana for medical purposes in the jurisdiction of issuance has the same force and effect when held by a visiting qualifying patient as a registry identification card issued by the department, except that a visiting qualifying patient is not authorized to obtain marijuana from a nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary.” (§36-2804.03(C)). Also: ADHS “shall… approve or deny an application or renewal within ten days of receiving a completed application or renewal” and “Issue a registry identification card to a qualifying patient and his designated caregiver, if any, within five days of approving the application or renewal.” (§36-2804.03(A)). Minor patients: no card under 18 unless the physician explained risks/benefits to the custodial parent/guardian, written certification from two physicians, and written parental consent to (a) allow use, (b) serve as designated caregiver, (c) control acquisition, dosage and frequency. (§36-2804.03(B)). Sources: Justia §36-2804.03; State v. Kemmish, 418 P.3d 1087 (Ariz. App. 2018).Prop 203.
§36-2806Nonprofit medical marijuana dispensaries — registration, operation, zoning notice, inventory control“A.R.S. §36-2806 dictates inventory control and record-keeping, but does not prescribe a specific software system” (Desert Sounds analysis — Desert Sounds). Mohave County zoning code cites §36-2806 as the county’s authority for reasonable zoning of nonprofit dispensaries — Mohave County §37.V. Verify the full §36-2806 text, including the former 1-per-Community Health Analysis Area (CHAA) allocation and its post-Prop 207 status — the CHAA system is historical and must not be presented as the current adult-use allocation.Prop 203; operationally superseded in part by dual licensing (Prop 207).
§36-2810Confidentiality / verification systemADHS-held patient/caregiver applications, dispensary applications/addresses, and cardholder identifying information are confidential and exempt from public disclosure (LegalClarity summary — Verify full text).Prop 203.
§36-2811Presumption of medical use; protections; civil penalty“There is a presumption that a qualifying patient or designated caregiver is engaged in the medical use of marijuana pursuant to this chapter” if the person holds a registry card and possesses no more than the allowable amount; the presumption “may be rebutted by evidence that conduct related to marijuana was not for the purpose of treating or alleviating the qualifying patient’s debilitating medical condition.” (§36-2811(A)). “A registered qualifying patient or registered designated caregiver is not subject to arrest, prosecution or penalty in any manner, or denial of any right or privilege, including any civil penalty or disciplinary action by a court or occupational or professional licensing board or bureau: 1. For the registered qualifying patient’s medical use of marijuana pursuant to this chapter, if the registered qualifying patient does not possess more than the allowable amount of marijuana.” (§36-2811(B)(1)). Source: mirror of §36-2811 — §36-2811 (mirror PDF).Prop 203.
§36-2813Discrimination prohibited (employment, schools, housing, etc.)See §1.8 for full treatment. Operative employment sentence (secondary quotation, Verify against official text): an employer may not discriminate in hiring, termination or terms of employment based on status as a registered qualifying patient or a positive drug test for marijuana components/metabolites, unless the patient used, possessed or was impaired on the premises/during work hours, or unless employing the patient would cause loss of a monetary/licensing benefit under federal law.Prop 203.
§36-2816Patient purchase / dispensing limits — 2.5 oz per 14 daysThe 2.5 oz / 14-day limit is consistently attributed to §36-2816 in secondary sources. Verify the section number and whether the limit is in §36-2816 or in R9-17-303 — ADHS rule summaries cite R9-17-303 for the 2.5 oz / 14-day allotment. Do not cite both interchangeably until checked.Prop 203.
§36-2817Medical marijuana fund; private donationsAmended by Prop 207 §3 to transfer $1,000,000 from the medical marijuana fund to ADHS “for the sole purpose of funding programs and grants to qualified nonprofit organizations for education and community outreach related to the provisions of chapter 28.2.” Source: Prop 207 text — Prop 207 initiative text (PDF).Prop 203, as amended by Prop 207 (2020).

B. Title 36, Chapter 28.2 — Smart and Safe Arizona Act (Prop 207, 2020)

SectionSubjectVerbatim operative textOrigin / amendment
§36-2850DefinitionsKey definitions (Prop 207 text): “‘Advertising’ means any public communication in any medium that offers or solicits a commercial transaction involving the sale or purchase of marijuana or marijuana products.” “‘Child-resistant’ means designed or constructed to be significantly difficult for children under five years of age to open, and not difficult for normal adults to use properly.” “‘Consume,’ ‘consumption’ and ‘consuming’ mean the act of ingesting, inhaling or otherwise introducing marijuana into the human body.” “‘Dual licensee’ means an entity that holds both a nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary registration and a marijuana establishment license.” (Verify whether “dual licensee” was in the original Prop 207 §36-2850 or added by Laws 2021, Ch. 394 (SB 1842) / SB 1121.) “‘Marijuana products’ means marijuana concentrate and products that are composed of marijuana and other ingredients and that are intended for use or consumption, including edible products, ointments and tinctures.” “‘Marijuana establishment’ is an entity licensed by ADHS to operate a single retail location, a single off-site cultivation location, or a single off-site manufacturing location.” Verify current §36-2850 off-site definitions verbatim.Prop 207 (2020); amended Laws 2021, Ch. 394 (SB 1842) — Ch. 394 — Verify three-quarters vote. Further amended by HB 2179 (2025, Ch. 166) as to §36-2859 only.
§36-2851Lawful acts / limits of chapterPreserves employer rights to prohibit/regulate conduct on their property; landlord/property-owner rights; school/health-care/day-care restrictions; federal-law compliance; and the right of localities under §36-2857. Verify full §36-2851 text — it is the single most-cited section for employment/landlord/school carve-outs and must be quoted verbatim in a final audit, not paraphrased from a law-firm blog.Prop 207 (2020).
§36-2852Allowable possession and personal useQuoted in full in §1.3 below.Prop 207 (2020).
§36-2853Violations; classification; civil penaltyQuoted in §1.3 below.Prop 207 (2020); amended Laws 2021 (HB 2171, court-jurisdiction/procedure — Verify whether substance or only procedure, and its vote).
§36-2854Rules; licensing; early applicants; fees; civil penalty; legal counselRulemaking mandate + licensing caps + fees + edible potency + delivery + tracking + inspections. Statutory fee/cap passages: “The department may not issue more than one marijuana establishment license for every ten pharmacies that have registered under section 32-1929, that have obtained a pharmacy permit from the Arizona board of pharmacy and that operate within this state.” And: “not later than six months after the department adopts final rules to implement a social equity ownership program pursuant to paragraph 9 of this subsection, the department shall issue twenty-six additional marijuana establishment licenses to entities that are qualified pursuant to the social equity ownership program.” Rural-county proviso: “the department may issue a marijuana establishment license to not more than two marijuana establishments per county that contains no registered nonprofit medical marijuana dispensaries, or one marijuana establishment license per county that has one registered nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary.” Edible potency: rules “Shall limit the strength of edible marijuana products to not more than ten milligrams of tetrahydrocannabinol per serving or one hundred milligrams of tetrahydrocannabinol per package” and require delineation/scoring + homogenisation for multi-serving products (§36-2854(A)(7)). Licences “valid for two years.” Early applicants: ADHS “shall accept applications for marijuana establishment licenses from early applicants beginning January 19, 2021 through March 9, 2021.” Sources: §36-2854 (azleg, directly opened in Parts 4–6 research); FindLaw §36-2854. Verify current fee amounts in §36-2854 vs R9-18 fee schedule — secondary sources give $25,000 initial application, $5,000 renewal, $2,500 approval-to-operate vs $4,000 social-equity application vs $5,000 social-equity application in another secondary source.Prop 207 (2020); amended Laws 2021, SB 1121 — SB 1121 (House-engrossed) — Verify chapter number, final passage, three-quarters vote.
§36-2854.01Added 2021 — subject VerifySB 1121 added this section; its subject was not confirmed in sources opened — do not guess. Obtain text.Laws 2021, SB 1121 — Verify.
§36-2856Smart and Safe Arizona Fund; disposition“The smart and safe Arizona fund is established consisting of all monies deposited pursuant to sections 36-2853, 36-2854, 36-2859, 42-5452 and 42-5453, private donations and interest earned on those monies. Monies in the fund are continuously appropriated… do not revert to the state general fund…” (§36-2856(A)). “On or before June 30 and December 31 of each year, the state treasurer shall transfer all monies in the smart and safe Arizona fund in excess of the amounts paid pursuant to subsection B… as follows: 1. 33 percent to community college districts… 2. 31.4 percent to municipal police departments, municipal fire departments, fire districts… and county sheriffs’ departments… for personnel costs. 3. 25.4 percent to the Arizona highway user revenue fund… 4. Ten percent to the justice reinvestment fund established by section 36-2863. 5. 0.2 percent to the attorney general to use to enforce this chapter or to grant to localities to enforce this chapter.” (§36-2856(D)). Source: §36-2856 (azleg, directly opened).Prop 207 (2020). A 2025 proposal (SB 1299, engrossed) would have diverted $2,500,000 per half-year FY2025-26–FY2029-30 to a new Safe Community Enforcement Fund (§36-2856.01) — not enacted on evidence found; Verify.
§36-2857Localities; marijuana establishments and testing facilitiesQuoted in full in §1.6 / Part 3.1 below.Prop 207 (2020). SB 1641 (2026, dead) would have added “marijuana producers” throughout — not law.
§36-2858Lawful operation of marijuana establishments and testing facilities“Except as specifically and expressly provided in section 36-2857 and notwithstanding any other law, it is lawful and is not an offense under the laws of this state or any locality, may not constitute the basis for detention, search or arrest, and may not constitute the sole basis for seizure or forfeiture of assets or the basis for imposing penalties under the laws of this state or any locality for: [a marijuana establishment / testing facility, or agent, to possess, purchase, sell, transport, cultivate, manufacture, test, etc., within the licence]” — opening language via SB 1641 introduced text reproducing §36-2858. Verify the full enumerated list, including: “a marijuana establishment may not sell more than one ounce of marijuana to a consumer in a single transaction, not more than five grams of which may be in the form of marijuana concentrate” — Laws 2021, Ch. 394 — Ch. 394.Prop 207 (2020); amended Laws 2021, Ch. 394 (SB 1842) and SB 1121 — Verify votes.
§36-2859Advertising; restrictions; warning; enforcement; civil penalty; exceptionAs amended by HB 2179 (2025, Ch. 166), effective from and after June 30, 2026 (paraphrased as 1 July 2026 in azleg summaries): “A marijuana establishment or nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary may advertise or authorize advertising for marijuana or marijuana products in accordance with restrictions imposed by this section.” Mandatory warning: “All advertising pursuant to this section must contain the following conspicuous and legible warning: ‘Do not use marijuana if you are under twenty-one years of age or pregnant. Keep marijuana out of reach of children.’” Billboard: “A billboard advertisement under this section is prohibited within one thousand radial feet of any child care center, church, substance abuse recovery facility, public park, public [school — Verify full list].” Violation: “advertises marijuana or marijuana products, including marijuana paraphernalia, in violation of this section or otherwise violates this section shall pay a civil penalty of $20,000 per violation to the smart and safe Arizona fund established by section 36-2856.” Advertising must “accurately and legibly identif[y] the marijuana establishment or nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary responsible for the content of the advertising by name and license number or registration number.” Source: HB 2179, Ch. 166. HB 2179 effective date — AUDIT RESOLVED: Ch. 166 §2 says “effective from and after June 30, 2026”; azleg summaries paraphrase it as 1 July 2026. Three-fourths vote satisfied (House 51-3-6, Senate 28-1-1).Prop 207 (2020); amended HB 2179 (2025, Ch. 166) — Verify three-quarters vote.
§36-2862ExpungementPetition process from 12 July 2021 for arrests/charges/convictions/adjudications/sentences for possession, consumption, transportation, cultivation of marijuana/plants/paraphernalia that would now be lawful under Prop 207. Detail is Part 12, not Part 1, but the section is listed here for completeness.Prop 207 (2020).
§36-2863Justice Reinvestment FundReceives 10% of Smart and Safe Fund excess (§36-2856(D)(4)). Full §36-2863 programme purposes — Verify full text; not quoted in sources opened for Part 1 (but see Part 2.5, where §36-2863 was directly opened).Prop 207 (2020).
§36-2864Transaction privilege tax; use tax; additional taxes prohibited; exception“For purposes of the transaction privilege tax and use tax levied and collected pursuant to title 42, chapters 5 and 6, marijuana and marijuana products are tangible personal property defined in §42-5001 and are subject to the transaction privilege tax in the retail classification and use tax.” (§36-2864(A)). “Except as provided in subsection A of this section and §42-5452, this state and localities may not levy or collect additional taxes of any kind on the sale of marijuana or marijuana products and may not levy or collect any fees or assessments of any kind on the sale of marijuana or marijuana products or on the licensing, operations or activities of marijuana establishments or marijuana testing facilities, unless the fee or assessment is of general applicability to individuals or businesses that are not engaged in the sale of marijuana or marijuana products.” (§36-2864(B)). Effective 30 Nov 2020 (Prop 207). Source: §36-2864 (azleg, directly opened).Prop 207 (2020).

C. Title 13 — Controlled substances (the criminal backstop)

SectionSubjectVerbatim / summaryNote
§13-3401Definitions (including “marijuana,” “cannabis,” threshold amounts)“Marijuana” threshold for possession-for-sale presumption is 2 pounds (secondary summary). Verify current §13-3401 definitions and whether “industrial hemp” is excluded from “marijuana”/“cannabis” in Title 13 after the 2018 hemp amendments.Amended Laws 2018, Ch. 287 (SB 1098) for hemp; otherwise longstanding.
§13-3405Possession, use, production, sale or transportation of marijuana; classification; exceptions“Except as provided in sections 36-2852 and 36-2853, a person shall not knowingly: 1. Possess or use marijuana. 2. Possess marijuana for sale. 3. Produce marijuana. 4. Transport for sale, import into this state or offer to transport for sale or import into this state, sell, transfer or offer to sell or transfer marijuana.” (§13-3405(A)). Penalties (§13-3405(B)): possession not for sale <2 lb = class 6 felony; 2–<4 lb = class 5 felony; ≥4 lb = class 4 felony; possession for sale <2 lb = class 4 felony; 2–4 lb = class 3 felony; >4 lb = class 2 felony; production <2 lb = class 5 felony, 2–4 lb = class 4, >4 lb = class 3; transport for sale/import/sale <2 lb = class 3 felony, ≥2 lb = class 2 felony. Source: mirror — §13-3405 (mirror PDF). Critical interaction: §13-3405(A) expressly yields to §§36-2852/36-2853, so 1 oz / 5 g / 6-plant conduct is not a §13-3405 offence, and 1–2.5 oz is a petty offence under §36-2853(A), not a felony — only >2.5 oz (outside §§36-2852/36-2853) falls back to §13-3405.Longstanding; opening clause updated by Prop 207 (2020) to cross-refer §§36-2852/36-2853. Verify whether the “Schedule I” listing for marijuana/cannabis in Title 13, Ch. 34 has been amended post-28 Apr 2026 federal Schedule III action — no Arizona schedule amendment was found in this research; do not assume Title 13 was conformed.
§13-3412.011996 Prop 200 medical provision — historicalDoctors were “first permitted to prescribe class one controlled substances such as marijuana in Arizona by ballot initiative Proposition 200 in 1996 (A.R.S. §13-3412.01)” — azleg SB 1024 summary (2019). Detail is Part 9 (history), not current operative law for dispensing.Prop 200 (1996).
§13-3415Drug paraphernaliaPossession of marijuana paraphernalia by an adult 21+ is lawful under §36-2852(A)(5) (POST lesson plan — Verify paragraph number/text). Outside that, §13-3415 remains the paraphernalia offence.—

  • §§42-5001, 42-6001 et seq. (TPT): marijuana is “tangible personal property” for TPT retail classification (§36-2864(A)); state TPT base 5.6% plus county/city rates (Part 2/Part 3 detail).

E. Title 3, Chapter 2, Article 4.1 — Industrial hemp (Laws 2018, Ch. 287, SB 1098)

SectionSubjectVerbatim / summary
§3-311Definitions“‘Industrial hemp’ means the plant cannabis sativa L. and any part of such a plant, whether growing or not, with a delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of not more than three-tenths percent on a dry-weight basis.” “‘Hemp products’ means all products made from industrial hemp, including cloth, cordage, fiber, fuel, grain, paint, paper, construction materials, plastics and by-products derived from sterile hemp seed or hemp seed oil. Hemp products excludes any product made to be ingested except food made from sterile hemp seed or hemp seed oil.” Source: Laws 2018, Ch. 287 (SB 1098) — Ch. 287; corroborated by AZDA FAQ — AZDA hemp FAQ. Note the AZDA FAQ’s gloss: AZDA states the standard as “Total Delta-9 THC concentration of not more than 0.3% on a dry-weight basis. This standard includes the total calculable amount resulting from the chemical conversion of Tetrahydrocannabinolic Acid (THCA)” — AZDA FAQ. That is AZDA’s interpretive total-delta-9 formulation; the statute as enacted says “delta-9 THC concentration.” Flag the statute-vs-FAQ wording difference; do not conflate AZDA’s total-delta-9 with the new federal total-THC definition.
§3-312–§3-314Director duties, licensing, fees, fingerprint clearanceAZDA director oversees licensing/production/management of industrial hemp seed; adopts fees; authorises qualified applicants to propagate, harvest, transport or process. Fingerprint clearance card (A.R.S. §41-1758.07) required for applicants/key participants. Verify §§3-312–3-314 full text and current fee schedule (Part 7).
§3-315Industrial hemp trust fundFees deposited to the industrial hemp trust fund (§3-315, per Laws 2018, Ch. 287).
§3-320Affirmative defence“It is an affirmative defense to any prosecution for the possession or cultivation of marijuana pursuant to title 13, chapter 34 that the defendant is a licensee, or a designee or agent of a licensee, who is in compliance with this article.” (SB 1702 introduced text reproducing §3-320 — SB 1702 would have extended this to narcotic drugs; that extension is not law.)
§§3-321–3-332PROPOSED ONLY (SB 1702, 2025, dead)Hemp-derived product regulation, licensing, testing, labelling, school-grounds ban, etc. Do not present as law. Introduced text: SB 1702 introduced text.

Hemp plan status (for Part 7, stated here because it determines which law applies): Arizona runs its own USDA-approved state plan, not the USDA direct programme. AZDA FAQ: “Does Arizona have a USDA Approved State Hemp Plan for Domestic Hemp Production? A: Yes, the USDA approved Arizona’s State Hemp Plan for implementation on January 1, 2022.” — AZDA Hemp FAQ (PDF, 12 Mar 2024). USDA list (updated 17 Feb 2026) lists “Arizona | AZ Approved Plan (pdf)*” and separately “Tonto Apache Tribe of Arizona | Approved Plan (pdf)*” — USDA plan-review page. Programme launched 1 June 2019 (pilot, Laws 2018, Ch. 287, effective 3 Aug 2019).

G. Other statutes cited in Part 1

  • §28-1381 (DUI): “It is unlawful for a person to drive or be in actual physical control of a vehicle in this state under any of the following circumstances: 1. While under the influence of intoxicating liquor, any drug, a vapor releasing substance containing a toxic substance or any combination… if the person is impaired to the slightest degree. … 3. While there is any drug defined in section 13-3401 or its metabolite in the person’s body.” Medical affirmative defence: Dobson v. McClennen, 238 Ariz. 389 (2015) — a registered patient may show by a preponderance that the marijuana/metabolite concentration was insufficient to cause impairment, as a defence to an (A)(3) charge only, not to an (A)(1) “slightest degree” charge. No per-se THC blood limit exists in Arizona. Verify whether later case law changes the (A)(3) “impairing metabolite” reading — one secondary guide asserts prosecutors must now prove actual impairment and active metabolites, not inactive carboxy-THC — treat as Verify, not settled law, until the opinions are read.
  • §36-601.01 (Smoke-Free Arizona Act): bars smoking in most enclosed public places, with narrow exceptions. Verify whether “smoking” in §36-601.01 covers marijuana smoke and vaping aerosol as of 2026.
  • Title 23, §§23-493–23-493.12 (Drug Testing of Employees Act, DTEA): see §1.8.
  • Title 4 (liquor): Dept. of Liquor Licences and Control’s 24 Apr 2025 advisory states a liquor licensee that knowingly permits illegal THC-beverage/edible sales may face sanctions up to revocation under §4-210(A)(2) — azliquor.gov advisory (PDF). This is an enforcement advisory, not a cannabis licensing statute.
  • Title 41, §41-1758.07 (fingerprint clearance cards): required for hemp applicants/key participants and for marijuana facility agents/dispensary agents (via ADHS rules).

1.3 Possession, home grow, public use, vehicles, gifting, product types

Adult-use — §36-2852 (verbatim, core)

“Except as specifically and expressly provided in sections 36-2851 and 36-2853 and notwithstanding any other law, the following acts by an individual who is at least twenty-one years of age are lawful, are not an offense under the laws of this state or any locality, may not constitute the basis for detention, search or arrest, and cannot serve as the sole basis for seizure or forfeiture of assets, for imposing penalties of any kind under the laws of this state or any locality or for abrogating or limiting any right or privilege conferred or protected by the laws of this state or any locality:

1. Possessing, consuming, purchasing, processing, manufacturing by manual or mechanical means, including sieving or ice water separation but excluding chemical extraction or chemical synthesis, or transporting one ounce or less of marijuana, except that not more than five grams of marijuana may be in the form of marijuana concentrate.

2. Possessing, transporting, cultivating or processing not more than six marijuana plants for personal use at the individual’s primary residence, and possessing, processing and manufacturing by manual or mechanical means, including sieving or ice water separation but excluding chemical extraction or chemical synthesis, the marijuana produced by the plants on the premises where the marijuana plants were grown if all of the following apply: (a) Not more than twelve plants are produced at a single residence where two or more individuals who are at least twenty-one years of age reside at one time. (b) Cultivation takes place within a closet, room, greenhouse or other enclosed area on the grounds of the residence equipped with a lock or other security device that prevents access by minors. (c) [Cultivation takes place in an area where the plants are not visible from public view without the use of binoculars, aircraft or other optical aids — wording from Prop 207 text / LegalClarity summary; Verify clause (c) lettering against current §36-2852(A)(2).]

3. Transferring one ounce or less of marijuana, of which not more than five grams may be in the form of marijuana concentrate, to an individual who is at least twenty-one years of age if the transfer is without remuneration and is not advertised or promoted to the public.

4. Transferring up to six marijuana plants to an individual who is at least twenty-one years of age if the transfer is without remuneration and is not advertised or promoted to the public.

5. [Possessing marijuana paraphernalia — §36-2852(A)(5), per POST lesson plan — Verify paragraph number/text.]

(C) The odor of marijuana or burnt marijuana does not by itself constitute reasonable suspicion of a crime. (Mesa Police policy summary of §36-2852(B)/(C) — Verify subsection lettering.)”

Sources for §36-2852 text: Justia 2021 — Justia §36-2852; Prop 207 initiative text — Prop 207 text (archive.org); Legislative Council draft analysis (I-23-2020) — Legislative Council analysis (PDF).

ItemLimitSource
