Medical possession (for comparison, not adult-use): 2.5 oz usable marijuana per 14 days; 12 plants if authorised (see §1.4). No concentrate sub-limit applies to the medical 2.5 oz on the secondary summaries found (MMJ.com: “Concentrates: No sub-limit (unlike rec 5g limit)”) — Verify against §36-2801(1) / R9-17-303, because “usable marijuana” includes concentrates/edibles by definition and the absence of a sub-limit is an inference, not a quoted statutory sentence.

Violations — §36-2853 (verbatim, core)

“A. Notwithstanding any other law and except as otherwise provided in this chapter, a person who possesses an amount of marijuana greater than the amount allowed pursuant to section 36-2852, but not more than two and one-half ounces of marijuana, of which not more than twelve and one-half grams is in the form of marijuana concentrate, is guilty of a petty offense.

B. Notwithstanding any other law, a person who is under twenty-one years of age and who possesses, consumes, transports or transfers without remuneration one ounce or less of marijuana, of which not more than five grams is in the form of marijuana concentrate, or paraphernalia relating to the consumption of marijuana or marijuana products: 1. For a first violation, shall pay a civil penalty of not more than $100 to the smart and safe Arizona fund established by section 36-2856 and in the court’s discretion may be ordered to attend up to four hours of drug education or counseling. 2. For a second violation, is guilty of a petty offense, and in the court’s discretion may be ordered to attend up to eight hours of drug education or counseling. 3. For a third or subsequent violation, is guilty of a class 1 misdemeanor.

C. A person who smokes marijuana in a public place or open space is guilty of a petty offense.

D. Except as otherwise provided in chapter 28.1 of this title and notwithstanding any other law, any unlicensed person who cultivates marijuana plants pursuant to section 36-2852 where they are visible from public view without using binoculars, aircraft or other optical aids or outside of an enclosed area that is equipped with a lock or other security device that prevents access by minors is guilty of: 1. For a first violation, a petty offense. 2. For a second or subsequent violation, a class 3 misdemeanor.

E. A person who is under twenty-one years of age and who misrepresents the person’s age… by means of a written instrument of identification or who uses a fraudulent or false written instrument of identification with the intent to induce a person to sell or otherwise transfer marijuana or a marijuana product to the person who is under twenty-one years of age [is guilty of a petty offense for a first violation and a class 1 misdemeanor for a repeat — completion from LegalClarity summary; Verify §36-2853(E) full text].”

Source: §36-2853 (viewdocument). Petty-offence fine: secondary sources state a petty offence carries a maximum $300 fine (§13-802). Verify §13-802’s current petty-offence fine cap before publishing a dollar figure.

Public consumption: Smoking in a public place or open space = petty offence (§36-2853(C)). That covers parks, sidewalks, restaurants, shopping centres, schools and anywhere the public has access (secondary gloss). Non-smoking consumption (edibles, tinctures) in public is not expressly covered by §36-2853(C)’s word “smokes” — Verify whether ADHS rules or local ordinances extend the ban to non-smoked consumption; do not state a blanket “no public consumption of any kind” without that check. Private-residence consumption is lawful (subject to landlord/employer/school/health-care carve-outs in §36-2851). Consumption lounges: no lounge licence class exists in Arizona (CannBus) — detail is Part 12 in Run 2.

Vehicle rules — what was and was not found: Driving: §28-1381(A)(1) “impaired to the slightest degree” and (A)(3) drug/metabolite prong, plus Dobson medical affirmative defence — see §1.2(G). No Arizona per-se THC nanogram limit. Open-container / in-vehicle possession / transport packaging: No Arizona “open container” marijuana statute was located in this research. Adult-use transport of ≤1 oz is lawful under §36-2852(A)(1); establishment transport is under licence (see §1.6 delivery/transport). Flag as Verify/gap: check §§28-1381, 36-2852, 36-2854 and R9-18 transport rules for any in-vehicle storage requirement before the final HTML states a vehicle-storage rule. Do not import another state’s open-container rule. Odour: “The odor of marijuana or burnt marijuana does not by itself constitute reasonable suspicion of a crime” (§36-2852, subsection lettering Verify).

Allowed product types

  • Flower, concentrates, vapes, tinctures, ointments, edibles, beverages: All are within “marijuana products” (§36-2850: “marijuana concentrate and products that are composed of marijuana and other ingredients and that are intended for use or consumption, including edible products, ointments and tinctures”). Vapes are concentrates for the 5 g cap (secondary gloss).
  • Edibles — adult-use caps (statute + rule, quoted): Statute (§36-2854(A)(7)): rules “Shall limit the strength of edible marijuana products to not more than ten milligrams of tetrahydrocannabinol per serving or one hundred milligrams of tetrahydrocannabinol per package” and “Shall require that if a marijuana product contains more than one serving, it must be delineated or scored into standard serving sizes and homogenized to ensure uniform disbursement throughout the marijuana product.” Rule (R9-18-313): “contain no more total tetrahydrocannabinol than: i. 10 mg of per serving; or ii. 100 mg per package; and c. If packaged as more than one serving, are: i. Scored or otherwise delineated into standard serving size, and ii. Of homogeneous consistency to ensure uniform disbursement of total tetrahydrocannabinol throughout the edible food product.” And the establishment “is responsible for the content and quality of any edible food product sold or dispensed.” Sources: FindLaw §36-2854; Cornell R9-18-313.
  • Edibles — medical caps: No separate medical per-serving/per-package THC cap was located in statute or R9-17 summaries in this research. Medical edibles are governed by the 2.5 oz / 14-day quantity limit, not by a 10 mg / 100 mg potency cap on the evidence found — Verify R9-17 edible rules before stating this as a positive rule; the absence of a cap in the sources opened is not proof of absence.
  • Beverages (licensed marijuana beverages): Treated as edible marijuana products for the 10 mg / 100 mg caps when sold by a licensed establishment. Hemp-derived THC beverages sold outside licensed establishments are unlawful under the AG position (§1.5), regardless of the licensed-beverage rule.
  • Prohibited product forms (licensed channel): SB 1641’s summary of existing advertising/packaging law reflects prohibitions on manufacturing/selling marijuana products resembling a human, animal, insect, fruit, toy or cartoon, or with names resembling/imitating food/drink brands marketed to children. Verify whether that prohibition is in §36-2859, R9-18-310/313, or both, before quoting it as a product-form ban.

1.4 Medical programme — Prop 203 as changed by Prop 207

What Prop 207 did and did not do to medical: Prop 207 did not repeal the medical programme. It added Ch. 28.2 alongside Ch. 28.1, amended §36-2817 (fund transfer, above), and created a separate adult-use channel so adults 21+ no longer need a card to buy. Medical advantages that remain, on the sources found: (1) 2.5 oz / 14 days vs 1 oz / transaction; (2) no 16% excise tax (TPT only); (3) access at 18+ (and minors via caregiver), whereas adult-use is 21+; (4) patient cultivation under the 25-mile rule (below), which adult-use home grow does not require. The medical programme is administered by ADHS under the same Title 9, Ch. 17 rules, with adult-use under Ch. 18.

Qualifying (“debilitating medical”) conditions — §36-2801(3), verbatim in §1.2(A): Named: cancer; glaucoma; positive status for HIV; AIDS; hepatitis C; amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS); Crohn’s disease; agitation of Alzheimer’s disease — or the treatment of these conditions. Symptom-based: a chronic or debilitating disease/medical condition, or its treatment, that produces: cachexia or wasting syndrome; severe and chronic pain; severe nausea; seizures, including those characteristic of epilepsy; or severe and persistent muscle spasms, including those characteristic of multiple sclerosis. ADHS-added: post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD) — added by ADHS under §36-2801.01 (MMJ.com — MMJ.com conditions; University of Arizona AzCANN FAQ — AzCANN FAQ). Not qualifying on evidence found: ADHD, fibromyalgia, lupus, rheumatoid arthritis, Tourette’s syndrome as standalone named conditions — some secondary guides list them loosely — do not list them as qualifying unless they fall under the symptom-based prongs (e.g. severe chronic pain / muscle spasms) or an ADHS addition; the loose lists are not an official source. Petitions to add conditions: ADHS accepts petitions in January and July (AzCANN FAQ) — Verify current R9-17 petition rule and the disposition of recent petitions.

Physician certification: “Written certification” = “a document dated and signed by a physician, stating that in the physician’s professional opinion the patient is likely to receive therapeutic or palliative benefit from the medical use of marijuana to treat or alleviate the patient’s debilitating medical condition or symptoms” (§36-2801, “written certification” definition). Initial application (R9-17-202): certification dated within 90 days of submission, attesting to a physical examination within the previous 90 days, with the initial exam specified as in-person (MMJ.com renewal guide — MMJ.com renewals). Renewal (R9-17-204): certification dated within 90 days of submission, attesting to a physical examination within the previous 90 days; the renewal rule does not specify in-person (same source). Telemedicine status is under ADHS review in 2026 per MMJ.com (see §0.6) — Verify with ADHS. Physician credential: Arizona-licensed MD, DO, NP, or NMD per R9-17-302 as summarised by MMJ.com — Verify; the statute’s word is “physician” and the NP/NMD inclusion, if correct, must be traced to a rule/statutory amendment. Minor patients: two physicians’ written certifications + parental explanation/consent + parent as designated caregiver controlling acquisition/dosage/frequency (§36-2804.03(B), quoted in §1.2(A)).

Patient and caregiver cards and fees (ADHS, via secondary summaries — Verify against current ADHS fee page / R9-17-102 before publication): Patient application/renewal: $150 standard; $75 with Supplemental Nutrition Assistance Program (SNAP) enrollment. Card validity: 2 years from issue. Caregiver card: $200 — Verify; another secondary source does not give a caregiver fee, and the original R9-17-102 fee list must be checked. Replacement card: $10, request within 10 working days of loss (R9-17-105). Application channel: ADHS Individual Licensing Portal (online); patient attestation form required. ADHS must approve/deny a completed application within 10 days and issue the card within 5 days of approval (§36-2804.03(A)). Residency: Arizona residency required for a patient card; adult patients 18+ may apply without a caregiver; minors require a caregiver. Caregiver rules: designated caregiver must be 21+ (§36-2801(5)(a) — “Is at least twenty-one years of age”); must have a registry card for each patient assisted (§36-2804.03(A)(2)); payment for costs is not an offence under Title 13, Ch. 34 or Title 36, Ch. 27, Art. 4. Verify the current cap on number of patients per caregiver — no cap was confirmed in sources opened; do not state a number.

Purchase limits (medical): 2.5 oz of usable marijuana per 14-day period, verified through ADHS’s medical marijuana electronic verification system before dispensing. The dispensary must verify the card and allotment; the transaction is recorded in the verification system at the time the dispensary relinquishes possession, including for delivery.

Patient cultivation — the 25-mile rule: A qualifying patient whose registry card states the patient is authorised to cultivate may cultivate 12 marijuana plants in an enclosed, locked facility (§36-2801(1)(a)(ii)). Authorisation is tied to distance: cultivation is available only if the patient lives more than 25 miles from the nearest operating nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary (straight-line radius, per LegalClarity summary; NORML). Designated caregiver: 12 plants per patient if authorised, under the same 25-mile / enclosed-locked-facility conditions. Interaction with adult-use home grow (important, do not conflate): An adult 21+ who is also a patient may grow 6 plants under §36-2852 without the 25-mile rule, plus — if separately authorised as a patient — 12 medical plants under §36-2801. Whether the two allotments stack at one residence, and how ADHS counts them, was not resolved in sources opened — Verify before stating a combined household total. July 2026 ADHS data show only 442 of 70,444 qualifying patients were cultivation-approved (July 2026 report), consistent with the 25-mile rule now rarely being met in urban Arizona. Verify the statutory source of the 25-mile rule itself — it is in ADHS rules / §36-2804 application provisions as summarised, but the exact section (R9-17-303? §36-2804.02?) was not confirmed.

Visiting patients: See §36-2804.03(C) verbatim in §1.2(A): a visiting qualifying patient (non-resident, or resident <30 days, with a home-state card/equivalent for a condition that is also an Arizona debilitating condition — State v. Abdi, 236 Ariz. 609 (App. 2015)) has the same presumptions/immunities for possession/use in Arizona, but is not authorised to obtain marijuana from a nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary. In practice, a visiting patient 21+ may buy adult-use marijuana under Ch. 28.2 limits instead. Contrary secondary claims that visiting patients “can purchase up to 2.5 oz every two weeks from Arizona dispensaries” contradict §36-2804.03(C) and must not be used — the statute controls.

How the medical programme has changed since Prop 207 (summary): (1) Adult-use channel added alongside, not replacing, medical (2020–2021). (2) Dual licensing: the same premises/entity may hold both a nonprofit medical registration and an adult-use establishment licence. (3) Patient numbers fell steadily (81,221 in Dec 2025 → 70,444 in July 2026; see §0.1) as adults 21+ migrated to the adult-use channel, which requires no card, certification, or $150 fee but charges 16% excise + TPT and caps single transactions at 1 oz. (4) Medical tax advantage preserved (no excise); medical purchase allotment preserved (2.5 oz / 14 days). (5) §36-2817 fund transfer ($1,000,000 for Prop 207 education/outreach) — the only Prop 207 amendment to Ch. 28.1 identified. (6) No statutory change to the qualifying-condition list, the 2.5 oz limit, or the 25-mile rule was identified in this research — Verify against the 2021–2026 session laws before stating “no change.”

1.5 Hemp and intoxicating hemp

Definition and THC measurement — current Arizona law

  • Statute (§3-311): “industrial hemp” = Cannabis sativa L. and any part of such plant, whether growing or not, with a delta-9 THC concentration of not more than 0.3% on a dry-weight basis. (Laws 2018, Ch. 287 — Ch. 287.)
  • AZDA gloss (FAQ, official AZDA page): “Total Delta-9 THC concentration of not more than 0.3% on a dry-weight basis. This standard includes the total calculable amount resulting from the chemical conversion of Tetrahydrocannabinolic Acid (THCA).” — AZDA FAQ.
  • Hemp products (§3-311): includes cloth, cordage, fiber, fuel, grain, paint, paper, construction materials, plastics and by-products derived from sterile hemp seed or hemp seed oil; “excludes any product made to be ingested except food made from sterile hemp seed or hemp seed oil.” That exclusion is the textual hinge of the AG’s delta-8 position (see below).
  • Measurement in practice: AZDA sampling within 30 days before intended harvest by AZDA inspectors/trained sampling agents (R3-4-1008, secondary); official analysis by the state agricultural laboratory or a laboratory certified by it. Verify the current AZDA testing protocol (pre-harvest sampling, post-decarboxylation / total-THC calculation method, and whether DEA-registered labs are required for Arizona hemp — the federal DEA-lab enforcement delay to 31 Dec 2026 is given, but Arizona’s own lab requirement was not confirmed).
  • USDA plan: Arizona’s own USDA-approved state plan, effective 1 Jan 2022 (see §1.2(E)). Growers/nurseries must also register crops with USDA-FSA (AZDA FAQ).

Delta-8, delta-9, THCA and other hemp-derived THC — legal status

The controlling Arizona position is the Attorney General’s, not the Farm Bill’s:

  • AG Opinion I24-005 (11 Mar 2024) — question presented: “Does Arizona law permit an entity that is not appropriately licensed by the Arizona Department of Health Services (‘Health Services’) to sell products containing hemp-synthesized intoxicants like delta-8 tetrahydrocannabinol (‘THC’), delta-10 THC, or any other product that has been synthetically converted from naturally occurring cannabidiol (‘CBD’) or other cannabinoids into intoxicating substances?” Answer: “No, Arizona law does not permit the sale of delta-8 and other hemp-synthesized intoxicants by entities that have not been licensed by Health Services. Irrespective of delta-8’s arguable federal legality under the 2018 Agriculture Improvement Act (‘Farm Bill’), Arizona continues to define and regulate ‘industrial hemp’ in a manner that precludes the sale of hemp-synthesized intoxicants in convenience stores, smoke shops, and other unlicensed locales.” Sources: Cannabis Law Report, 19 Mar 2024 — cannabislaw.report; Bradley, 27 Mar 2024 — bradley.com.
  • AG reasoning (four factors, per Bradley): (1) AMMA legislative findings permitting only those meeting statutory conditions to use medical marijuana; (2) Arizona’s “industrial hemp” definition (2010/2018) vs the broader post-dating federal “hemp” definition — Arizona’s definition omits “extracts” and “derivatives” in the AG’s reading; (3) Arizona’s express exclusion of ingestible hemp products (except sterile-seed food) from “hemp products” (§3-311); (4) public-safety concerns (FDA adverse-event reports: 104 reports, 1 Dec 2020–28 Feb 2022, cited by the AG). The AG treats delta-8/delta-10 as Schedule I controlled substances in Arizona (cannabis/marijuana listing in Title 13, Ch. 34) and as a cannabis “derivative” / “manufacture… derivative… or preparation” of THC.
  • Licensed-channel caveat (AG’s own words): “The Opinion, however, should not be construed as a general endorsement of the sale of hemp-synthesized intoxicants by licensed cannabis sellers. … Delta-8 is subject to Health Services’ regulatory oversight, and future regulatory decisions will necessarily be guided by evolving public health and safety information…” (Bradley, quoting the Opinion). In other words: unlicensed sale = unlawful; licensed-dispensary sale = not endorsed, subject to ADHS approval for the specific product/manufacturer — Verify whether ADHS has approved any hemp-synthesized intoxicant product for licensed sale; none was located.
  • 2025 extension to all THC edibles/beverages: AG letters 24 Mar 2025 (see §0.5): “all THC-infused edible products cannot be sold in Arizona by an unlicensed entity. Even if such products are legal under the Farm Bill of 2018, federal law does not preempt Arizona’s more stringent State laws” (Dept. of Liquor advisory quoting the AG). The 2025 letter expressly includes delta-9 THC and “similar derivatives” and cites Virginia Hemp & Agriculture, LLC v. Virginia, 125 F.4th 472, 495 (4th Cir. 2025) for state authority to regulate hemp products within its borders.
  • Delta-9 THC (hemp-derived, ≤0.3% dry weight, in an ingestible product): Under the AG’s 2025 position, an ingestible delta-9 product sold by an unlicensed retailer is unlawful in Arizona even if it meets the federal Farm Bill definition — because §3-311 excludes ingestibles (other than sterile-seed food) from “hemp products” and Prop 207 reserves THC-product sales to licensed establishments. This is the AG’s enforcement position, not a standalone “delta-9 ban” statute — cite it as such.
  • THCA / THCA flower: No Arizona statute, rule, or AG opinion specifically addressing THCA flower was located in this research. AZDA’s total-delta-9 gloss (including THCA conversion) applies to production compliance (pre-harvest crop testing). Whether THCA flower sold at retail by an unlicensed seller is treated as a hemp product, a marijuana product, or a hemp-synthesized intoxicant under the AG position is Unverified — do not state a THCA-flower rule. The federal total-THC change after 11 Dec 2026 will directly affect THCA accounting at the federal level; Arizona’s response is unknown (see below).
  • Other cannabinoids (delta-10, HHC, THCP, etc.): Covered by the AG opinion’s phrase “or any other product that has been synthetically converted from naturally occurring CBD or other cannabinoids into intoxicating substances” and the 2025 letter’s “similar derivatives.” Naturally occurring, non-intoxicating hemp cannabinoids (CBD, CBG, CBN in non-ingestible or sterile-seed-food forms) are not the subject of the AG ban — but CBD ingestibles (gummies, oils for ingestion) fall into the §3-311 ingestible exclusion as a matter of statutory text, even though the AG’s enforcement focus has been intoxicating products. Secondary guides diverge on CBD gummies. Flag this as an unresolved tension: statutory exclusion ≠ express prohibition, and no Arizona court ruling on CBD ingestibles was located. Do not state “CBD gummies are legal/illegal in Arizona” without that ruling.
  • Minimum age for hemp products: No Arizona minimum-age statute for non-intoxicating hemp/CBD products was located. Intoxicating THC products, when lawfully sold, are sold only through licensed establishments to adults 21+ (adult-use) or registered patients (medical, 18+ / minors via caregiver). Do not invent a “21+ for hemp” rule for CBD.
  • Testing and labelling for hemp products: No Arizona hemp-product testing/labelling statute or rule (potency, contaminants, child-resistant packaging, warning labels) for retail hemp products was located, because Arizona has no hemp retail licence. Production-side testing (pre-harvest THC compliance) is AZDA’s. SB 1702 (dead) would have created retail hemp testing/labelling/licensing — it is not law. Flag as a gap: hemp retail testing/labelling is unregulated by a hemp-specific Arizona rule on current evidence; the AG position fills the gap by channelling intoxicating products into the ADHS-licensed system, where R9-18 testing/labelling applies.
  • THC beverages: Two separate regimes — (1) Licensed marijuana beverages: edible products under R9-18-313 (10 mg / 100 mg caps, food-establishment licence). (2) Hemp-derived THC beverages sold by unlicensed retailers/bars: unlawful under the AG’s 24 Mar 2025 position, with liquor-licensee sanctions possible (Dept. of Liquor advisory, above). SB 1556 (2025, dead) would have created an alcohol-model hemp-beverage framework (<30 mg total THC/litre concept) — not law.

State response to the new federal hemp definition and the 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 dates

No response found. No Arizona statute, AZDA rule, ADHS rule, AG opinion, or governor’s statement responding to PL 119-37 §781 or PL 119-103 §2019 was located as of 31 Oct 2026. Arizona’s hemp definition (§3-311, delta-9, 0.3% dry weight) and AZDA’s total-delta-9 FAQ gloss predate the federal change and are not, on their face, the new federal total-THC definition (which counts all THC isomers/analogues and imposes a 0.4 mg total THC per container cap on certain products after 11 Dec 2026, per the given baseline).

What will matter after 11 Dec 2026 (questions for Run 2 / follow-up, not answers): (1) Will Arizona conform §3-311 to the federal total-THC definition, retain its delta-9 definition, or adopt a third definition? (Conforming would require legislation; §3-311 is not a voter measure, so ordinary legislation suffices — Verify whether any 2027 bill is pre-filed.) (2) Will AZDA’s USDA-approved state plan need resubmission to USDA if §3-311 changes? (USDA plan-review rule: substantive revisions altering how the plan functions must be resubmitted — USDA plan-review page.) (3) Does the 0.4 mg/container federal cap affect Arizona-licensed marijuana products? No — on the given baseline it applies to hemp products, not to state-licensed marijuana products, which remain under Chs. 28.1/28.2. Do not conflate the caps. (4) Does the AG’s 2024–2025 position become moot, stricter, or unchanged after 11 Dec 2026? The AG position already treats unlicensed intoxicating hemp sales as unlawful under state law; a stricter federal definition would reinforce, not weaken, that position for products exceeding the federal hemp definition — but products within the new federal definition (e.g. a compliant hemp beverage under federal law) would still face the AG’s state-law ingestible exclusion. Verify with a post-11 Dec 2026 AG/AZDA statement before publishing any “now legal/illegal” conclusion.

Federal hemp dates to keep distinct: 12 Nov 2026 = PL 119-37 §781 effective date, limited by PL 119-103 §2019 until 11 Dec 2026; 11 Dec 2026 = full total-THC definition + 0.4 mg/container cap + end of the federal medical-marijuana rider (given baseline); 31 Dec 2026 = USDA DEA-lab enforcement delay end (given baseline). Arizona’s AZDA FAQ does not mention any of these dates — do not attribute the federal dates to Arizona law.

Failed hemp bills — do not present as law (summary for §1.9 cross-reference)

  • SB 1453 (2023, 56th Leg., 1st Reg. Sess.): would have prohibited possessing/preparing/producing/manufacturing/distributing/selling hemp-derived manufactured impairing cannabinoids without a nonprofit medical dispensary registration or marijuana establishment licence and ADHS approval to convert CBD to THC/an isomer with a substantially similar molecular structure using a chemical catalyst. Fact sheet: SB 1453 fact sheet — not enacted on evidence found; Verify final status.
  • SB 1715 (2022, 55th Leg., 2nd Reg. Sess.): same concept. Introduced text: SB 1715 introduced text (PDF) — not enacted; Verify.
  • HB 2679 (2024, 56th Leg., 2nd Reg. Sess.): hemp-derived products regulation. Summary: HB 2679 summary — not enacted on evidence found; Verify.
  • SB 1271 (2023): hemp-derived products; regulation. Summary: SB 1271 summary (PDF) — not enacted on evidence found; Verify.

1.6 Packaging, labelling, advertising, delivery, zoning and buffer rules

Packaging and labelling — adult-use (R9-18-310, summarised from Sana Packaging’s rule summary — Verify against current R9-18-310 text)

  • Marijuana/marijuana products sold to consumers must be placed in clearly and conspicuously labelled containers with accurate warnings and product information, and in child-resistant packaging when the product exits the marijuana establishment (Sana Packaging — Sana Packaging guide).
  • Consumer label must include (R9-18-310, per Sana): marijuana establishment licence number; amount, strain, and batch number; product form; product weight (as applicable); total THC potency; total CBD potency; potency of any other cannabinoid for which the establishment makes an effect claim; the required ADHS warning; ingredients in order of abundance (for a marijuana product); and the required quick-response (QR) code (or similar technology) linking to product-specific information.
  • Potency must be based on results from a licensed marijuana testing facility, reported as milligrams per designated unit or as a percentage, as applicable.
  • Required ADHS warning addresses: addiction, impairment, driving and machinery, health risks associated with marijuana smoke, pregnancy and the unborn child, and keeping marijuana out of reach of children (Sana summary). Obtain the verbatim ADHS warning text from R9-18-310 before publication — it was not quoted in full in any source opened.
  • False/misleading packaging/labelling prohibited; product descriptions, potency claims, ingredient statements, strain information and graphics are reviewed as a complete presentation (Sana).
  • Sana notes: Arizona’s principal adult-use rules “do not expressly require every immediate consumer package to be opaque, resealable, or tamper-evident” — another requirement, licence condition, operating procedure, locality, delivery method, food-safety standard, or commercial agreement may still require one or more of those features. Do not state an opacity/resealability/tamper-evidence rule for Arizona adult-use packaging without a rule citation.
  • Medical packaging/labelling: governed by Title 9, Ch. 17 (R9-17-3xx) — Verify the medical equivalents of R9-18-310; secondary guides state medical products must be in child-resistant, tamper-evident containers — treat as Verify, not quoted rule text.
  • Universal symbol: secondary guides describe a cannabis-leaf-in-yellow-triangle symbol — Verify whether Arizona requires a universal symbol in R9-18-310 or only in practice; do not assert it without the rule.

Advertising — §36-2859 as amended by HB 2179 (2025, Ch. 166), effective from and after June 30, 2026 (paraphrased as 1 July 2026 in azleg summaries)

See §1.2(B) for verbatim warning, billboard buffer, and $20,000 penalty. In plain terms (HB 2179, Ch. 166 — Ch. 166; CannBus summary — CannBus legal summary): Who may advertise: a marijuana establishment, a nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary, and (as amended) an individual/entity that sells marijuana paraphernalia — each “in accordance with restrictions imposed by this section.” Prohibited: advertising to under-21s, including names resembling/imitating food/drink brands marketed to children; (per CannBus) cartoons, toys, or consumption imagery; advertising without age checks (per CannBus — Verify the age-check mechanism in Ch. 166 text). Mandatory warning on all advertising (verbatim in §1.2(B)): “Do not use marijuana if you are under twenty-one years of age or pregnant. Keep marijuana out of reach of children.” Billboard buffer: prohibited within 1,000 radial feet of any child care center, church, substance abuse recovery facility, public park, public [school — full list Verify from Ch. 166]. Identification: advertising must accurately/legibly identify the responsible establishment/dispensary by name and licence/registration number. Enforcement: Attorney General; civil penalty $20,000 per violation to the Smart and Safe Arizona Fund. SB 1641 (dead) would have extended the same advertising regime to marijuana producers — not law. Pre-HB 2179 advertising law: The original Prop 207 §36-2859 already contained a warning and restrictions; HB 2179 is an amendment, not the first advertising law. Verify the pre-amendment text if the final HTML needs a “before/after” comparison.

Delivery — statute + R9-18-312.01

  • Statutory basis: Prop 207 required ADHS to adopt delivery rules; delivery was prohibited until ADHS adopted rules after 1 Jan 2023 (Legislative Council draft analysis), and “Delivery would be prohibited to any property owned or leased by the federal, state or local government, including the universities under the control of the Arizona Board of Regents” (same analysis). ADHS adopted rules early; adult-use delivery began 1 Nov 2024 (see §0.3).
  • Rule (R9-18-312.01, secondary summary — Verify against current rule text): Delivery must originate from a licensed retail location after the consumer places an order; no warehouse/off-site shipping origin; no distance cap; only licensed marijuana facility agents may deliver (no third-party delivery services); ID verification (21+ government photo ID matching the order) at delivery; medical delivery requires registry-card + photo-ID verification through the electronic verification system before approval and again at delivery; paraphernalia cannot be delivered (Dutchie — Dutchie).
  • Vehicle / trip rules (secondary summary of R9-18-312.01 — Verify): Unmarked vehicle (no cannabis branding), GPS tracking, video surveillance, locked compartment; vehicle may not carry more than $10,000 retail value in one trip; driver may carry no product not tied to a specific customer order (no “extra inventory”); dispensary must maintain a trip plan / daily vehicle-use log with retail value (LegalClarity delivery guide — LegalClarity).
  • Delivery prohibitions: To federal/state/local government property (including Arizona Board of Regents universities); to an address where the property owner has notified the dispensary that delivery is not permitted (LegalClarity — Verify in R9-18-312.01); and in any locality that has prohibited/restricted delivery under §36-2857(A)(6) (see below).
  • Medical delivery: Available longer than adult-use delivery, under Title 9, Ch. 17 and ADHS Medical Marijuana Delivery Guidelines (GD-107-PHS-EDC, cited by LegalClarity). Verify GD-107’s current status and whether it was superseded by the 2024 Ch. 17/18 rule package.
  • No separate delivery licence: Delivery is authorised under the establishment licence (CannBus: “No separate wholesale, transport, delivery, microbusiness or lounge license”). A non-dispensary delivery operator must partner with a licensed dispensary — Verify the partnership model in R9-18-312.01.

Zoning and buffer rules — §36-2857 (verbatim, core) + §36-2806 (medical)

“A. A locality may: 1. Enact reasonable zoning regulations that limit the use of land for marijuana establishments and marijuana testing facilities to specified areas. 2. Limit the number of marijuana establishments or marijuana testing facilities, or both. 3. Prohibit marijuana establishments or marijuana testing facilities, or both. 4. Regulate the time, place and manner of marijuana establishment and marijuana testing facility operations. 5. Establish reasonable restrictions on public signage regarding marijuana, marijuana establishments and marijuana testing facilities. 6. Prohibit or restrict delivery within its jurisdiction.

B. A county may exercise its authority pursuant to subsection A of this section only in unincorporated areas of the county.

C. A locality may not enact any ordinance, regulation or rule that: 1. Is more restrictive than a comparable ordinance, regulation or rule that applies to nonprofit medical marijuana dispensaries. 2. Makes the operation of a marijuana establishment or marijuana testing facility unduly burdensome if the locality has not prohibited marijuana establishments or marijuana testing facilities. 3. Conflicts with this chapter or rules adopted pursuant to this chapter. 4. Prohibits the transportation of marijuana by a marijuana establishment or marijuana testing facility on public roads. 5. [— subsection (C)(5) not reproduced in search results; Verify full (C)(5) — see Part 3.1, where (C)(5) is given as restricting/interfering with dual-licensee co-location.] 6. Except as expressly authorized by this section or section 36-2851, prohibits or restricts any conduct or transaction allowed by this chapter, or imposes any liability or penalty in addition to that prescribed by this chapter for any conduct or transaction constituting a violation of this chapter. 7. Allows a marijuana establishment to locate within five hundred feet of a public or private school, a child care facility or a facility that provides preschool programs.”

Sources: §36-2857 (viewdocument); FindLaw §36-2857.

Plain-English consequences (for Part 3, stated here because §1.6 requires it): A city/town may ban establishments/testing facilities outright, cap their number, zone them to specified areas, regulate time/place/manner, restrict signage, and ban/restrict delivery — but a county may do so only in unincorporated areas. A locality may not ban/restrict possession or personal use by adults (that would violate §36-2857(C)(6) and §36-2852), nor impose extra penalties for chapter-allowed conduct, nor prohibit transport on public roads, nor allow an establishment within 500 feet of a public/private school, child-care facility, or preschool facility (§36-2857(C)(7) — note the phrasing: the locality’s ordinance may not allow such a location; the 500-foot rule is a state floor, not a local option). Parity rule: Adult-use local rules may not be more restrictive than comparable rules for nonprofit medical dispensaries (§36-2857(C)(1)) — illustrated in Pinetop-Lakeside’s 23 Apr 2026 Planning & Zoning Commission materials, which note the Town’s recreational establishments are prohibited unless operated as a dual-licence dispensary, and that §36-2857(C)(1) parity limits stricter recreational standards — Pinetop-Lakeside agenda (PDF). Medical zoning (§36-2806): Counties/cities may enact reasonable zoning for nonprofit dispensaries (Mohave County code, §37.V). Mohave’s example: dispensaries/establishments and associated cultivation in C-MO, M-1, M-2, or M-X zones, at the state-law minimum distance from schools — local example only, not a statewide zone list. State-level buffers beyond §36-2857(C)(7): Advertising billboards: 1,000 radial feet (§36-2859, HB 2179). No other statewide establishment buffer (e.g. from churches, parks, residential zones) was located in statute — local ordinances may impose additional buffers within §36-2857(A) authority, subject to the parity and undue-burden limits. Verify R9-18 for any additional ADHS siting buffer before stating “500 feet is the only buffer.”

1.7 Seed-to-sale tracking system and requirements — CORRECTED FINDING

Arizona does NOT use Metrc (or BioTrack, or any single state-contracted seed-to-sale platform). This is the most common error in secondary sources and must be corrected in any reuse of this file.

Correct position (multiple independent secondary analyses of the statute/rules): “Arizona does not contract with a third-party vendor like METRC… Instead, each licensee is responsible for procuring and maintaining its own compliant tracking system. ADHS can inspect these records during site visits, but the data lives with the dispensary, not in a centralized state database that automatically ingests every transaction in real time.” (LegalClarity — LegalClarity). “Unlike most cannabis states, Arizona does not contract Metrc, BioTrack, or any single statewide seed-to-sale platform for adult-use. Instead, ADHS requires every establishment to procure and maintain its own system to track marijuana at all points of cultivation, manufacturing, and sale, keep records for at least five years, and file a monthly excise (MET-1) report with [ADOR].” (IndicaOnline — IndicaOnline). “A.R.S. §36-2806 dictates inventory control and record-keeping, but does not prescribe a specific software system. Instead, it authorizes ADHS to set rules regarding inventory and record-keeping. Meanwhile A.R.S. §36-2854 addresses marijuana establishment licensing and rule-making authority… again without mandating a particular seed-to-sale software.” (Desert Sounds).

What the licensee’s system must do (statute/rule, via LegalClarity summary of §36-2854): Track cannabis at all points of cultivation, manufacturing, and sale; account for plant propagation, processing, transfers between licensed facilities, and disposal of waste; each entry records who made it and when, with a transactional stamp to prevent tampering/intentional misreporting. Verify the exact R9-17 / R9-18 inventory-control rule numbers and the 5-year retention period against current rules — the 5-year figure is from IndicaOnline, not a quoted rule.

Medical verification system (different system, do not conflate): ADHS operates a medical marijuana electronic verification system to verify patient/caregiver cards and the 2.5 oz / 14-day allotment and to record medical dispensing (Dutchie). “Arizona uses Medical marijuana electronic verification system; no state system for adult-use” (Dutchie). Adult-use sales generate business records (product, quantity, price, date/time) for ADHS inspection and ADOR tax reporting, but individual adult-use purchase history is not reported to a state system (LegalClarity).

Incorrect secondary claims to discard: CannBus Q3 2026’s legal summary states “Every ADHS-licensed marijuana establishment must track inventory in Metrc” (CannBus legal summary) and IndicaOnline’s dispensary-licence guide states “Arizona mandates the Metrc system.” Both contradict the statute/rule analysis above and the Dutchie/IndicaOnline market guide’s own “no state-mandated Metrc” statement. This file follows the no-Metrc position and flags the conflict in research notes, with a final check of R9-18 inventory rules before publication. A CannLabs guide passage attributing “Seed-to-sale tracking through METRC” to Arizona is in fact describing Colorado’s MED rules in context — another source of the error.

Hemp tracking (for Part 7 cross-reference): Hemp production tracking is via AZDA licensing, FSA crop registration, and pre-harvest sampling — not via the marijuana verification system. Verify AZDA’s hemp inventory/reporting rules in Part 7.

1.8 Employment and drug-testing protections

Medical patients — §36-2813 (strong, but qualified, protection)

  • Rule (secondary quotation — Verify against official §36-2813 text before publication): “An employer may not discriminate against a person in hiring, termination, or terms of employment based on that person’s status as a registered qualifying patient, or based on a registered qualifying patient’s positive drug test for marijuana components or metabolites, unless the patient used, possessed, or was impaired by marijuana on the employment premises or during hours of employment.” Sources: US Drug Test Centers; LegalClarity.
  • Federal-benefit exception: The protection also does not apply if employing the patient would cause the employer to lose a monetary or licensing-related benefit under federal law/regulation (§36-2813). Federal contractors and DOT-regulated employers therefore fall outside the protection in practice.
  • “Impairment” is not a positive test: A positive test alone does not prove on-the-job impairment; the employer needs evidence of actual impairment (observable symptoms while working). Whitmire v. Walmart (D. Ariz.) held high metabolite levels alone insufficient to show impairment and that the AMMA implies a private cause of action where no other remedy exists. Verify Whitmire citation, date, and whether it was appealed / remains good law.
  • Safety-sensitive positions: CANAZ states the §36-2813 protection “does not apply if… the position is a ‘safety sensitive position’” and cites §36-2814 (employer may prohibit/discipline use, possession, or impairment while working/on the job). Verify whether “safety-sensitive” is a §36-2813 exception, a §36-2814 rule, or a DTEA (§23-493) definition — the guides conflate the three, and the final HTML must cite the correct section.
  • DTEA safe harbour (§23-493.06): An employer that has a written policy and conforms to the DTEA is protected from a cause of action for acting on on-premises use/possession/impairment. Verify §23-493.06 text.

Adult-use (recreational) users — NO equivalent protection

Prop 207 expressly preserves employer rights: the chapter does not restrict an employer’s ability to maintain a drug-free workplace, enforce policies restricting marijuana use, or prohibit marijuana on company property (§36-2851). An employer that follows DTEA procedures may decline to hire a person who tests positive for THC even for lawful off-duty adult-use. Bottom line: Medical cardholder = protected from adverse action based on status/positive test alone (with on-premises/impairment and federal-benefit exceptions). Adult-use-only user = no statutory testing protection; employer discretion governs, subject only to DTEA procedure.

Drug Testing of Employees Act (DTEA), §§23-493–23-493.12 — procedure

  • Applies to the State, its political subdivisions, and any private business/corporation/labour organisation with at least one full-time employee (§23-493). “Drugs” includes federal-schedule and Arizona-schedule substances plus metabolites (§23-493).
  • Employers are not required to test, but those that do must: maintain a written policy, notify applicants/employees in advance, and keep results confidential (§23-493.04 testing-policy requirements).
  • DOT testing is separate: Arizona medical protections do not modify DOT requirements.
  • Schedule III interaction (open question — Verify): If state-licensed medical marijuana is now federally Schedule III, does that change DTEA “drugs” coverage, DOT treatment, or the §36-2813 federal-benefit exception? No Arizona guidance was found; do not assume the DTEA or DOT position changed on 28 Apr 2026. Flag for post-April 2026 ADHS/DOT guidance check.

Other §36-2813 discrimination contexts (Verify full text)

§36-2813 is headed “Discrimination prohibited” generally (not only employment) — secondary guides state it also covers schools, housing, and professional licensing in the patient/caregiver/dispensary-agent contexts. Verify the full §36-2813 subsections (A)–(D) before listing non-employment protections; landlord/tenant and school rules are Part 12 and must match this section.

PART 2: TAXES

2.1 Adult-use excise tax — 16% — A.R.S. §42-5452

Source opened directly: §42-5452 (azleg) — accessed 31 October 2026. Title 42, Chapter 5, Article 10 (Proposition 207).

Verbatim, §42-5452(A):

“There is levied and the department shall collect an excise tax on all marijuana and marijuana products sold to a consumer by a marijuana establishment at a rate of sixteen percent of the price of the marijuana or marijuana product sold. This subsection does not apply to marijuana dispensed to a registered qualifying patient or registered designated caregiver pursuant to title 36, chapter 28.1 by a dual licensee or nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary.”

Verbatim in substance, §42-5452(B): if the United States levies and collects an excise tax on marijuana and marijuana products, the aggregate of federal and state excise taxes may not exceed 30% of the price, and the state tax “shall be lowered accordingly and automatically on the effective date of the federal excise tax.” No federal marijuana excise tax is in force at the research date, so the 16% rate is not currently reduced by this subsection. Verify before publishing any future federal-excise interaction.

§42-5452(C): a product subject to this tax may not be bundled with a product or service not subject to it. §42-5452(D): the excise tax “shall not be included in computing the tax base, gross proceeds of sales or gross income of a marijuana establishment for purposes of title 42, chapters 5 and 6, and is not subject to any transaction privilege, sales, use or other similar tax levied by a county, city, town or special taxing district.” Practical consequence: transaction privilege tax (TPT) is calculated on the sale price alone, not on sale price plus excise tax. §42-5452(E): “Notwithstanding section 42-3102, the department shall deposit all monies levied and collected pursuant to this section in the smart and safe Arizona fund established by section 36-2856.”

Administration (Arizona Department of Revenue, ADOR — ADOR Marijuana Tax Collection and ADOR adult-use FAQ, accessed 31 October 2026): adult-use retailers need a TPT licence and a Marijuana Excise Tax (MET) registration number. MET is reported on Form MET-1, not Form TPT-2. The MET-1 return is due the 20th day of the month following the reporting period (from the form itself, via a secondary template copy — Verify against the live ADOR form if a primary citation is required). Registration is via paper Form JTM-1 (Medical, Adult Use or Dual License Transaction Privilege/Use/Excise Tax Application), not online. TPT licences are annual and must be renewed, but marijuana businesses should not renew the excise licence separately (ADOR TPT Update, December 2025 archive, via search result).

ADOR business codes found in ADOR material: adult-use retail 420, medical retail 203, excise 920, ancillary/accessory marijuana retail 366; single-item portion over $14,338 codes include adult-use 686 and medical 687. These are filing-code details, not statute. The ADOR February 1, 2026 Transaction Privilege and Other Tax Rate Tables state: “MEDICAL AND ADULT USE MARIJUANA TPT RATES and CODES ARE LOCATED ON PAGES 20 – 43” — ADOR TPT Rate Table 1 Feb 2026 (PDF, search result). That confirms marijuana has its own TPT code pages; it does not create a separate marijuana TPT rate (see §2.2).

2.2 Transaction privilege tax (TPT) and the ban on extra marijuana taxes — A.R.S. §36-2864

Source opened directly: §36-2864 (azleg) — accessed 31 October 2026.

Verbatim, §36-2864(A):

“For purposes of the transaction privilege tax and use tax levied and collected pursuant to title 42, chapters 5 and 6, marijuana and marijuana products are tangible personal property defined in section 42-5001 and are subject to the transaction privilege tax in the retail classification and use tax.”

Verbatim, §36-2864(B):

“Except as provided in subsection A of this section and section 42-5452, this state and localities may not levy or collect additional taxes of any kind on the sale of marijuana or marijuana products and may not levy or collect any fees or assessments of any kind on the sale of marijuana or marijuana products or on the licensing, operations or activities of marijuana establishments or marijuana testing facilities, unless the fee or assessment is of general applicability to individuals or businesses that are not engaged in the sale of marijuana or marijuana products.”

§36-2864(C): the prohibition does not apply to uniform increases to the retail TPT rate or use tax rate by the state or a locality, or to uniform increases to fees/assessments allowed under subsection B.

Consequences, stated plainly: The state retail TPT rate is 5.6% (retail classification), consistently stated by ADOR. The state-only adult-use rate before county/city TPT is therefore 21.6% (5.6% + 16%). There is no Arizona state marijuana-specific local tax. What a locality adds is its general retail TPT rate (plus any county rate), not a marijuana surcharge. Any “local marijuana tax” figure must be flagged unless it is shown to be a uniform/general rate (see §3.7 for the Prescott example, which is a uniform dedicated TPT increase, not a marijuana-only tax). TPT revenue from marijuana follows the normal TPT path — state general fund plus shared revenue to counties/cities and education distributions, as with any other retail business — per ADOR/JLBC/secondary summaries. The exact state/county/city/education split for marijuana TPT was not extracted from a primary ADOR distribution table in this research. The follow-up source is the ADOR “Collections/Distribution by Jurisdiction Type” PDF for September 2026 (see §2.8). Do not invent a split. The ADOR Total Collections PDF opened 31 October 2026 does break TPT into State General Fund, County, City and Education categories, but its text extraction scrambles column alignment, so no per-jurisdiction split is quoted from it here — Verify by reading that PDF visually.

2.3 Medical marijuana tax treatment

  • No 16% excise tax on marijuana dispensed to a registered qualifying patient or registered designated caregiver under Title 36, Chapter 28.1 by a dual licensee or nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary — §42-5452(A) exception quoted in §2.1.
  • Medical sales are subject to retail TPT (and use tax) under §36-2864(A). ADOR states this plainly: “Medical marijuana is subject only to retail TPT and not MET.”
  • No separate medical excise tax was found in current law. A 2018 proposal (SB 1282, 53rd Legislature, 2nd Regular Session — 3.5% medical excise tax for the Classroom Site Fund) was a bill summary/fact sheet only and is proposed, not law; its status was not confirmed as enacted and it must not be presented as law.
  • Dual-licensee split: a dual licensee must treat the medical sale (patient/caregiver, Chapter 28.1) and the adult-use sale (consumer, Chapter 28.2) as different tax events — excise applies only to the latter. Under the given federal baseline, federal §280E relief applies to medical activity only; adult-use activity remains Schedule I and fully subject to §280E, so dual licensees must split activity and records. Whether a tribal medical programme counts as “state-licensed” for this purpose is Verify (no tribal tax treatment was confirmed in the Parts 2–3 searches; tribal issues belong to Run 2 Part 10).

2.4 Smart and Safe Arizona Fund — A.R.S. §36-2856

Source opened directly: §36-2856 (azleg) — accessed 31 October 2026. Note the azleg compilation caveat: the online version reflects law effective 1 January of the year following the most recent legislative session (57th Legislature, 2nd Regular Session) and the official version is published by Thomson Reuters.

§36-2856(A), in substance (verbatim elements quoted): the fund is established consisting of all monies deposited pursuant to §§36-2853, 36-2854, 36-2859, 42-5452 and 42-5453, private donations and interest earned; monies are continuously appropriated, may not be transferred to any other fund except as provided in the section, do not revert to the state general fund, are exempt from §35-190 lapsing, and the State Treasurer administers the fund.

Version difference — flag, do not silently mix: the 2021 Justia text listed deposits under §§36-2854, 42-5452 and 42-5503; the current azleg text lists §§36-2853, 36-2854, 36-2859, 42-5452 and 42-5453. The 2023 change (HB 2446, Chapter 0035, 56th Legislature, 1st Regular Session — HB 2446, Ch. 0035, search result) amended §36-2856 to replace 42-5503 with 42-5453, retroactive to 31 December 2020.

§36-2856(B) — first-use order, before any percentage distribution. The fund first pays: (1) actual reasonable costs of ADHS to implement/carry out/enforce Chapter 28.2 and its rules; (2) actual reasonable costs of ADOR to impose/enforce §42-5452; (3) actual reasonable costs of the Supreme Court and Department of Public Safety to process expungement petitions/orders under §36-2862 and otherwise implement §36-2862; (4) actual reasonable costs of the State Treasurer to administer the fund; (5) any other mandatory expenditure of state revenues required by Chapter 28.2.

§36-2856(C): the Treasurer may prescribe forms for subsection B transfers.

§36-2856(D): “On or before June 30 and December 31 of each year, the state treasurer shall transfer all monies in the smart and safe Arizona fund in excess of the amounts paid pursuant to subsection B of this section as follows:”

ShareRecipient and statutory purpose
33%Community college districts and provisional community college districts, but not community college tuition financing districts under §15-1409, for workforce development, job training, career and technical education, and science, technology, engineering and mathematics programs — as follows: (a) 15% of the 33% divided equally between each community college district; (b) 0.5% of the 33% divided equally between each provisional community college district, if any exist; (c) the remainder to community college districts and provisional districts in proportion to each district’s full-time equivalent student enrollment (FTSE) percentage of total statewide audited FTSE in the preceding fiscal year under §15-1466.01.
31.4%Municipal police departments, municipal fire departments, fire districts and joint powers authorities under Title 48, Chapter 5, and county sheriffs’ departments, in proportion to the number of members as defined in §38-842 for each agency in the Public Safety Personnel Retirement System (Title 38, Chapter 5, Article 4) and the Public Safety Personnel Defined Contribution Retirement Plan (Article 4.1), for personnel costs.
25.4%Arizona Highway User Revenue Fund under §28-6533.
10%Justice Reinvestment Fund under §36-2863 (see §2.5).
0.2%Attorney General to enforce Chapter 28.2 or to grant to localities to enforce it.

The percentages sum to 100.0% (33 + 31.4 + 25.4 + 10 + 0.2). §36-2856(E): transferred/received monies are in addition to other appropriations and may not supplant/replace/reduce other funding; and are not considered local revenues for Arizona Constitution Article IX, §§20 and 21.

Failed amendment — not law: SB 1299 (57th Legislature, 1st Regular Session, 2025) would have added §36-2856.01 (Safe Community Enforcement Fund) and diverted $2,500,000 per distribution (on or before each 30 June and 31 December), i.e. $5,000,000 per year, FY2025-2026 through FY2029-2030, from the Smart and Safe Fund to ADHS/DPS enforcement. The House-engrossed version itself states: “(Sections 36-2856, 36-2856.01 and 36-2863 not enacted — failed to obtain the three-fourths vote pursuant to article IV, part 1, section 1, Constitution of Arizona.)” — SB 1299 House-engrossed (PDF). Do not present that diversion as current law. Similarly, SB 1324 (2022) and SB 1715 (2022) proposals to change the §36-2856 distribution were not adopted in the current text opened.

2.5 Justice Reinvestment Fund — A.R.S. §36-2863

Source opened directly: §36-2863 (azleg) — accessed 31 October 2026.

§36-2863(A): fund established from monies deposited under §36-2856 plus interest; continuously appropriated; no transfer except as provided; no reversion to general fund; exempt from §35-190; Treasurer administers. §36-2863(B): first pay (1) reasonable Treasurer administration costs, (2) reasonable ADHS administrative costs to carry out duties under the section. §36-2863(C): on or before June 30 and December 31 each year, the Treasurer transfers the excess as follows: (1) 35% to county public health departments in proportion to county population under the most recent U.S. decennial census, to provide justice reinvestment programmes or distribute grants to qualified nonprofit organisations to provide them in that county; (2) 35% to ADHS to distribute grants to qualified nonprofit organisations providing justice reinvestment programmes statewide; (3) 30% to ADHS to address important public health issues affecting the state. §36-2863(D): grants exempt from Title 41, Chapter 23; each grantee must provide an annual report detailing use of granted monies. §36-2863(E): monies transferred/received are not local revenues for Constitution Article IX, §20. §36-2863(G) definition of “justice reinvestment programs”: (1) public and behavioural health, including evidence-based/informed substance-use prevention, treatment and early intervention; (2) restorative justice, jail diversion, workforce development, industry-specific technical assistance or mentoring for economically disadvantaged persons in communities disproportionately impacted by high arrest/incarceration rates; (3) addressing underlying causes of crime, reducing drug-related arrests and reducing the prison population; (4) creating/developing technology and programmes to assist restoration of civil rights and expungement of criminal records.

Related reporting (follow-up source, not extracted): §36-147 requires ADHS, on or before 1 July each year, to submit to the Joint Legislative Budget Committee (JLBC) an expenditure report for the preceding fiscal year on monies from the Medical Marijuana Fund (§36-2817) and the Justice Reinvestment Fund (§36-2863), including expenditures by programme and a list of grants distributed. Grant-level amounts were not extracted in this research — Verify against that JLBC report if grant-level figures are needed.

2.6 IRS §280E and Arizona decoupling — A.R.S. §43-108

Source opened directly: §43-108 (azleg) — accessed 31 October 2026.

Verbatim, §43-108(A):

“Notwithstanding any other law, in computing Arizona adjusted gross income or Arizona taxable income for a taxpayer, all ordinary and necessary expenses paid or incurred during the taxable year in carrying on a trade or business as a marijuana establishment, marijuana testing facility, or dual licensee that elects to operate on a for-profit basis pursuant to title 36, chapter 28.2 shall be subtracted from Arizona gross income to the extent not already excluded from Arizona gross income.”

§43-108(B): “dual licensee,” “marijuana establishment,” and “marijuana testing facility” have the same meanings as in §36-2850.

Arizona therefore decouples from federal §280E at the state income-tax level for the §43-108 class, even before the given federal Schedule III change. This is the correct current reading, with the entity limits in the statute.

ADOR implementation: Arizona Schedule DFE (Disallowed Federal Expenses for Marijuana Establishments), 2025 instructions via search result — report only ordinary and necessary expenses that would have been deductible federally if not governed by IRC §280E; do not include amounts already in cost of goods sold. The instructions state: “Do not complete this form if you are a nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary only or are a Dual Licensee” — the sentence is truncated in the snippet; quote only the confirmed portion and flag the full exclusion wording as Verify against the live form.

Related exemption (via azleg view-document search result for §43-1201 — open the live page before finalising if a directly-opened citation is required): §43-1201(B) — “Nonprofit medical marijuana dispensaries under title 36, chapter 28.1, are exempt from the taxes imposed under this title [Title 43, Taxation of Income].”

Conflict to flag explicitly: an older secondary source (AndreTaxCo, Arizona Cannabis 280E page) states Arizona conforms to the IRC with no provision stating §280E does not apply. That is contradicted by the current §43-108 text and Schedule DFE and must not be carried forward; the statute controls. Secondary CannBus summaries describe the same decoupling and note the 28 April 2026 federal Schedule III order covers state-medical-licensed marijuana only, so adult-use sales stay under federal §280E until a broader decision — consistent with the given baseline, but CannBus is secondary and must be labelled as such.

Critical Verify — dual-licensee medical-portion limitation: a 2021 Form 140 instruction note states a for-profit dual licensee does not qualify for a subtraction for the medical marijuana portion of the business. Reconcile this against the current Schedule DFE instructions and §43-108 before stating a single rule for dual licensees. In short: federally, medical activity that is state-licensed moves out of §280E on 28 April 2026 while adult-use remains subject; at the Arizona level, §43-108 already allowed the subtraction for qualifying establishments/testing facilities/for-profit dual licensees. A nonprofit-only dispensary gets the §43-1201 income-tax exemption but does not file Schedule DFE. The two regimes have different entity/activity conditions, so dual licensees still need to separate medical vs adult-use books.

2.7 Hemp product tax — no Arizona hemp-specific excise tax located

Negative finding, stated as a search result, not as proof of exemption from TPT: no Arizona hemp-specific excise tax was found in current law (search route: azleg Title 42, ADOR hemp pages, SB 1556 history). Hemp/cannabis-adjacent retail sales fall under the ordinary retail TPT framework where they are tangible personal property; no §42-5452-type rate applies to hemp products as such. Verify hemp/CBD retail classification against ADOR retail classification guidance — no ADOR hemp/CBD retail classification guidance was opened in this research.

SB 1556 (2025), “adult hemp beverages; policies; procedures” — PROPOSED, DEAD, not law: Would have created a Department of Liquor Licenses and Control (DLLC) licensing regime for adult hemp beverages, with testing/labelling, a 10 mg total THC per single-serve container limit (container at least 200 mL / 6.76 oz in the Senate-engrossed summary), and a luxury privilege tax of $1.50 per gallon on each sealed container (proportionate for other quantities), plus ordinary state/local TPT. Legislative Council fiscal note (Senate-engrossed version — SB 1556 fiscal note (PDF, search result)): estimated $105 million annual Arizona hemp-beverage sales, $8.4 million combined TPT + luxury tax, $5.9 million state TPT, $2.9 million net total state revenue increase after RTD spirits effects; the note stated the 16% marijuana excise tax was not believed applicable to DLLC-authorised hemp beverages under that bill. Status: Senate passed 23–6 on 5 March 2025 (third reading); transmitted to House; House Commerce Committee action withdrawn 27 March 2025; removed from House Appropriations agenda and failed to be heard by deadline — reported as dead in the 2025 session by LegiScan, TrackBill, and Arizona cannabis press (April 2025). A 2026 bill also numbered SB 1556 is a different subject (forged/fraudulent deeds) — do not confuse the two. Because SB 1556 died, its $1.50/gallon tax, DLLC licensing, and age/packaging rules must not appear in the current-law tax table. Attorney General context (for Part 1 cross-reference, not a tax): AG Opinion I24-005 (11 March 2024) and a March 2025 AG warning letter stating enforcement against unlicensed THC edibles/beverages could begin after 24 April 2025. No hemp-beverage tax follows from the opinion itself. Under the given federal baseline, after 11 December 2026 the federal total-THC definition and 0.4 mg/container cap apply to hemp products; that federal change does not itself create an Arizona hemp excise tax.

2.8 Revenue figures — official ADOR reports, with bases distinguished

Official source page (opened directly): ADOR “Marijuana Tax Collection” at azdor.gov Marijuana Tax Collection — accessed 31 October 2026 — states: “For adult use (recreational) marijuana, there is a transaction privilege tax (TPT) rate and an excise tax (16%) on the retail sales.” As opened it listed September 2026 Reports: (1) Collections and Taxable Sales by Period Covered (PDF), (2) Collections/Distribution by Jurisdiction Type (PDF), (3) Total Collections (PDF), source ADOR Office of Economic Research and Analysis, updated monthly. The underlying PDFs are overwritten in place each month, so cite the report month, not just the URL.

Two bases — do not mix them: Period covered = the month in which the transaction occurred, different from the month the tax revenue was received. Taxable sales are estimated based on revenue received and change as late returns/payments/amendments/audits are processed. Month received / Total Collections = state, county, city and education TPT collections plus marijuana excise tax, in the month received. ADOR defines “Total Collections” this way.

ADOR Total Collections PDF — opened directly 31 October 2026 at MJ_byTotalCollectionsCategory.pdf (the September 2026 edition, via the ADOR page’s “Total Collections” link). Its text extraction scrambles column alignment and truncates trailing digits, so the figures below are given as the PDF’s legible prefixes cross-checked against secondary reporting of the same ADOR table; the last digits are secondary-confirmed, not directly extracted:

The tax-revenue implication relevant to Part 2 is the medical TPT collapse vs relatively stable excise — but it must be labelled secondary/estimated, not ADOR-quoted.

2.9 Worked examples (arithmetic, not quoted rates)

On a $100 adult-use sale in Phoenix at the current ordinary retail combined TPT rate of 9.1% (5.6% state + 0.7% Maricopa County + 2.8% city, effective 1 July 2025, Ord. G-7369), tax = $16.00 excise + $9.10 TPT = $25.10 total, because TPT is on the $100 price (excise excluded from the TPT base, §42-5452(D)). The 8.6% figure was Phoenix’s pre-1 July 2025 combined rate (5.6% + 0.7% + 2.3%) and must not be used as a current Phoenix rate. A secondary Tucson interview example states “a $100 transaction yields about $25 in taxes” — consistent with the arithmetic, but cite the arithmetic, not the interview, as the authority. A $100 medical sale in Phoenix pays $9.10 TPT and no excise. Combined adult-use rates vary by address because city/county TPT varies (see §3.7); do not use any single city’s rate as a statewide rate.

  2. Dual-licensee medical-portion subtraction limitation (2021 Form 140 note vs current Schedule DFE/§43-108).
  3. ADOR September 2026 PDF exact totals (especially September 2026 total and excise split) and the TPT distribution split (state/county/city/education) from the “Collections/Distribution by Jurisdiction Type” PDF.
  4. December 2025 Treasurer highway/AG dollar amounts and June 2025 agency-reimbursement breakdown beyond the Treasurer line (calculated figures above are labelled CALCULATED).
  5. Hemp/CBD retail TPT classification guidance from ADOR.
  6. Tribal tax treatment (Run 2 Part 10).
  7. Any future federal excise tax triggering the §42-5452(B) 30% aggregate cap.

PART 3: LOCAL RULES

3.1 State framework — who can do what

Adult-use — A.R.S. §36-2857, opened directly at §36-2857 (azleg) — accessed 31 October 2026. Full text (including the azleg “(Caution: 1998 Prop. 105 applies)” note, which does not change the rate or powers quoted):

“A. A locality may: 1. Enact reasonable zoning regulations that limit the use of land for marijuana establishments and marijuana testing facilities to specified areas. 2. Limit the number of marijuana establishments or marijuana testing facilities, or both. 3. Prohibit marijuana establishments or marijuana testing facilities, or both. 4. Regulate the time, place and manner of marijuana establishment and marijuana testing facility operations. 5. Establish reasonable restrictions on public signage regarding marijuana, marijuana establishments and marijuana testing facilities. 6. Prohibit or restrict delivery within its jurisdiction.

B. A county may exercise its authority pursuant to subsection A of this section only in unincorporated areas of the county.

C. A locality may not enact any ordinance, regulation or rule that: 1. Is more restrictive than a comparable ordinance, regulation or rule that applies to nonprofit medical marijuana dispensaries. 2. Makes the operation of a marijuana establishment or marijuana testing facility unduly burdensome if the locality has not prohibited marijuana establishments or marijuana testing facilities. 3. Conflicts with this chapter or rules adopted pursuant to this chapter. 4. Prohibits the transportation of marijuana by a marijuana establishment or marijuana testing facility on public roads. 5. Restricts or interferes with the ability of a dual licensee or an entity eligible to become a dual licensee to operate a nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary and a marijuana establishment cooperatively at shared locations. 6. Except as expressly authorized by this section or section 36-2851, prohibits or restricts any conduct or transaction allowed by this chapter, or imposes any liability or penalty in addition to that prescribed by this chapter for any conduct or transaction constituting a violation of this chapter. 7. Allows a marijuana establishment to locate within five hundred feet of a public or private school, a child care facility or a facility that provides preschool programs.”

Proposed change — not law: SB 1641 (57th Legislature, 2nd Regular Session, 2026, Senate-engrossed) would have amended §36-2857 to add “marijuana producers” alongside establishments/testing facilities and restated the delivery prohibition. It is bill text, not the current statute opened. Do not merge its “producer” language into the current-law quote.

Medical — A.R.S. §36-2806.01, via azleg view-document search result at §36-2806.01 (viewdocument):

“Cities, towns and counties may enact reasonable zoning regulations that limit the use of land for registered nonprofit medical marijuana dispensaries to specified areas in the manner provided in title 9, chapter 4, article 6.1, and title 11, chapter 6, article 2.”

Medical law therefore gives localities reasonable zoning power, not an express statutory power to ban dispensaries outright in the same words as §36-2857(A)(3). The practical history is that many localities used zoning plus a “dual-licensee only” rule to achieve a near-ban on standalone adult-use retail while preserving medical/dual sites. The Maricopa County litigation (§3.4, unincorporated Maricopa County) shows the limit: a zoning scheme that categorically made dispensaries impossible was struck down under the medical act.

Level split — state this explicitly: “Locality” covers cities, towns and counties, but a county acts only in unincorporated areas (§36-2857(B)). Inside an incorporated city/town, the city/town code governs, not the county code. Testing facilities are expressly included in §36-2857(A) (zoning, caps, bans, time/place/manner, signage, delivery). Delivery is a locality power under (A)(6) — a city can prohibit/restrict delivery into/inside its jurisdiction, but cannot prohibit transport through on public roads under (C)(4). Possession, home grow, and personal-use conduct are state-law matters under Chapters 28.1/28.2 and are not a local ban power. A locality can ban establishments/testing facilities and can prohibit/restrict delivery, but it cannot ban possession itself (possession is governed by Chapter 28.2, and §36-2857(C)(6) bars prohibiting/restricting conduct allowed by the chapter except as expressly authorised), cannot block transport on public roads, cannot interfere with dual-licensee co-location, and cannot go below the 500-foot school/childcare/preschool floor. The “more restrictive than medical dispensaries” limit in (C)(1) is the main brake on adult-use-only restrictions where the locality has not banned establishments outright.

State floor for siting (cross-reference to Part 1): §36-2857(C)(7) bars a locality from allowing an establishment within 500 feet of a public/private school, childcare facility, or preschool facility. Medical dispensary siting under §36-2804 also uses a 500-foot school/childcare/preschool buffer (secondary summaries; Verify against the live §36-2804 text before finalising). Local buffers are almost always larger and stack on top of the state floor.

3.2 The recurring Valley pattern — “standalone adult-use prohibited; dual licensee excepted”

This is not a total ban on marijuana sales in those cities. The typical code says, in substance: “To the fullest extent allowable by law, operation of a marijuana establishment is prohibited, except where authorised for a dual licensee who operates both a licensed nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary and a licensed marijuana establishment cooperatively in a shared location and has not forfeited/terminated the medical registration.” Consequences: a social-equity-only or adult-use-only licence (not paired with a medical registration at the same site) cannot open retail in such a city; a dual site can. Whether this pattern survives §36-2857(C)(1) and (C)(5) for a given city is a legal question, not a fact to decide in Part 3 — flag it as a Verify/legal-interpretation note where the code is old or was adopted before Proposition 207.

3.3 No official statewide ban/allow list was located

Finding, stated explicitly: no ADHS, League of Arizona Cities and Towns, or state publication listing every jurisdiction as ban/allow was located in the searches. ADHS publishes licensee lists by name/licence number (Part 5 scope), not a local-ban register. Stating “none located” is not proof none exists — follow-up routes are ADHS licensee lists, League model-ordinance surveys, and live code checks for each jurisdiction. The jurisdiction-by-jurisdiction table in §3.4 is therefore the evidence, not a substitute statewide list.

3.4 Jurisdiction-by-jurisdiction table (all required jurisdictions)

Distances are as stated in the cited source; measurement methods differ (exterior wall to property line, property line to property line, straight line without regard to intervening structures, shortest horizontal line) — cite the method per city where captured, and flag where it was not. Hours cluster at 7:00/8:00/9:00 a.m. opening and 8:00/9:00/10:00 p.m. closing, but each city’s hours differ and must be cited city-by-city, not as a statewide rule.

Oct 2026 hearing notice: use permit to allow a medical marijuana dispensary facility, with variances to allow the facility within 1 mile of another medical marijuana use, within 500 feet of R-4 and R-4A residential zoning districts, within 1,284 feet of a school where 1,320 feet is required between a dispensary facility and a school, and within 1,320 feet of a place of worship. Companion 2025–2026 hearing results: cultivation/infusion facilities require a use permit with minimum 5,280 feet separation from the same type of use, and minimum 1,320 feet from a place of worship or homeless shelter. In White Mountain Health Center, Inc. v. Maricopa County (Maricopa County Superior Court No. CV2012-053585; Court of Appeals Division One, Nos. 1 CA-CV 12-0831 / 13-0697 / 14-0372, consolidated, filed 20 December 2016 — Justia opinion), Judge Michael Gordon (14 October 2013 pretrial verdict, reported) held the county ordinance was a “transparent attempt” to keep dispensaries out of unincorporated areas and that a county zoning ordinance posing a categorical prohibition of medical marijuana violates state law limiting county zoning jurisdiction. The Board of Supervisors then unanimously approved (4–0, January 2014, reported 30 January 2014) a zoning ordinance permitting dispensaries and cultivation sites in non-residential areas under county zoning control — dispensaries in commercial zones farther from residential areas and in industrial zones; cultivation in industrial zones. Secondary summary of the resulting Maricopa County Zoning Ordinance (MCZO) distances: not within 1,320 feet of a school/daycare/church (closest property line to closest property line), not within 500 feet of a residential zone, not within 1,320 feet of another dispensary. These distances are from a secondary dispensary-directory summary — flag as Verify against the live MCZO. The current MCZO marijuana section number was not opened. The county’s permissive-zoning structure matters: the MCZO (adopted 1969) prohibits a use unless it is listed as permitted; the 2010 text amendment (TA2010017) created a Special Use Permit category for dispensaries in C-2 and C-3 districts. Present this as litigation history, not as a claim that the current MCZO still uses the 2010 Special Use Permit procedure.White Mountain opinion (Justia); secondary MCZO summary.
