3.5 Delivery bans/restrictions — the most conflict-prone Part 3 item

State power is clear (§36-2857(A)(6)): a locality may prohibit or restrict delivery within its jurisdiction. What each locality has actually done is not uniformly documented in official code in the searches, and two secondary sources directly conflict on Flagstaff:

  • Flagstaff: Chapter 6-10, §6-10-001-0006 (Ord. 2021-03) as reproduced in a sample/adopted text: “It is unlawful to facilitate or accept orders for delivery, or to deliver marijuana or marijuana products in Flagstaff. The prohibition in this Section 6-10-001-0006 does not apply when delivery is done by a nonprofit medical marijuana [dispensary in compliance with ADHS rules]” (sentence truncated in the snippet — Verify the full exception). A June 2025 EIN Presswire release (Kind Meds / Love Bud) claims Flagstaff is the only opt-out in Arizona and that “currently, delivery is available throughout the state, with the only exception being Flagstaff.” A Prescott Valley Planning and Zoning Commission minutes item (8 September 2025, ZOA25-002) records a presenter stating Yavapai County, Prescott, Sedona, Cottonwood and Flagstaff have delivery services, then a correction by the applicant’s attorney that Flagstaff, Maricopa County and Scottsdale all have deliveries, while a commissioner stated Prescott does not allow delivery and Yavapai County does not allow delivery. Present this as an unresolved conflict: the Flagstaff code text (2021) prohibits non-medical delivery, but 2025 meeting/press claims about current delivery practice conflict and were not resolved against a live Flagstaff code check. Do not state “only Flagstaff bans delivery” as fact.
  • Casa Grande: §17.52.590(F) prohibits soliciting/facilitating/accepting delivery orders or delivering in the city, except delivery by a nonprofit medical dispensary in compliance with ADHS medical programme rules. This is the clearest code-text delivery carve-out found.
  • Prescott Valley: Town Code §10-07-050, “Marijuana Delivery Prohibited; Exception” (Ord. 887, 28 January 2021) originally prohibited facilitating/accepting delivery orders or delivering in the town except as provided by §36-2801 et seq. The section was repealed and reenacted (Ord. 2025-962, 9 October 2025) with the heading “Marijuana Delivery Prohibited; Exception. Reserved.” and the same prohibition text reproduced in the exhibit — the exhibit’s “Reserved” heading vs operative text is ambiguous. A September 2025 Prescott Valley P&Z item (ZOA25-002) proposed amending §13-08-040 to allow delivery within town limits, with a map of delivery jurisdictions presented. Whether that amendment was adopted after September 2025 was not confirmed — mark Prescott Valley delivery status as Verify (pending post-September 2025 code check).
  • Chandler: 2018 Ord. 4855 “no longer prohibit[s] off-site deliveries” (per the 2025 Chandler memo) — i.e., Chandler removed a delivery prohibition in 2018. Present as a removal, not as a current delivery licence rule.
  • Scottsdale: §25-6 (secondary summary) allows dual-licensed establishments and nonprofit dispensaries to deliver. Flag as secondary.
  • Tempe, Mesa, Glendale, Peoria, Phoenix, Tucson, Yuma, Lake Havasu City, Sedona, City of Maricopa, Surprise: no delivery-prohibition code section was opened for these jurisdictions in this research, except for the general state power. Do not list them as “delivery allowed” or “delivery banned” without a live-code check; state “no delivery ordinance located in this research — Verify.”
  • Transport vs delivery distinction to keep explicit: §36-2857(C)(4) bars a locality from prohibiting transportation on public roads. A delivery ban governs the delivery transaction/destination inside the jurisdiction, not through-transport.

3.6 Moratoriums, caps, and other local limits found

  • Caps (numerical limits on number of establishments/dispensaries): Yuma: one dual-purpose facility per 50,000 population (§154-22.03(H)). Sedona: two dispensaries within city limits — proposal-stage only (May–June 2021), Verify adoption. Tucson: a cap on the number of dispensaries was adopted 23 February 2016 (official Tucson history) — the cap number itself was not extracted; mark Verify. State-level caps (for context, Part 4 scope, not local): medical 1 per 10 pharmacies, adult-use licensing ratios — do not import Part 4 caps into Part 3 except to note that local caps sit on top of state caps.
  • Moratoriums: no current, dated local moratorium ordinance (with start/end dates) was located for any required jurisdiction in the searches. Older 2011-era resistance (Maricopa County’s blocking classification, Gilbert/Chandler drafting delays) is history, not a current moratorium. State explicitly: “No current moratorium found in this research for the jurisdictions checked; a moratorium search of council minutes after 2021 was not completed — Verify if a moratorium sweep is needed.”
  • Zoning districts pattern (to summarise, not to over-generalise): dispensaries are typically confined to commercial (C-2/C-3/G-O) and sometimes industrial districts, with cultivation/manufacturing in industrial (M-1/M-2/I-1/I-2) districts; residential zones are excluded everywhere checked.
  • Buffer pattern: the state floor is 500 feet (schools/childcare/preschool). Local buffers found range from 500 feet (Lake Havasu City, Flagstaff for protected uses, Prescott Valley for dual-vs-dual/residential boundaries) to 1,000 feet (Yuma, Peoria for schools/daycare, Marana/Pima examples), 1,320 feet (Phoenix, Mesa for schools/residential in some versions, Tempe 2015 text, Glendale for schools), 2,000 feet (Tucson inter-dispensary, Flagstaff inter-establishment, Marana inter-dispensary), 2,640 feet (Peoria inter-dispensary, = ½ mile), and 5,280 feet (1 mile: Mesa/Glendale/Yuma inter-dispensary, Peoria in older summaries). Measurement methods differ — cite the method per city where captured, and flag where it was not.

3.7 Local fees and local tax rates

Local fees: under §36-2864(B), a locality may not levy fees/assessments on the sale, licensing, operations or activities of establishments/testing facilities unless the fee is of general applicability. Therefore: Generally-applicable fees that do apply include city/town business licences/registrations (e.g., Chandler business registration/licence, Casa Grande business licence applicable to medical uses, Flagstaff business licence, Marana business licence, Glendale city medical establishment licence under Chapter 21.4 — note Glendale’s Chapter 21.4 licence is marijuana-specific in name, so its current fee status under §36-2864(B) is a Verify/legal question, not a settled fee figure). Zoning/conditional-use, building, fire-inspection and plan-review fees are generally-applicable processes, but no dollar fee schedule for any required jurisdiction was extracted from an official fee schedule in this research. Do not invent fee amounts. State: “No marijuana-specific local fee amount was verified from an official fee schedule in this research; generally-applicable business-licence and zoning fees apply — Verify against each city’s current fee schedule.” State fees (ADHS licence/registration fees deposited into the Smart and Safe Fund under §§36-2854/36-2856) belong to Part 4, not Part 3(4).

Local TPT rates (general retail rates, not marijuana-specific taxes): ADOR is the authoritative source: ADOR Tax Rate Tables, updated monthly, and the ADOR TPT Update newsletter, plus the AZTaxes.gov Tax Rate Look-Up Tool (address/ZIP-based). The live January/February 2026 rate table’s city-by-city retail pages were not fully opened in this research — all combined rates below are secondary unless marked ADOR, and must be flagged Verify against the ADOR table.

Official ADOR city-profile figures located via search result (ADOR domain, accessed 31 October 2026): Tucson: Retail Sales (business code 017) 2.60% — ADOR Tucson city profile. Combined Tucson retail TPT on that basis = 5.6% state + 0.5% Pima County + 2.6% city = 8.7% (arithmetic from the ADOR city rate; county component secondary — Verify against the ADOR county table). Prescott: Retail Sales (017) 2.95% — ADOR Prescott city profile. History on the same ADOR page: 2.95% effective April 1, 2025; 2.00% effective January 1, 2023; 2.75% effective January 1, 2018. The April 2025 step is the dedicated public-safety TPT below.

Official ADOR TPT Update example (primary, February 2025 update, via search result): City of Prescott — effective 1 April 2025, Ordinance 2024-1858 (adopted 10 December 2024, voter-approved 5 November 2024) adopted a dedicated TPT of 0.95% for public safety, reducing to 0.75% by 31 December 2035, applying to business classifications including Medical Marijuana Retail Sales (203), Adult Use Marijuana Retail Sales (420), and Retail Sales (Accessories/Ancillary Products). This is a uniform/dedicated TPT increase, not a marijuana-only tax, and it illustrates §36-2864(C)’s uniform-increase exception. A secondary Prescott example calculates medical TPT in Prescott at ~9.3% (5.6% + 2.95% city + 0.75% Yavapai County) and adult-use at ~25.3% (plus 16% excise) — label as secondary calculation, Verify against the ADOR table. Note the ADOR Prescott page also records the termination, effective 1 January 2023 (Ord. 2022-1801, 11 October 2022), of the city’s earlier 0.75% PSPRS-liability TPT (rate falling from 2.75% to 2.00%) — do not confuse the terminated 0.75% PSPRS tax with the new 0.95%/0.75% public-safety tax.

Secondary combined-rate examples — all flagged Verify against the ADOR table, with conflicts preserved: Phoenix: 9.1% (5.6% state + 0.7% county + 2.8% city) in an Avalara-based 2026 guide vs 8.6% (5.6% + 0.7% + 2.3% city) in older guides — AUDIT RESOLVED: 9.1% is the current ordinary rate (effective 1 July 2025, Ord. G-7369); 8.6% was the pre-1 July 2025 rate. Note the ADOR TPT Update (January 2026 archive) records a City of Phoenix change effective 1 January 2026 concerning new threshold amounts for the retail sales and use tax two-level tax rate structure (Proposition 104, August 25, 2015 city election; inflation adjustment affecting sales transacted 1 January 2026–31 December 2027). That two-level structure is a likely reason different guides show different Phoenix city components — Verify the Phoenix retail rate and threshold against the ADOR city profile / rate table before publishing any Phoenix combined rate. Tucson ~8.7% (consistent with the ADOR 2.60% city rate above). Mesa 8.3% (5.6% + 0.7% + 2.0% Mesa city rate, ADOR June 2026 rate table) — AUDIT RESOLVED; 8.05% uses the superseded 1.75% Mesa city rate. Tempe ~8.1% (5.6% + 1.8% + 0.7%). Scottsdale ~8.05% (5.6% + 1.75% + 0.7%). Chandler ~7.8% in one guide (components not stated). Surprise ~7.8% in one guide. Queen Creek example (Maricopa County): 8.55% (5.6% + 0.7% + 2.25% city). County TPT/excise components from a secondary guide (Zamp): Maricopa County 0.7%, Pima County 0.5%, Yuma County 1.112%, Pinal County 1.1%, Coconino County 1.3%. Flag all as Verify against ADOR. Note a conflicting secondary figure: Mohave County 0% in one 2026 guide vs other guides’ county figures — do not publish a Mohave combined rate without the ADOR table. Hemp TPT: no hemp-specific local rate was found; hemp retail, where taxable, uses the general retail TPT rate for the jurisdiction. Mark as “no hemp-specific local rate located — Verify against ADOR retail classification guidance for hemp/CBD products.”

3.8 Business concentration — only official-source statements

Do not rank or estimate clusters. The following are the only official-framework statements located: ADHS medical dispensary allocation uses 126 Community Health Analysis Areas (CHAAs), pre-existing public-health geographic areas (A.A.C. R9-17-101(7) as cited in White Mountain). The original medical model allowed one dispensary per CHAA (126 CHAAs ≈ 1 per 10 pharmacies, the §36-2804(C) ratio), with a statutory requirement to ensure at least one certificate in each county where an application is approved (Saguaro Healing LLC v. State, Arizona Supreme Court, 20 August 2020, summarised by Osborn Maledon: ADHS must prioritise an approved application from a county without a dispensary even if the county had a dispensary at the start of the application period; prioritisation otherwise by CHAA patient-card counts under A.A.C. R9-17-303). White Mountain (Court of Appeals, 20 December 2016) confirms CHAAs do not necessarily coincide with municipal/county boundaries and that nothing explicitly requires a dispensary in every CHAA. Cochise County example (2011 official agenda): six CHAAs in Cochise County, so up to six dispensaries could be issued there under the CHAA model. Yavapai County 2012 application example (secondary press): 54 applicants for eight Yavapai County licences, by CHAA — Yavapai Northeast (Verde Valley) 13, Prescott 8, Chino Valley/Ash Fork 8, Yavapai South/Bagdad 7, Sedona 7, Prescott Valley 6, Cordes Junction 5, Yavapai-Prescott Indian Tribe CHAA 0. These are historical application counts, not current operating counts. What was NOT found: no current ADHS publication giving operating establishments/dispensaries by county or by CHAA as a concentration table was opened in this research. Secondary counts (e.g., Herring Bank citing ADHS July 2026 monthly report: 170 licensed adult-use establishments (169 operating), 136 medical dispensaries, 8 labs, 70,444 patients, 16,061 facility agents as of 1 August 2026) are statewide totals, not county/CHAA concentration, and belong to Parts 5–6, not Part 3(5). State in the assembled dossier: “No official county/CHAA concentration table was located in this research; the CHAA allocation framework above is the only official concentration-related statement extracted. Do not infer that Maricopa/Pima counties have the most licensees from population alone — that inference is not in the sources opened.” Treasurer D2 distribution list is not a licence-concentration table: the June 2026 Treasurer report lists police/fire/sheriff recipients and amounts (e.g., Phoenix Police $3,638,810.14) — that distribution is by PSPRS membership, not by licence location, and must not be used as a proxy for where businesses are concentrated.

PART 4: LICENCE TYPES

4.0 Architecture — read first

Arizona does not have separate state licences for cultivation-only, manufacturing-only, wholesale, transport, delivery-only, microbusiness or consumption-lounge operation as of October 2026.

  • The adult-use system has one vertically integrated licence: the marijuana establishment licence.
  • The medical system has one registration: the nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary registration certificate.
  • A dual licence is not a third licence type — it is holding both of the above at a shared location.
  • The social equity licence is not a different activity licence — it is a marijuana establishment licence awarded through the Social Equity Ownership Program.
  • Delivery is an activity under an establishment licence, not a separate delivery licence.
  • The only other business licences are marijuana testing facility licences (adult-use, AAC Title 9, Ch. 18) and, on the medical side, independent third-party laboratory registration certificates (AAC Title 9, Ch. 17, Art. 4).
  • Worker permissions are agent cards, not business licences — see §4.7.
  • Secondary summary agreeing on this architecture: CannBus Q3 2026 — CannBus [Secondary].

Current stock — official ADHS July 2026 Monthly Report, as of 1 August 2026 (directly opened: ADHS July 2026 (PDF)): 170 adult-use marijuana establishments, 169 operating; 136 medical marijuana dispensaries, 136 operating; 8 labs (“Current Labs” — report does not in the opened extract split medical vs adult-use labs); 16,061 facility agents; 107 laboratory agents; 2 dispensary agents (legacy medical agent cards; near-zero shows medical agent card is now residual); 70,444 qualifying patients — context only.

Validity / enforcement common to establishment and testing facility — ARS §36-2854(A)(1)(g) (§36-2854, directly opened): “Licenses issued by the department to marijuana establishments and marijuana testing facilities shall be valid for a period of two years. A dual licensee’s initial renewal date, which will be the ongoing renewal date for both the dual licensee’s marijuana establishment license and nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary registration, is the earlier of: (i) The date of the marijuana establishment license renewal. (ii) The date of the nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary registration renewal.” ARS §36-2854(B)(1)–(3): ADHS may deny/suspend/revoke for violation of Ch. 28.2 or its rules, or substantial violation endangering public health/safety; civil penalty up to $2,000 per violation, each day a separate violation; at least one unannounced visit annually to each licensed facility. Medical registration certificates likewise expire two years after issue — ARS §36-2804.06 (via Justia mirror — [Verify against azleg.gov if azleg-only citation is required]).

4.1 Nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary registration certificate

Statute: ARS Title 36, Ch. 28.1 (AMMA, Prop. 203, 2010). Rules: AAC Title 9, Ch. 17. Regulator: ADHS.

What it allows: a not-for-profit entity that acquires, possesses, cultivates, manufactures, delivers, transfers, transports, supplies, sells or dispenses marijuana or related supplies and educational materials to cardholders only (registered qualifying patients and designated caregivers). It is medical-only and must remain nonprofit unless it becomes a dual licensee and elects for-profit status under §36-2858 (see §4.2.3). It does not authorise sale to adult-use consumers aged 21+.

Cap — verbatim, ARS §36-2804(C) (§36-2804, directly opened): “The department may not issue more than one nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary registration certificate for every ten pharmacies that have registered under section 32-1929, have obtained a pharmacy permit from the Arizona state board of pharmacy and operate within the state, except that the department may issue nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary registration certificates in excess of this limit if necessary to ensure that the department issues at least one nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary registration certificate in each county in which an application has been approved.”

Current numeric ceiling: [Unavailable/Verify]. No opened official source states the current registered-pharmacy count, so 136 operating dispensaries cannot be converted into a “headroom” number without inventing the denominator. Secondary estimates of a ~143 standard-licence ceiling conflate the medical and adult-use 1:10 ratios and are not adopted. A Jan 2026 Fennemore summary of ADHS’s Dec 2025 annual audit states 136 DRCs vs 1,323 pharmacies, concluding no new DRCs under the 1-to-10 rule but that DRCs could be awarded for Apache and La Paz Counties as “empty counties” — Fennemore summary [Secondary — Verify the Dec 2025 ADHS allocation notice itself]. Note 1,323 ÷ 10 = 132.3, below the existing 136 DRCs, so the cap does not arithmetically explain current stock (grandfathered/pre-cap licences, dual licences and the empty-county exception all complicate it).

County floor: at least one per county where an application has been approved, even above the 1:10 ratio. Premium Leaf v. ADHS (2019) is cited in secondary material as holding §36-2804(C) requires at least one per county — case citation and holding [Verify — not opened in full].

What must be filed — verbatim summary, ARS §36-2804(B) (same azleg link): ADHS shall, not later than 90 days after receiving an application, register the dispensary and issue a registration certificate and a random 20-digit alphanumeric identification number if: (a) the application fee is submitted; (b) the application includes (i) legal name, (ii) “The physical address of the nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary and the physical address of one additional location, if any, where marijuana will be cultivated, neither of which may be within five hundred feet of a public or private school, a child care facility or a facility that provides preschool programs existing before the date of the nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary application,” (iii) name, address and date of birth of each principal officer and board member, (iv) name, address and date of birth of each dispensary agent; (c) operating procedures consistent with department rules for oversight, including accurate recordkeeping and adequate security; (d) if the city/town/county has enacted zoning restrictions, a sworn statement certifying compliance; no principal officer/board member has been convicted of an excluded felony offense; none has served as such for a dispensary whose registration certificate was revoked; none is under 21; ADHS may conduct a criminal records check.

Historic application extras [Verify current status — do not present as current without re-checking AAC R9-17-304 in full]: 2011–2012 rule summaries required a medical director, a business plan demonstrating ongoing viability as a nonprofit, inventory/recordkeeping/security/education policies, local-jurisdiction zoning documentation and occupancy documentation, and a financial-institution letter showing $150,000 under the control of the entity or a principal officer for at least 30 days before application (R9-17-302(A)(5)/R9-17-304(D)(1)(f)(ii) as summarised 2011) — [Secondary/historic — the $150,000 figure is NOT a current fee and must not be quoted as one].

Fees — AAC R9-17-102 (Cornell mirror, Cornell R9-17-102, directly opened; nonrefundable; re-confirm against Secretary of State official AAC before publishing): Registration of a dispensary: $4,000 (older 2011–2017 versions showed $5,000; current Cornell text shows $4,000); Renew registration: $1,000; Change location of dispensary: $2,500; Change location of dispensary’s cultivation site or add a cultivation site: $2,500; Change activities at current location / add activities at a new location: $2,500; Registry identification card — dispensary agent: $500 initial and $500 renewal; laboratory agent: $500 initial and $500 renewal; amend/change card: $10 (patient $150 / $75 SNAP; caregiver $200 — person permissions, not business licences); Registration of a laboratory (medical): $5,000; renewal: $1,000 — see §4.5.

Fee-cap rule for adult-use fees: ARS §36-2854(A)(2) caps adult-use establishment/testing fees at amounts “reasonable and related to the actual cost” and not exceeding five times the fees to register/renew a nonprofit medical dispensary — §36-2854.

How awarded — historic, not an open round: original allocation was by ADHS application windows and, where oversubscribed for a Community Health Analysis Area (CHAA), random selection (first allocation 7 August 2012, after 14–25 May 2012 applications — ADHS 2012 annual report, ADHS 2012 annual report (PDF)). No new medical-only registration round was found open as of October 2026 — see §4.8. New medical entry in practice is by acquiring/transferring an existing certificate or by dual-licensing an existing certificate, subject to ADHS change/transfer rules in AAC R9-17-303/304 [Verify current transfer text — not opened in full].

Off-site cultivation under the medical certificate: statute contemplates the dispensary address plus one additional cultivation location (§36-2804(B)(1)(b)(ii)). That is narrower on its face than the adult-use establishment’s three-site model (§4.3). Whether a medical-only holder may today hold more than one off-site cultivation site via successive $2,500 “add a cultivation site” changes under R9-17-102(4) is [Verify — the fee line proves a change/add mechanism exists; it does not by itself prove unlimited sites]. Edible/food manufacturing additionally requires compliance with ADHS food-establishment requirements — detail [Verify — county summaries assert an ADHS food licence is needed for infusion; not confirmed in opened state text].

4.2 Marijuana establishment licence — adult-use-only and dual

Statute: ARS Title 36, Ch. 28.2 (Smart and Safe Arizona Act, Prop. 207, 2020). Rules: AAC Title 9, Ch. 18. Regulator: ADHS. Term: 2 years (§36-2854(A)(1)(g)).

What it allows — verbatim definition, ARS §36-2850(21) (numbering varies by year — 18 in 2020 text; via Justia and corroborated in 2026 bill summaries): “‘Marijuana establishment’ means an entity licensed by the department to operate all of the following: (a) A single retail location at which the licensee may sell marijuana and marijuana products to consumers, cultivate marijuana and manufacture marijuana products. (b) A single off-site cultivation location at which the licensee may cultivate marijuana, process marijuana and manufacture marijuana products, but from which marijuana and marijuana products may not be transferred or sold to consumers. (c) A single off-site location at which the licensee may manufacture marijuana products and package and store marijuana and marijuana products, but from which marijuana and marijuana products may not be transferred or sold to consumers.”

Consequences: one licence = up to three physical footprints (retail + off-site cultivation + off-site manufacturing/packaging/storage), but only the retail site may sell/transfer to consumers. Adult-use sale limit per transaction: 1 oz marijuana, max 5g concentrate — ARS §36-2858(A)(1)(c). Edibles: max 10 mg THC per serving / 100 mg per package, delineated/scored and homogenised if multi-serving — ARS §36-2854(A)(7). The establishment licence alone authorises adult-use sales only; medical dispensing requires the medical certificate held alongside it (dual status). For-profit operation is permitted for an establishment licence; the nonprofit restriction applies only to the medical certificate, subject to the dual-licensee election in §4.2.3.

Cap — verbatim, ARS §36-2854(A)(1)(b) (§36-2854): “The department may not issue more than one marijuana establishment license for every ten pharmacies that have registered under section 32-1929, that have obtained a pharmacy permit from the Arizona board of pharmacy and that operate within this state.”

Rural-county exception — verbatim, §36-2854(A)(1)(c): “Notwithstanding subdivision (b) of this paragraph, the department may issue a marijuana establishment license to not more than two marijuana establishments per county that contains no registered nonprofit medical marijuana dispensaries, or one marijuana establishment license per county that contains one registered nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary. Any license issued pursuant to this subdivision shall be for a fixed county and may not be relocated outside of that county.”

Current numeric ceiling: [Unavailable/Verify] for the same reason as §4.1 — no opened official pharmacy count. Secondary sources variously estimate “~143 standard licences” and “12 rural county licences.” Neither the ~143 nor the 12 is adopted as official, because (a) the pharmacy denominator was not opened, and (b) 170 establishments are already licensed, which necessarily includes rural-exception and social-equity licences on top of the 1:10 base — so “170” is not the cap. Contemporaneous MJBizDaily (20 Apr 2021) reports 13 rural licences awarded Apr 2021 across 8 rural counties (Greenlee, La Paz, Apache, Santa Cruz, Cochise, Gila, Graham, Yuma), from ~400 applicants at $25,000 each — MJBizDaily, 20 Apr 2021 — use 13, flag 12 as an error/different counting basis [Verify].

Other statutory bars — verbatim: §36-2854(A)(1)(a): an application may not require disclosure of a person entitled to <10% of profits of a publicly-traded corporation applicant. §36-2854(A)(1)(h): “The department may not issue a marijuana establishment or marijuana testing facility license to an applicant who has an ownership interest in an out-of-state marijuana establishment or marijuana testing facility, or the other state’s equivalent, that has had its license revoked by the other state.” No ADHS revocation/denial decision citing it was found in opened official material — see §4.4.5 in the underlying research (enforcement gap).

Fees — AAC R9-18-102 (Cornell R9-18-102, directly opened; all nonrefundable; amendment history: adopted by exempt rulemaking eff. 15 Jan 2021; amended 1 June 2021, 13 Oct 2021, 1 Oct 2023, and 1 Nov 2024 (30 A.A.R. 3451) — the 1 Nov 2024 amendment aligns with the adult-use delivery rules effective date in §4.6): Marijuana establishment licence — initial application fee: $25,000; renewal licence fee: $5,000; Approval to operate a marijuana establishment: $2,500; Change location of retail, cultivation or manufacturing site: $2,500; Add a cultivation site or manufacturing site: $2,500; Change/add approved activities for a retail, cultivation or manufacturing site: $2,500; Marijuana testing facility licence — initial: $25,000; renewal: $5,000 (see §4.5).

Requirements (adult-use establishment): from ARS §36-2854(A)(1) + AAC Title 9, Ch. 18, Art. 3 (R9-18-301 et seq. — article text not opened in full, so this is a structured summary [Verify against R9-18-303/304]): ADHS investigation and background checks; principal officers/board members (PO/BMs) listed; persons entitled to ≥10% of profits disclosed (subject to publicly-traded <10% exception); excluded-felony bar and age-21 bar applied via facility-agent/PO/BM definitions (see §4.7); local zoning compliance, secure premises, inventory tracking system procured/maintained by the licensee (ARS §36-2854(A)(4) requires tracking of propagation, production, processing, inter-licensee sales, inter-premises transfers, waste disposal, and who/when/where each entry was made, with a transactional stamp/chain of custody). Arizona does not impose a single state Metrc/BioTrack system in the opened text — licensees maintain their own compliant system [Secondary — IndicaOnline/Dutchie summaries; Verify against R9-18-312/313 if a tracking-system paragraph is needed; Metrc is referenced in third-party summaries as mandatory but no Metrc public holder list exists and Metrc is licensee-only, not a public register]. Inspection and approval to operate ($2,500) before opening; at least annual unannounced ADHS visit. No state residency requirement for general establishment ownership was found in opened statute — secondary summaries state this expressly (CannBus) [Secondary — absence of a residency clause in §36-2854 as opened is consistent, but a full-text residency search was not completed; mark as Verify if published as a positive claim]. Social-equity ownership-control condition at application (51% — §4.4) does not apply to ordinary establishment licences after issuance: ordinary licences are transferable subject to ADHS change rules (R9-18-305/306), which is precisely why the social-equity transfer controversy in §4.4 is distinct.

4.2.3 Dual licence — what it is, what changes

Definition: an entity holding both a marijuana establishment licence and a nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary registration certificate. ARS §36-2858(D)–(F): Must conduct both operations at a shared location: (1) sell to adult-use consumers under Ch. 28.2, and (2) dispense to registered qualifying patients/designated caregivers under Ch. 28.1. May operate on a for-profit basis if it promptly notifies ADHS and the Department of Revenue and takes necessary actions, including converting corporate form and amending organisational/operating documents. Must continue to hold both instruments regardless of ownership change, unless it terminates dual status and forfeits one instrument by notifying ADHS. Is not required to employ/contract with a medical director, nor to obtain dispensary/facility agent registrations for outside vendors without regular unsupervised access to the interior, nor to have a single secure entrance as required by §36-2806(C) (appropriate security measures may still be required). Dual licensees may not separately transfer or assign the dispensary certificate or the establishment licence — AAC R9-18-305(A) (Cornell mirror — Verify against official AAC). Dual renewal date = earlier of the two renewal dates (§36-2854(A)(1)(g)). Delivery rules for dual licensees: §36-2854(D)(9) requires dual licensees to comply with the adult-use delivery rules — see §4.6. Dual-licensee overlap count: [Unavailable as an official count — do not infer dual = 136.] ADHS reports the two counts separately and does not publish the overlap. Most retail sites are dual-licensed in practice, but that is not an official count.

How dual status was first created — early applicants: §36-2854(A)(1)(d): ADHS “shall accept applications for marijuana establishment licenses from early applicants beginning January 19, 2021 through March 9, 2021. Not later than sixty days after receiving an application … shall issue a marijuana establishment license to each qualified early applicant.” Outcome, secondary but contemporaneous: ADHS received 79 applications in the first days; 73 facilities approved to begin adult-use sales 22 January 2021, 6 still under review — MJBizDaily. The early-applicant route was therefore overwhelmingly existing medical dispensaries converting to dual status. That window is closed; it is not a current application route. After early applicants: §36-2854(A)(1)(e): licences available under (b) and (c) “shall [be issued] by random selection and according to rules adopted pursuant to this section. At least sixty days before any random selection, the department shall prominently publicize the random selection on its website and through other means of general distribution … and shall provide notice through an email notification system to which interested parties can subscribe.” This 60-day publicity duty is the test for whether a future round is genuinely open — see §4.8.

4.3 Off-site cultivation and manufacturing — not separate licences

Adult-use: covered by the establishment definition in §4.2: one off-site cultivation location + one off-site manufacturing/packaging/storage location, in addition to cultivation/manufacturing at the retail site itself.

  • Transport between sites: a marijuana facility agent may transport between (1) the establishment’s retail, cultivation or manufacturing site, (2) that site and another establishment, (3) that site and a Ch. 17 dispensary, (4) retail site and a consumer only under §36-2854(D) and R9-18-312.01, and (5) that site and a testing facility / Ch. 17 laboratory — R9-18-312(C).
  • Security: indoor cultivation in a closed, locked room; outdoor cultivation surrounded by solid 10-foot metal/concrete/stone walls preventing viewing, with a 1-inch-thick metal gate; access to cultivation/manufacturing areas limited to PO/BMs and authorised facility agents unless supervised — R9-18-312(A)–(B).
  • Physical plant: retail/cultivation/manufacturing buildings must have toilet/handwash facilities, designated marijuana storage separate from toxic/flammable storage, and a sanitizable designated preparation/packaging area if preparation/packaging occurs there; commercial devices must be licensed/certified under ARS §3-3451 — R9-18-316.
  • Location changes: an establishment may change a retail/manufacturing/cultivation location to another location in-state, except a §36-2854(A)(1)(c) rural-county licensee may only move its retail site within the same county of original issuance; no cultivation/manufacture/distribution/dispensing/sale at a new location or changed activity until ADHS issues an amended licence or approval — R9-18-305. Fee per change/add: $2,500 (§4.2).
  • Joint use: “Two or more marijuana establishments or dual licensees may designate a single off-site location as prescribed in section 36-2850, paragraph 21, subdivision (c) to be jointly used” — i.e. the off-site manufacturing (c) site, not the (b) cultivation site, may be shared — via SB 1121 bill text quoting the statute [Verify against current §36-2850].
  • Cultivation/manufacturing site counts: [Unavailable for 2024–2026.] Only site figure found is a third-party summary citing an ADHS/JLBC figure of 111 cultivation sites for FY2023 as “latest published” [Verify — CannBus citing JLBC fiscal note for SB 1641]. The 2024 Citation Report gives licences cited by site type, not total sites (Part 5, §2B).

No standalone cultivator/manufacturer licence — and a dead 2026 proposal: SB 1641 (2026), “marijuana producers; licensure”: would have required ADHS to accept marijuana producer licence applications from 1 January 2029, initially only from entities that by 1 January 2026 contracted with an establishment/dispensary/management company to cultivate/manufacture and retain profits, with additional licences possible from 2032, 2-year validity, single non-retail location — House Commerce summary; JLBC fiscal note. Outcome: Senate passed 23–5 (11 Mar 2026), assigned to House Rules 16 Mar 2026, never heard in House Rules, died at sine die 13 June 2026 — sine-die wrap [Secondary legislative reporting — consistent with LegiScan last action 16 Mar 2026]. SB 1641 is NOT law. Do not list “marijuana producer licence” as a current licence type. SB 1713 (2025), “rural opportunity initiative”: would have added up to 18 rural-opportunity establishment licences + matching medical certificates for fixed unserved rural communities, with 1 retail + 1 off-site cultivation/manufacturing location, 24 hrs/week minimum retail operation, retail open within 18 months. Enactment [Verify — no enactment was found in opened sources; do not list as a current licence type].

4.4 Social equity ownership programme licence

Statute: ARS §36-2854(A)(9) (programme creation) + §36-2854(A)(1)(f) (26 licences). Rules: AAC R9-18-303 (social-equity application). This is a marijuana establishment licence with a restricted award route, not a different activity.

Statutory mandate — verbatim, directly opened: §36-2854(A)(9): rules for “The creation and implementation of a social equity ownership program to promote the ownership and operation of marijuana establishments and marijuana testing facilities by individuals from communities disproportionately impacted by the enforcement of previous marijuana laws.” — §36-2854. §36-2854(A)(1)(f): “Notwithstanding subdivisions (b) and (c) of this paragraph, and not later than six months after the department adopts final rules to implement a social equity ownership program pursuant to paragraph 9 of this subsection, the department shall issue twenty-six additional marijuana establishment licenses to entities that are qualified pursuant to the social equity ownership program.” Scope note: the statute says the programme promotes ownership of establishments and testing facilities, but the 26-licence mandate in (f) is for establishment licences. Whether any social-equity testing-facility licences were ever issued under the programme is [Unavailable — none found in opened sources; the 26 are consistently reported as establishment/dispensary licences].

Eligibility — 51% + 3 of 4. ADHS Social Equity Application Checklist (directly opened official ADHS PDF, revised 17 Nov 2021), ADHS Social Equity Checklist (PDF): “The Department will accept applications for 26 adult use establishment licenses from social equity applicants from December 1, 2021 through December 14, 2021. Applications must be submitted through the Department’s Facility Licensing Portal by the designated principal officer or board member of the applying entity. For a complete list of the application and eligibility requirements, please refer to A.A.C. R9-18-303.” “At least 51% of the applying entity must be owned by principal officer(s) or board member(s) (PO/BM) who each meet at least 3 out of the 4 eligibility criteria listed below.” The four criteria, as stated in the ADHS checklist and in the Maricopa County Superior Court order dismissing the challenge (CV2021-017808, 31 Jan 2022): (1) Annual household income <400% of the poverty level in at least 3 of the years 2016–2020; (2) Personally adversely affected by enforcement of previous marijuana laws — granted expungement under ARS §36-2862 or convicted of a marijuana/marijuana-paraphernalia violation; (3) Adversely affected because related to someone convicted of such a violation or eligible for expungement under §36-2862; (4) Lived for at least 3 of the years 2016–2020 in an ADHS-identified area disproportionately affected by enforcement — implemented as 87 qualifying ZIP codes (Cannabis Business Times; IndicaOnline) [Secondary for the “87” number — the ADHS checklist itself refers to ADHS-identified areas without enumerating 87 in the opened extract]. Additional application requirements in the checklist: Arizona address for the applying entity, good standing with the Arizona Corporation Commission, PO/BM listing per R9-18-301, completion of the ADHS Social Equity Training Course via the Facility Licensing Portal, signed PO/BM attestations, disclosure of any non-PO/BM person entitled to ≥10% of profits, and $4,000 payment by credit card or electronic check.

Fee discrepancy — flagged, not smoothed over: the ADHS 2021 checklist says $4,000. Some 2026 secondary guides say social-equity applicants received a “reduced $5,000 application fee vs $25,000 standard” (IndicaOnline). The $4,000 figure is the contemporaneous official checklist figure for the 2021 round and is adopted here for that round; the $5,000 figure is [Verify — possibly a conflation with the medical laboratory $5,000 fee or a later proposal; do not publish both as if both applied to the same round]. Social-equity renewal fee treatment under R9-18-102 ($5,000 establishment renewal) is [Verify].

Application limit [Secondary — Verify against R9-18-303 in full]: reporting on the Vasquez litigation states PO/BMs “may not submit more than two license applications” (Marijuana Moment). Entities were permitted up to two applications in secondary summaries (LegalClarity). The two-application limit is not confirmed in the opened checklist extract.

What the licence allows once issued: the same as any marijuana establishment licence (§4.2) — retail + off-site cultivation + off-site manufacturing. Secondary reporting states social-equity licences were adult-use-only at issuance with no dual-licence opportunity at that time (GreenPharms) [Secondary — Verify: the statute does not on its face bar a later medical certificate, but no opened source shows a social-equity licensee subsequently dual-licensing; do not assert a permanent bar without the rule text]. Secondary reporting also states winners had 18 months to open (Cannabis Business Times) — i.e. to around October 2023 for April 2022 winners.

Lottery — date, numbers, method, results: Application window: 1–14 December 2021 (ADHS checklist above). Applications filed: “more than 1,500 applications” filed ahead of the deadline (Cannabis Business Times, 1 Feb 2022). Applications in the drawing: 1,301 — MJBizDaily, MJBizDaily; Arizona Center for Investigative Reporting (AZCIR), AZCIR, 27 July 2023. The gap between “>1,500 filed” and “1,301 in drawing” is administrative/substantive completeness screening — exact disqualification reasons [Verify — not stated in opened sources]. Drawing: Friday 8 April 2022, livestreamed, using Smartplay International lottery software, operated/audited by Henry & Horne LLP — Soft Secrets summary [Secondary]; MJBizDaily confirms the 8 April 2022 award date. Licences awarded: 26. The 26 are included in the 170 total, not additional to it. Winners named in the MJBizDaily report (20 of 26 named in the opened snippet — the remaining 6 are NOT invented here): A.M.Y. Investments II; Arizona Tree Equity 2; AZ Flower Power; Blue Palo Verde 1; Curious Cultivators I; Desert Boyz; Dynamic Trio Holdings; Green Lightning; Higher than High I; Joint Junkies I; Juicy Joint I; Life Changers Investments; MCCSE206; MCD-SE Venture 25; MCD-SE Venture 26; Pleasant Plants I; Swallowtail 3; Woodstock 1; Your Bright Horizon 117; Your Bright Horizon 197. Full official ADHS winner list [Verify — ADHS was reported to have published the winner and applicant lists online, but the list itself was not opened in this research; pull it from ADHS before publishing a complete table]. Corporate-partner footprint at award: MJBizDaily/Arizona Republic reporting at award: at least 10 of 26 went to applicants partnered with existing marijuana companies; about a third of applicants partnered with existing companies. AZCIR (July 2023) quantified applications, not just winners: Copperstate Farms submitted 106 applications with social-equity counterparts; Mohave Cannabis Co. 322; The Mint Cannabis 59 — collectively 37% of all 1,301 applications.

Litigation over the award rules — outcome: Acre 41 Enterprises LLC + Greater Phoenix Urban League v. ADHS (Maricopa County Superior Court, CV2021-017808): filed November 2021. Allegation: ADHS rules were deficient — no prohibition on licence transfers, no requirement that expenditures/profits remain in disproportionately impacted communities, and the rules would let existing companies buy out token holders. Dismissed 1 February 2022 by Judge Randall Warner. Warner held the rules “satisfy the broad mandates” of Prop. 207 and are “reasonably designed to meet [their] objectives”; “The voters left to ADHS how the social equity ownership program should work, and the rules [ADHS] developed comply with the law.” Sources: Cannabis Business Times dismissal report; MJBizDaily; dismissal order PDF, dismissal order (PDF). Effect: cleared the way for the 8 April 2022 lottery. Whether plaintiffs appealed is [Verify — contemporaneous reporting said plaintiffs vowed to fight / a second suit might follow depending on the lottery; no appellate outcome was found in opened sources].

ADHS action on social-equity fronting / ownership-control violations — the critical gap: No published ADHS revocation, suspension, denial or ownership-control enforcement order against a social-equity licensee for fronting/predatory control was found in opened official ADHS material as of 31 Oct 2026. That is a research finding (“not found”), not proof that no such action exists in an unpublished file. Do not write “ADHS took no action” as a positive official fact without an ADHS statement. ADHS position as reported: ADHS told AZ Big Media it administered the lottery “with integrity and in full accordance with the law” and that “the department does not have legal standing to intervene in the sale of a dispensary” — AZ Big Media [Secondary reporting of an ADHS spokesperson email — not an ADHS order]. Legislative oversight pressure: Rep. Alma Hernandez wrote to ADHS Deputy Director Jennie Cunico in July 2023 alleging the two-application limit had been gamed (individuals connected with Helping Handz / Investing in the Future backed 203 other entrants and won 5 of 26) — MJBizDaily reform-failure report [Secondary]. Outcome reporting (AZCIR / legislative debate, not ADHS findings): AZCIR July 2023 found most winners had sold to or partnered with corporate dispensaries/investors; by the February 2024 SB 1262 debate, Sen. Sonny Borrelli stated “24 of 26 of these social equity licenses are now fully controlled by companies or people who do not belong in these special groups” (Marijuana Moment/AZCIR); MJBizDaily’s 2024 reform-failure piece reports “only three of the original 26 license winners still have a stake” per court allegations and legislative repetition — MJBizDaily reform-failure piece. These are allegations/reporting tallies, not ADHS adjudications, and the “24 of 26” vs “3 retain a stake” figures differ because they measure different things (control vs any stake). Do not merge them into a single ADHS-verified statistic. Positive example reported: Alicia Deals / Cookies, opened June 2023, reported as one of the few holders to open and retain 100% equity, and one of the only Black women owning/operating a dispensary in Arizona — AZ Big Media, above [Secondary].

Operating count — OFFICIAL: UNAVAILABLE. ADHS monthly reports publish total establishments (170/169) but do not break out how many of those hold a social-equity-origin licence, nor how many social-equity licences are operating vs licensed-but-not-operating. No ADHS social-equity operating list was found. Any “X social-equity dispensaries operating” figure in this dossier must be labelled secondary/dated, not official. Secondary timeline (dated, conflicting — present as a timeline, not a single number): July 2023 (AZCIR, 27 Jul 2023): of 26 licences — 11 owned outright by corporate dispensaries, 7 held by investors/other Arizona dispensaries, 8 original lottery winners retained any equity share. ~October 2023, ahead of the 8 Oct 2023 opening deadline (AZCIR, via Marijuana Moment): 13 dispensaries had opened using a social-equity licence; only 1 was owned by an original licensee without corporate-dispensary support; the other 12 operated under familiar names including Sol Flower, JARS Cannabis, Story Cannabis, Mint Cannabis. Separately, the same AZCIR update states only 4 of the original 26 still had an equity stake, corporate dispensaries owned half the licences outright (13), and private investors held equity in 10 more — 4+13+10 = 27, which exceeds 26, so at least one of those three numbers is rounded, overlapping, or misreported. [VERIFY — do not sum.] February 2024 Senate hearing (Sen. Sonny Borrelli, SB 1262, via AZCIR/Marijuana Moment secondary): “24 of 26 … now fully controlled by companies or people who do not belong in these special groups”; only 1 original applicant fully owned their licence and had opened independently, 2 more partnered with prominent brands while retaining equity, the other 23 owned by investors/corporations (Sol Flower, Mint Cannabis, Mohave Cannabis, JARS Cannabis, Story Cannabis named). SB 1262 sought to claw licences back to original applicants; its outcome was confirmed as failed — see below. Reconciliation: direction is consistent across all secondary reports (rapid transfer from original winners to MSOs/investors between Apr 2022 and early 2024), but the exact operating count in 2025–2026 is unknown — the 13-opened figure is from late 2023 and the 170-total in 2026 does not reveal how many of the 26 are inside it. Do not extrapolate 13→26 or assume all 26 are operating. The October 2023 deadline also carried an ADHS enforcement threat (spokesman Tom Herrmann, via LeafyLeaks secondary: ADHS “has the right to proceed with enforcement actions” against non-open licensees, who could appeal) — no published enforcement outcome was found.

Court (not ADHS) action — Vasquez / Juicy Joint: Anavel Vasquez won as Juicy Joint I. She alleged investor Michael Halow (Helping Handz / Investing in the Future) used a term sheet/arbitration to seize control; she had secretly transferred the licence to Menvas for $2.7 million without partner consent, according to the court. July 2023 arbitration: transferred the licence back to Helping Handz; Vasquez ordered to pay Halow’s attorneys’ fees and, per AZCIR, not compensated for her majority share at that point. Maricopa County Superior Court, Judge Christopher Coury, 4 October 2024: declined to void or reassign the permit; held Vasquez breached her contractual agreement by the secret Menvas transfer — MJBizDaily, MJBizDaily, 4 Oct 2024. A separate claim that ADHS should never have granted the licence was reported as still pending at that date [Verify subsequent outcome — not found].

Denzel Mason / Copperstate Farms (“Your Bright Horizon”) dispute: AZCIR reports Mason, a 2022 winner, spent 20 April 2024 in court defending against a Copperstate Farms lawsuit over his licence and lost that fight, considering appeal — Arizona Agenda summary of AZCIR [Secondary — court name/date of final judgment not opened; Verify before naming it as a decided case].

Failed legislative fix — SB 1262 (2024): would have added ARS §36-2866: the original ≥51% PO/BM of an 8 April 2022 social-equity licensee could transfer the licence to an entity of their choice (subject to Attorney General approval) if (a) subject to a predatory agreement — defined as an agreement signed before or within 12 months after issuance requiring sale/transfer/control for less than minimum fair market value — or (b) a minority-interest PO/BM violated Ch. 28.2 rules, (c) failed to disclose an excluded felony, or (d) lacked a facility-agent card at application; AG to investigate complaints and, with ADHS, take enforcement action, including at licence/agent renewal. Sources: engrossed text header stating “(Section 36-2866 not enacted — failed to obtain the three-fourths vote pursuant to article IV, part 1, section 1, Constitution of Arizona.)”, SB 1262 engrossed (PDF); introduced text, SB 1262 introduced; Senate history — passed Senate Third Reading 18–12 on 6 Mar 2024, motion to reconsider 7 Mar 2024, then died in chamber (LegiScan). SB 1262 is NOT law. There is therefore no enacted Arizona “predatory agreement” transfer/enforcement regime for social-equity licences as of Oct 2026. MJBizDaily attributes the failure in part to interference from the Hobbs administration — MJBizDaily [Allegation in secondary reporting, not an official finding].

Other award litigation — Trulieve / Sherri Dunn / Cave (not social-equity, but a licence-award dispute): Sherri Dunn LLC (Trulieve subsidiary) applied for a medical certificate in La Paz County in 2016, denied, ultimately obtained it in late 2021 after Arizona Supreme Court rulings on improper 2016 denials; its early-applicant dual-licence application was rejected by ADHS, affirmed by an administrative law judge and superior court, then ADHS settled in February 2024 and approved the dual licence. Mason Cave / Arizona Wellness Center Springerville sued ADHS, Gov. Hobbs, Chief of Staff Chad Campbell and Sherri Dunn alleging gift-clause/state-law violations. Outcome as reported 2025: Judge Joseph Kreamer dismissed from the bench — Cave lacked standing, and even with standing the settlement did not violate the Gift Clause or prior court orders — MMJ Daily (secondary) [Secondary — exact dismissal date not in opened snippet; Verify].

4.5 Testing facility licence(s) — two parallel regimes, do not conflate

4.5.1 Adult-use marijuana testing facility licence (AAC Title 9, Ch. 18, Art. 4)

  • What it allows: obtain, possess, process, repackage, transfer, transport and test marijuana/marijuana products — ARS §36-2858(A) (testing-facility prong). It does not authorise retail sale, cultivation or manufacture for sale. Before a batch is offered for sale/transferred to a consumer, the establishment must ensure testing complies with R9-18-408/Table 3.1 — R9-18-311.
  • Term: 2 years (§36-2854(A)(1)(g)). Fees (R9-18-102, directly opened): initial $25,000; renewal $5,000 — Cornell R9-18-102.
  • Independence — verbatim statute: §36-2854(A)(10): rules shall include “Prohibiting a marijuana testing facility from having any direct or indirect familial relationship with or financial ownership interest in a marijuana establishment or related marijuana business entity or management company” and the reciprocal prohibition on establishments.
  • Application/approval structure (R9-18-402/403, Justia mirrors — Verify against official AAC): licence first, then separate ADHS approval for testing for each parameter/analyte, applied for at least 60 calendar days before expiry of the initial licence, with current accreditation, limit of quantitation, proficiency-testing report, SOPs, initial demonstration of capability, QA programme, and a technical laboratory director designated under R9-18-405.
  • Denial/suspension/revocation grounds (R9-18-415): owner convicted of excluded felony or under 21; non-compliance with Ch. 28.2/Title 9 Ch. 18; false/misleading information; testing before approval; diversion/acquisition from unauthorised persons; failure to implement policies/procedures or corrective-action plan.
  • Accreditation: ISO/IEC 17025 technical competence, through an approved accreditor — secondary summaries list A2LA, PJLA, ANAB, IAS, COLA (CannBus) [Secondary for the accreditor list; the ISO/IEC 17025 standard itself is stated in A2LA’s regulator page].
  • Required tests [Secondary summary — Verify against R9-18-408/Table 3.1 before publishing analyte list]: potency, microbials/mycotoxins, heavy metals, residual solvents, pesticides (CannBus). Medical testing law SB 1494 (2019) originally mandated testing for microbial contamination, pesticides/herbicides/fungicides, growth regulators, residual solvents and potency.
  • Cap: none stated in §36-2854(A)(1)(b) — the 1:10 pharmacy ratio applies by its terms to marijuana establishment licences, not testing facilities. Testing-facility numbers are therefore market/approval-driven, not ratio-capped. Current count conflict: 8 labs total per ADHS July 2026 (§4.0) vs 7 certified adult-use labs in CannBus Q3 2026, down from 21 in Dec 2022 — UNRESOLVED, do not pick one (see Part 5, §6). Possible explanations (none proven): different cut-off dates within Aug 2026; “certified” vs “total labs” definitions; a lab closing/losing certification; or a counting error. [Verify from a dated AZCareCheck Marijuana Facilities extraction.]
  • Out-of-state revocation bar: §36-2854(A)(1)(h) applies to testing applicants as well as establishments (see §4.2).

4.5.2 Medical independent third-party laboratory registration certificate (AAC Title 9, Ch. 17, Art. 4)

  • What it allows: independent testing of medical marijuana/marijuana products for dispensaries. It is a registration certificate, not an “establishment licence.”
  • Fees (R9-17-102, directly opened): registration $5,000; renewal $1,000 — Cornell R9-17-102. Do not quote $25,000/$5,000 for a medical lab — that is the adult-use testing-facility fee.
  • Independence: same no-direct/indirect financial or familial relationship rule, applied to nonprofit dispensaries/related entities/management companies and to designated caregivers for whom the lab tests — secondary summaries of SB 1494/R9-17-404 [Secondary — Verify against R9-17-404 text].
  • Whether medical and adult-use lab permissions are now a single unified permission in practice [Verify]: ADHS monthly reporting counts “labs” as one line (8) and agent reporting counts “laboratory agents” as one line (107), suggesting operational convergence, but the two rule chapters and two fee schedules remain distinct in the opened texts. Do not collapse them into one licence type without an ADHS unification notice.

4.6 Marijuana establishment delivery — activity, timeline, rules

No separate “delivery licence” exists. Delivery is a regulated activity of a licensed establishment (and, for medical, of a registered dispensary). Unauthorised delivery is separately penalised.

Statutory timeline — verbatim, ARS §36-2854(C)–(E) (§36-2854, directly opened): (C) “Until the department adopts rules allowing and regulating delivery by marijuana establishments pursuant to subsection D of this section, delivery is unlawful under this chapter.” (D) “On or after January 1, 2023, the department may, and not later than January 1, 2025 the department shall, adopt rules to allow and regulate delivery by marijuana establishments.” (D)(1)–(9) mandatory rule contents (paraphrased only where the statute is long; verbatim heads): delivery and product must originate from a designated retail location and only after an order is made by a consumer; rules may not limit distance between delivery location and originating retail location; no delivery to property owned/leased by the United States, this state, a political subdivision, or the Arizona Board of Regents; limit the retail-price amount in a delivery vehicle on a single trip; prohibit extra/unallocated product in vehicles; deliveries only by marijuana facility agents in unmarked vehicles equipped with GPS/similar tracking and video surveillance/recording, with a locked compartment; rules may not limit the number of vehicles; delivery logs; inspections; dual licensees must comply. (E) Unauthorised delivery: “an individual or entity that delivers marijuana or marijuana products in a manner that is not authorized by this chapter shall pay a civil penalty of $20,000 per violation to the smart and safe Arizona fund … This subsection may be enforced by the attorney general.”

Rollout — what actually happened: Medical delivery pre-dated adult-use delivery under separate ADHS medical guidance (GD-107-PHS-EDC, Medical Marijuana Delivery Guidelines, cited in LegalClarity). Exact date medical delivery was first authorised [Verify — not found in opened official text]. Adult-use delivery rules: AAC Title 9, Ch. 18 amendments effective 1 November 2024 — evidenced by R9-18-102 amendment history (30 A.A.R. 3451, eff. 11/1/2024) and by multiple secondary reports that adult-use delivery “launched statewide on November 1, 2024.” The 1 Nov 2024 date is therefore adopted as the adult-use delivery launch date, sourced secondarily (the ADHS rule adoption notice itself was not opened). Delivery rule: AAC R9-18-312.01 — Delivery to Consumers. Its full text was not opened verbatim in this research (Cornell page not returned as an openable result). The following operational details are therefore [Secondary summaries of R9-18-312.01 — Verify against the rule text before publishing as rule quotations]: Orders only during the establishment’s posted business hours; delivery must originate from the designated retail location after the order (no warehouse/off-site dispatch). Driver = marijuana facility agent of that establishment only; no third-party delivery services (DoorDash/Uber Eats model not permitted). At handover: verify government photo ID and age (21+ for adult-use; registry card + photo ID for medical, verified in the electronic verification system before approval and again at delivery); confirm the recipient is the ordering consumer; obtain handwritten signature; no doorstep drop, no handover to another person. Vehicle: unmarked (no marijuana identification/branding), current ADMV registration, GPS capable of showing prior 90 days’ location, operational video surveillance/recording, locked compartment — R9-18-312(D) corroborates the vehicle standard. Trip cap: $10,000 retail value per vehicle per single trip; no extra/unallocated product — LegalClarity/CannBus summaries of R9-18-312.01. The statute requires a retail-price limit but does not state $10,000, so $10,000 rests on the rule summaries, not the opened statute. No delivery if the property owner has posted/notified that deliveries are not allowed at that address — R9-18-312(C)(4)(b). Local-government restriction: cities/towns/counties may restrict/prohibit delivery within their jurisdictions under Prop. 207 local-control provisions [Secondary — LegalClarity; Verify against ARS §36-2857 / local ordinances for any specific city]. Purchase limits on delivery are the ordinary limits: adult-use 1 oz / 5g concentrate per transaction; medical 2.5 oz per 14 days (electronic verification check) — Dutchie guide [Secondary]. Number of establishments actually offering delivery as of Oct 2026: [Unavailable — CannBus Q3 2026 lists “N/A”]. “Delivery licence” application/fee: None. There is no delivery-licence fee in R9-18-102 or R9-17-102 as opened. Any source claiming a standalone Arizona delivery licence fee is conflating Arizona with another state or with a courier-partner commercial arrangement. ADHS late-2024 summaries mentioning “partnered couriers” (IndicaOnline) are [Secondary and in tension with the facility-agent-only rule — Verify: the statute requires delivery “only by marijuana facility agents,” so a “partnered courier” would still have to act as the establishment’s facility agents, not as an independent licensed courier].

4.7 Other permissions — facility agent cards and related person permissions

These are person permissions, not business licences, but are included because they are commonly confused with business licences.

4.7.1 Marijuana facility agent licence (adult-use) — AAC Title 9, Ch. 18, Art. 2

  • Who needs it: a principal officer, board member or employee of a marijuana establishment or marijuana testing facility — ARS §36-2850 definition of “marijuana facility agent” (21+, no excluded-felony conviction) [Bill text quoting definition — Verify against current §36-2850]; ARS §36-2855 registration/card statute, cited in LegalClarity.
  • Requirements (R9-18-201, Cornell mirror): applicant at least 21; Department-provided format; Arizona driver’s licence (issued on/after 1 Oct 1996) or equivalent ID; fingerprints for ADHS criminal-records check under §36-2855(B)(2) (or Level 1 Fingerprint Clearance Card copy); attestation of no excluded-felony conviction; attestation information is true/correct; applicable fee — Cornell R9-18-201.
  • Excluded felony offense (ARS §36-2801, via secondary summaries): (a) violent crime defined in §13-901.03(B) classified as a felony where convicted; (b) state/federal controlled-substance felony, except (i) sentence (including probation/incarceration/supervised release) completed 10+ years earlier, or (ii) conduct that would be immune under §36-2811 except it occurred before 14 Dec 2010 or was prosecuted by a non-Arizona authority — [Secondary summary of statute — Verify against §36-2801].
  • Fees (R9-18-102, directly opened): initial $300 with fingerprints on a fingerprint card, $150 with a current Level 1 Fingerprint Clearance Card under §41-1758.07; renewal same ($300/$150); change information $10; replacement $10; fee waiver for initial licence if applicant attests to meeting §41-1080.01 waiver criteria — Cornell R9-18-102.
  • Validity: 2 years (secondary summaries agree; statutory term is in §36-2855/R9-18-202 — [Verify against R9-18-202 text, not opened in full]).
  • One card, multiple facilities? Secondary sources split: some say one card covers multiple facilities, others say employer-sponsored per establishment. ADHS portal practice [Verify — do not publish either version as settled].
  • Count: 16,061 active facility agents at 1 Aug 2026 (ADHS July report, §4.0). July 2026 ADHS data show 701 facility-agent applications in July 2026 alone (441 new + 245 renewal + changes) and 5 laboratory-agent applications, proving the individual portal remains active.

4.7.2 Nonprofit medical marijuana dispensary agent registry identification card — AAC Title 9, Ch. 17

Fee (R9-17-102, directly opened): $500 initial, $500 renewal. Count: 2 active at 1 Aug 2026 (ADHS July report) — effectively superseded by facility-agent cards for dual-licensed operations. Dual licensees are expressly not required to obtain dispensary-agent registrations for outside vendors without regular unsupervised interior access (§36-2858, §4.2.3).

4.7.3 Laboratory agent registry identification card (medical)

Fee (R9-17-102): $500 initial, $500 renewal. Count: 107 laboratory agents active at 1 Aug 2026 (ADHS July report). Whether adult-use testing-facility workers hold “facility agent” licences or “laboratory agent” cards is [Ambiguous in opened reporting — the July report lists both categories separately, and R9-18 defines testing-facility workers as facility agents; do not add 107 + 16,061 into a single worker total without that clarification]. CannBus’s “98 active lab agent cards in Aug 2026” vs 107 in July is a second, smaller conflict — different definitions/dates, unresolved.

Not licences — do not list as licence types: qualifying patient / designated caregiver registry cards ($150/$75 SNAP / $200 in R9-17-102) — person permissions to possess/purchase; principal officers / board members (PO/BMs) — persons disclosed on a business licence, not separately licensed (except that they must themselves hold facility-agent eligibility; social-equity PO/BMs were the 51% owners in §4.4); medical director — a contracted role historically required for medical dispensaries; dual licensees are expressly exempt from the medical-director requirement (§36-2858). Current medical-only medical-director rule [Verify against current R9-17-306 et seq. — not opened in full]. Local licences/permits (city zoning, occupancy, food-establishment permits for edibles manufacturing) — required in practice but not ADHS marijuana licences; local rules vary by jurisdiction (Part 3 scope).

4.8 Are applications open as of October 2026?

Short answer: no open general establishment or social-equity round was found; agent renewals/changes and existing-licence transfers are a different matter.

  • Marijuana establishment (standard + rural-county): Not open as a general round. Statute permits issuance only via (a) the closed Jan–Mar 2021 early-applicant window, (b) random selection publicised ≥60 days in advance (§36-2854(A)(1)(e)), or (c) the closed social-equity allocation. A secondary check dated 27 September 2026 states: “No open round was found as of Sept 2026” and “Not right now” for establishment licences — Sativa University [Secondary — but consistent with the absence of any ADHS 60-day random-selection notice found in this research]. Parent action before publishing “closed”: check ADHS’s website/email notification system for a 60-day notice dated after September 2026 — absence of a notice in search results is not the same as an ADHS “closed” announcement.
  • Social equity: Closed and exhausted. The 26 licences were the statutory “additional” allocation; no second social-equity round is authorised by §36-2854(A)(1)(f) as written, and no 2026 social-equity application window was found. Future social-equity entry would require new legislation or a new ADHS programme — none enacted as of Oct 2026 (SB 1262 failed; no successor enacted law found).
  • Nonprofit medical dispensary registration: No new general round found open. Entry is via acquisition/transfer of an existing certificate or dual-licensing route, subject to ADHS approval — transfer procedure [Verify].
  • Marijuana testing facility (adult-use) / medical laboratory: the rules provide an application route (R9-18-402/403; R9-17 Art. 4) and the statute imposes no ratio cap on testing facilities, so a testing application is not barred by the 1:10 rule. Whether the ADHS Facility Licensing Portal is accepting testing applications on any given day in Oct 2026 [Verify — no dated ADHS portal-open notice was opened]. Do not publish testing as “open” or “closed” without that check.
  • Facility / dispensary / laboratory agent cards: ongoing — see §4.7.1.
  • Changes to existing licences (location, added site, changed activity, approval to operate): fee-bearing mechanisms exist in R9-18-102/R9-17-102 (§§4.1, 4.2) and are therefore available routes for existing holders, distinct from new-licence rounds.

Official information pages to cite: ARS §36-2854: azleg §36-2854; ARS §36-2804: azleg §36-2804; AAC R9-18-102: Cornell R9-18-102; AAC R9-17-102: Cornell R9-17-102; ADHS July 2026 Monthly Report: ADHS July 2026 (PDF); ADHS Social Equity Application Checklist (Nov 2021): ADHS checklist (PDF). ADHS is also cited in sources as operating the Facility Licensing Portal and the Individual Licensing Portal; portal URLs were not returned verbatim in this research and are therefore not hyperlinked here — insert the current ADHS licensing hub URL from ADHS itself rather than copying a guessed azdhs.gov path.

4.9 Estimated startup costs — ALL NON-OFFICIAL, ALL SECONDARY, CONFLICTING

These are NOT ADHS figures. ADHS publishes only the fees in §§4.1, 4.2, 4.5, 4.7. Anything beyond fees is a commercial estimate.

  • IndicaOnline, “How to Get a Dispensary License in Arizona 2026” (accessed via search, last updated ~117 days before 31 Oct 2026): “Budget $500K–$2M minimum for buildout and first-year operations, depending on location and scale”; “transfers regularly command seven-figure premiums”; “Most successful [social-equity] applicants come in with at least $1M in verifiable capital”; separate IndicaOnline cost page: Arizona “$300,000–$1,000,000” — IndicaOnline dispensary guide.
  • Cannabis Training University, state-by-state 2026 snapshot (36 days before 31 Oct 2026): Arizona application fee $25,000, “Startup Range: $750,000–$1.5 million” — Cannabis Training University.
  • THD Wholesale guide (61 days before 31 Oct 2026): “minimum cost of liquid assets … $500,000 … up to $2,000,000” (generic US framing applied to Arizona; its fee table mixes the old $5,000 medical registration figure) — THD Wholesale.
  • Secondary “licence alone $2M+” claim: IndicaOnline 2026 guide states new standard licences are “extremely difficult” and “expect $2M+ for a license alone” on acquisition — [Secondary, unverified transaction data — do not publish as a market price].
  • Testing-lab buildout cost: [Unavailable — no Arizona-specific lab startup estimate was found in opened sources; do not extrapolate the dispensary range to labs]
  • Delivery-only startup cost: [Unavailable as a standalone figure — delivery has no separate licence fee; its marginal cost is vehicles, GPS/video, locked compartments, insurance and facility-agent labour within an existing establishment, none of which was quantified in an opened Arizona-specific source]
  • Reconciliation: the honest published range is “state fees $25,000 application + $2,500 approval to operate + $5,000 biennial renewal for an establishment (plus $300/$150 per agent), with secondary commercial estimates of $300,000–$2,000,000 all-in for a dispensary buildout/first year, and seven-figure secondary-market premiums for acquiring an existing capped licence — all non-official.” Any single “cost to open in Arizona” number would misrepresent the sources.
