PART 7 — HEMP LICENCES (AZDA Industrial Hemp Program)

7.1 Legal basis

Statute: A.R.S. Title 3, Chapter 2, Article 4.1 (§§3-311 to 3-320), enacted by SB 1098 (2018). §3-311 defines industrial hemp as Cannabis sativa L. with delta-9 THC ≤0.3% dry weight. AZDA’s FAQ applies a total delta-9 gloss (including THCA conversion) — the statute-vs-FAQ tension carried from Run 1 is not resolved here; do not merge them. Source: AZDA Industrial Hemp FAQ (accessed 31 Oct 2026).

Rule: A.A.C. Title 3, Chapter 4, Article 10, §§R3-4-1001 to R3-4-1014. Adopted by exempt rulemaking at 25 A.A.R. 1447, effective 31 May 2019; amended by final rulemaking at 27 A.A.R. 1570, effective 16 September 2021. All rule text below is the post-2021 version as reproduced on Cornell LII, which does not reproduce Table 1 — see the fee warning in §7.5. Source: Cornell, R3-4-1005 and companion section pages (accessed 31 Oct 2026). Verify the table against the Secretary of State’s official compilation before republication.

Federal/state plan: Arizona operates its own USDA-approved state hemp plan, effective 1 January 2022 (Run 1, not contradicted). USDA’s approved-plans list, updated 17 February 2026, still shows “AZ Approved Plan (pdf)”. Source: USDA AMS, List of USDA-approved Hemp Plans (accessed 31 Oct 2026). The Arizona plan procedures document (Exhibit A, Summary of Procedures, dated 27 December 2021 in the extract) is a procedures document, not a producer roster. Source: Arizona Hemp Plan (accessed 31 Oct 2026).

No licence needed for finished goods: “You do not need to secure a license issued by the Department if your retail operation is exclusively selling, manufacturing, or marketing post-processed hemp materials or finished wholesale goods.” (AZDA FAQ.) AZDA also states it cannot give legal advice on whether raw hemp flower may be sold at retail — consult a private attorney. Hemp may not be grown for personal/backyard use — only for verified commercial or formal research purposes (same page).

7.2 Who must be licensed, and the five licence types (R3-4-1003(A))

“Any person that grows, harvests, transports, or processes industrial hemp in any of the following categories shall obtain the appropriate license from the Department and shall abide by the terms and conditions set forth in the licensing agreement.”

Source: Cornell, R3-4-1003 (accessed 31 Oct 2026).

LicenceWhat it allows (R3-4-1003(A))
GrowerObtain seed/propagative materials for planting; possess authorised seed/propagative materials; cultivate the crop; harvest plant parts; possess and store harvested plant parts; transport plant parts for processing.
NurseryPropagate eligible seed and propagative materials for planting for a licensed grower. A licensed nursery shall not grow industrial hemp for harvesting unless also licensed as a Grower.
HarvesterHarvest an eligible industrial hemp crop for a licensed grower.
TransporterTransport a harvested industrial hemp crop for a licensed grower.
ProcessorProcess, handle, and store industrial hemp or hemp seed at one or more authorised locations in the state; may sell, distribute, transfer, or gift products processed from harvested hemp that are not restricted in R3-4-1012.

Grower fee relief: a grower applicant/licensee need not pay separate harvester or transporter fees unless providing harvesting/transport services for other licensed growers — R3-4-1005(B). Research is a distinct treatment, not a sixth licence type: R3-4-1004 — see §7.4.

Per person/entity/site: a licence is issued to a person or business entity (with a named responsible party — an individual with signing authority, R3-4-1001), and it covers one or more registered sites under that licence. Growers, processors and nurseries add or move sites by site-modification application (R3-4-1003(G)); harvesters/transporters register the locations where their equipment/vehicles are primarily based (R3-4-1003(B)(4)–(5)). Location information is retained by the Department for not less than three years (R3-4-1003(B)).

Caps: None. Current AZDA FAQ: “The Department does not enforce any caps or limitations on the total number of licenses issued to eligible applicants, nor are there minimum or maximum size requirements for the acreage to be planted.” Scale (secondary, dated — Verify): a secondary industry page updated 14 April 2024 reported AZDA had issued 92 licences as of February 2024, “including” 18 grower, 6 nursery, 3 harvester, 9 transporter and 12 processor licences. Flag: those five figures sum to 48, not 92, so the breakdown is incomplete as reported. Source (secondary): Arizona Hemp Industry (secondary) (accessed 31 Oct 2026). No more recent official count was found.

7.3 Eligibility and application requirements

Eligibility — R3-4-1002 (source: Cornell, R3-4-1002, accessed 31 Oct 2026): (1) possess a valid fingerprint clearance card issued by Arizona DPS under A.R.S. §41-1758.07; (2) be a U.S. citizen or legal resident alien (exception: an individual enrolled in an academic programme at an accredited college/university who does not meet the criteria may be sponsored by an academic member who does meet them and provides proof); (3) be 18 years or older at application. Proof: legible photocopy (paper or electronic) of the fingerprint clearance card; documents listed in A.R.S. §41-1080(A) as evidence of age and citizenship/legal residency.

Statute — A.R.S. §3-314: “A grower, harvester, transporter or processor shall obtain an industrial hemp license from the department”; an original or renewal application must be on a department form and accompanied by the director-prescribed fee (deposited in the Industrial Hemp Trust Fund, §3-315); “An applicant shall provide proof to the department of having a valid fingerprint clearance card issued pursuant to section 41-1758.07”; “A license issued pursuant to this section is valid for one year, unless revoked, and may be renewed as provided by the department.” Source: azleg.gov, §3-314 (accessed 31 Oct 2026). Verify: a two-year renewal fee mechanism (paying twice the rule fee schedule) appears in a 2020 floor-amendment document for SB 1019; whether that language is in the current §3-314 text was not confirmed from the azleg page extract alone.

AZDA programme-page gloss (stricter wording — flag as programme guidance, not rule text): the current AZDA programme page states a Level I Fingerprint Clearance Card is mandatory “for the primary applicant and all key operational participants” and that AZDA “cannot accept alternative baseline background check credentials.” Source: AZDA Industrial Hemp Program (accessed 31 Oct 2026). The rule itself (R3-4-1002) speaks of the applicant/responsible party; the “all key participants” extension is programme-page wording.

Application contents — R3-4-1003(B) (all licences, at minimum): full name, mailing address, telephone, email; fingerprint clearance card identification number of the person or responsible party; if a business entity, full business name, principal Arizona business location address, full name/title/email of the responsible party; Tax ID or Social Security Number; disclosure/explanation of any denial, debarment, suspension, revocation, or other prohibition from public procurement or licensing. Plus, by type: Grower — registered planting site(s) (street address or major crossroads, legal description, GPS coordinates for each field, greenhouse, building or site; updated annually or within 30 days of a change; estimated acreage (outdoor) and/or square footage (indoor/greenhouse); maps/aerial photos showing entrances, field boundaries, and GPS-corresponding locations; storage locations (GPS) for seed/propagative materials and harvested plants/parts, with labelled maps/aerial photos); Nursery — storage locations (GPS) for seed/propagative materials; locations (GPS) of all propagation areas; labelled maps/aerial photos; Harvester — maps plus street address, legal description, GPS coordinates for each location where harvesting equipment will be primarily based; Transporter — same for transporting vehicles/equipment; Processor — identification of the part of the crop to be received (floral and leaf material; seed for oil or grain; stalks for fiber or hurds; seed or propagative materials for planting); registered processing site(s) (street address or major crossroads, legal description, GPS coordinates for each building/site or where mobile processing equipment will be primarily based; labelled maps/aerial photos).

FSA registration: the AZDA programme page states producers are “legally required to register all hemp crops with the USDA Farm Service Agency (FSA), with zero exceptions allowed” and that crops not properly filed with FSA are “strictly prohibited from entering the stream of commerce”; acreage reports must carry the state licence tracking string, identify fields/greenhouse subfields/“lots”, and classify intended market use as Fiber, Cannabidiol (CBD), Grain, or Seed. Verify whether it is imposed by the licensing agreement rather than by rule — it does not appear in the R3-4-1008/1011 rule extracts read.

Licensing agreement — R3-4-1003(E): must be completed before a licence is received. At minimum the applicant agrees to: provide access at any time to authorised inspectors for all hemp/hemp seed planted or stored and all records; maintain records under R3-4-1008; pay all Table 1 fees; comply with pesticide use restrictions; comply with state seed laws; defend/indemnify/hold harmless the Department from liability for destruction of any crop/harvested plant in violation of the article; be solely responsible for all financial/other losses; be solely responsible for all land-use restrictions and applicable city/county zoning, building, and fire codes/ordinances; follow all regulatory, notification, and reporting requirements.

Term, renewal, withdrawal, site modification, transfer — R3-4-1003(C)–(H): applications may be submitted at any time during the year; licence expiration is 31 December annually, or biennially for a two-year renewal authorised under subsection (D). Renewal applications are due no later than 15 December. One- or two-year renewal at licensee discretion only if: licensed in the programme within the previous calendar year; licence in good standing at renewal; no change in person or responsible party; no change in physical location of the site; licensee owes no civil penalties, fees, or late charges; and the associated one- or two-year renewal fee is submitted. Voluntary withdrawal: Department-prescribed withdrawal notice at least two weeks before withdrawal unless otherwise authorised by the Associate Director; any hemp/hemp seed planted, harvested, or stored must be inspected before transport off property, destruction, or transfer to a new/existing licensee; licensing/inspection fees paid or invoiced before the withdrawal notice are not refundable. Site modification: if a licensed grower, processor, or nursery changes an existing site’s location or adds sites during the licensing period, submit a site modification application and associated Table 1 fee. Transfer: authorised only if licensee and eligible applicant complete a Department-issued transfer application and submit applicable Table 1 transfer fees; receiver must complete a licensing application and execute a licensing agreement; all duties/responsibilities transfer and are acknowledged by receiver in a written agreement; any licence or other fees paid by the licensee are credited to the receiver.

Applications open in October 2026? Yes, on the current wording: applications may be made at any time with no seasonal cut-off, and the FAQ page states an expired licence holder “can re-apply for the program at any time” with “no administrative penalties or fines assessed for allowing a previous license to expire”. Wording conflict flagged: the older AZDA FAQ PDF dated 12 March 2024 (file Hemp FAQ 03-12-24.pdf, Hemp FAQ 03-12-24) states a previous holder can re-apply for up to three years with no penalty. The current FAQ page wording (no time limit stated) governs for this report; the three-year formulation should not be relied on.

How to apply (portal/form, exact names): no online portal was confirmed. The operative instrument found is a paper form: AZDA Industrial Hemp Program Application, file HempApplication.pdf (form footer “ADA PSD 06/22/22 BKM”, 10 pages), HempApplication.pdf (accessed 31 Oct 2026). Per that form, fees are due with applications by check or money order; new applicants may only apply for a one-year licence and become eligible for one- or two-year renewal after completing one season as a licensee. Related forms found: Additional Locations Supplement, file HempAddtlLocations (footer 06/23/22), which states a site-modification fee of $300 to add/relocate locations, no fee to remove a location or for additions/relocations submitted during renewal; and the Processor Notification Form, file AZDA-ProcessorNotificationForm (2019 version found), for monthly processor assessment reporting. The FAQ page gives the programme hotline (602) 542-0955 and intake email azhemp@azda.gov (official public contacts). Verify the current form version and submission route with AZDA before advising an applicant, as the newest form found is dated June 2022.

7.4 Research and the not-for-profit fee exemption — R3-4-1004

Source: Cornell, R3-4-1004 (accessed 31 Oct 2026). (A) A person, company, college, or university conducting research into growth, harvesting techniques, transportation methods, or processing of industrial hemp must obtain a licence under the article. Research is therefore not a separate licence type; it is licensed activity under the same article. (B) Not-for-profit research may be exempted from the licensing fee(s) if: (1) a written exemption request is submitted; (2) a summary of the research is provided; (3) a summary of the benefit to the agricultural community is provided; (4) the applicant signs an agreement that the research will not gain any monetary profit; (5) the research complies with the article and any other governing law/rule/order; and (6) the results or summary will be published or made publicly available. (C) The Department holds no rights to any intellectual property resulting from industrial hemp research. (D) Restrictions: (1) a licensee shall not change not-for-profit research to for-profit research without notifying the Department and paying the required licensing fee; (2) hemp and hemp products produced under a hemp research exemption, excluding hemp seed, are not eligible to enter the commercial stream of commerce. Research page: AZDA, Industrial Hemp Research (accessed 31 Oct 2026).

7.5 Fees — R3-4-1005 and Table 1 (read the warning first)

Table 1 warning — do not publish a single “current fee” per licence type without this note. Cornell LII reproduces the rule text but not Table 1. The AZDA-hosted Article 10 PDF (AZDA Title 3 Ch 4 Art 10) appears to be the pre-2021 compilation. The 2021 final-rulemaking preamble expressly states: “Changes to Table 1 include a reduction in licensing fees to reduce a burden on the regulated community and clarify language to indicate that the per-acreage inspection fee is for at least one acre that is inspected.” Source: Notice of Final Rulemaking [R21-156], 2021 final rulemaking (accessed 31 Oct 2026); also 27 A.A.R. 1570 (16 Sept 2021). The best current evidence is therefore the June 2022 AZDA application form.

Rule text verified (Cornell, post-2021, R3-4-1005): (A) all licensing/registration fees due at application; (B) grower harvester/transporter fee relief as in §7.2; (C) inspection/assessment fees invoiced by the Department, due within 30 calendar days of invoice; (D) site modification fees submitted with the site modification application under R3-4-1003(G); (E) processor assessment fees based on tonnage reports, shipping manifests, or scale receipts of unprocessed hemp plants/plant parts received; (F) all outstanding inspection/assessment fees invoiced before 15 November must be paid in full before the Department processes a renewal application; (G) THC sample analysis fees: beyond the initial pre-harvest sample for regulatory compliance of a harvest lot, a licensee must pay analytical fees before results are released for (1) pre-harvest re-tests for crops above the compliance threshold, (2) post-harvest samples determined to be a regulatory concern, (3) grower-requested official analysis for commerce.

LicencePre-2021 Table 1 / 2019 form (initial) — SUPERSEDED, for reconciliation only2022 form renewal, 1-year2022 form renewal, 2-year

A secondary summary citing the 2021 update likewise lists Grower $1,000 / Nursery $650 / Harvester $100 / Transporter $100 / Processor $2,000 (secondary, accessed 31 Oct 2026). Interpretation for the audit: the post-2021 licensing fees are most plausibly $1,000/$650/$100/$100/$2,000, but a verbatim current Table 1 from an official compilation was not reproduced in this session — Verify Table 1 against the Secretary of State before publishing fees as current.

Inspection/assessment amounts (pre-2021 Table 1 extract + AZDA Regulatory Bill of Rights, PSD Regulatory Bill of Rights — Verify against current Table 1): Grower: $25 per outdoor acre up to 100 acres, $5 per additional acre; $75 per indoor facility up to 3 acres, $25 per acre for facilities over 3 acres; $150 per THC sample analysis (plus laboratory analytical fees). Nursery/Harvester/Transporter: no inspection/assessment fee shown (N/A in the pre-2021 Table 1 extract). Processor: per-ton assessments on unprocessed material received — $5 per ton oil seed/grain; $100 per ton floral material; $150 per THC sample analysis (plus lab fees). A pre-2021 mirror extract also shows a fiber figure rendered as “$0.5 ton Fiber” — Verify; do not publish the fiber rate without confirming whether it reads $0.50/ton. Processor assessment notifications: within 30 calendar days of receipt, the processor files grower licence number, harvest lot number, amount, and date received — R3-4-1011(G)(2) (Cornell, R3-4-1011, accessed 31 Oct 2026). Site modification fee: $300 (2022 Additional Locations Supplement; also shown in the AZDA PDF Table 1 extract). Transfer fee: Verify. R3-4-1003(H) refers to “any applicable transfer fees listed in Table 1,” but no transfer-fee row appeared in any Table 1 extract read. No hemp-specific excise tax (Run 1 finding, not contradicted). Processor per-ton amounts above are regulatory assessments, not a sales/excise tax.

7.6 Location requirements, signage, indoor vs. outdoor — R3-4-1007

Source: Cornell, R3-4-1007 (accessed 31 Oct 2026). (A)(1) A licensed grower or processor shall not grow, process, or store industrial hemp in any residential dwelling. (A)(2) A licensee is responsible for maintaining compliance with all applicable city and county land use restrictions, zoning laws, building, and fire codes and ordinances. (A)(3) A registered location shall be made available for inspection at the request of an inspector during normal business hours. (A)(4) A licensed grower or processor shall not grow, process, or store any forms of Cannabis that are not classified as industrial hemp within a single structure at the registered location. (B) Signage: use of the AZDA logo or likeness is not permitted on signage (added 2021). A licensed grower or processor shall conspicuously post signage at the perimeter of the registered location stating: (1) “Arizona Department of Agriculture Industrial Hemp Program - No Trespassing Allowed”; (2) licensee’s name or company name and licence number; and (3) the AZDA Industrial Hemp Program phone number. (C)–(D) (from the 2021 rulemaking text): no unauthorised person shall grow/cultivate/handle/store/harvest/transport/import/process hemp, trespass on a registered site, disturb/damage/destroy a crop, or tamper with required signage; no authorised licensee shall transfer germinable hemp plants/parts/seed to an unauthorised person, destroy a crop/stored hemp/seed without prior Department notification, or import/export hemp plants/parts for processing or seed/propagative material for planting without notifying the Department and complying with all import/export regulatory requirements.

Indoor vs. outdoor: no distinct indoor containment, lighting, security, or setback rule for hemp was found in Article 10. The indoor/outdoor distinction in the material found is administrative only: applications state outdoor acreage vs. indoor/greenhouse square footage (R3-4-1003(B)(2)(b)), and inspection fees differ for outdoor acreage vs. indoor facilities (§7.5). There is no hemp-specific distance-from-school setback in R3-4-1007 (the 1,000-foot figures sometimes seen in search results belong to marijuana advertising/dispensary rules, not hemp). Verify before asserting any local indoor rule — city/county zoning under R3-4-1007(A)(2) is where an indoor restriction would bite.

7.7 Sampling, testing, THC compliance, destruction/remediation — R3-4-1008

Source: Cornell, R3-4-1008 (accessed 31 Oct 2026), post-2021 version.

Recordkeeping (B): records documenting geospatial location, growth, propagation, harvesting, storage, agronomic data, shipping, receiving, transportation, distribution, processing, sale, purchase, third-party analysis, or research of all plants, seeds, and materials shall be kept within Arizona, made available for inspection on request; an in-state agent must be maintained for receipt/storage of records; all records maintained for not less than five years. Additional requirements may be set by annually updated policy. Licensees are subject to audits for recordkeeping compliance; authorised inspectors have access to growing, storage, and processing locations, material, and handling/processing equipment for visual inspection.

Sampling (C): all licensees are subject to collection of a representative sample of any Cannabis plant, hemp crop, or harvested hemp to determine total delta-9 THC as reported by a certified laboratory (the State Agriculture Laboratory or a lab it certifies, R3-4-1001). Unless an alternative performance-based sampling policy applies, crops shall be sampled within 30 days prior to the intended harvest date, from mature flowering plants; all sampling agents must have undergone official sampling training by an authorised Department representative. A licensed grower shall not harvest before collection of an official sample. Only an authorised Department inspector or authorised sampling agent may collect an official sample; the licensee or authorised representative must be present; the sample is split in thirds (Department/certified lab; confirmation; licensee/third-party lab — third-party results do not supersede official results); samples are labelled (sample number, date, collector, location ID, grower licence ID), sealed, and chain-of-custody documented. A sample with a broken seal or incomplete/missing chain of custody is null and void. Official samples retained by the Department are Department property; the Department is not liable to reimburse the licensee.

Lab standards (C)(5)–(6): testing must detect the total calculable amount of delta-9 THC using post-decarboxylation or similarly reliable methods approved by the U.S. Secretary of Agriculture (GC or HPLC); results on a dry-weight basis; labs must report measurement of uncertainty (± value, same unit as the total delta-9 value) per USDA Laboratory Testing Guidelines (15 Jan 2021) / AOAC SMPR 2019.003; a result showing with at least 95% confidence that total delta-9 THC exceeds the acceptable hemp level is conclusive evidence of non-compliance. Certified labs must be DEA-registered to handle controlled substances under 21 CFR part 1301.13 no later than 31 December 2022 (rule text) — read with the federal enforcement delay below.

Compliant crops (D)(1): on a compliant finding the grower receives documentation authorising movement of the harvest lot; the lot shall not be comingled with any other compliant or non-compliant lot; the grower shall harvest within 30 business days (notify the Department of delay due to inclement weather or other natural causes) and notify the Department before shipping/transporting under R3-4-1011(D).

Non-compliant crops (D)(2): crops with total delta-9 THC greater than 0.3% shall not enter commerce. Within 15 business days of notification of non-compliance, the grower must either voluntarily dispose by a R3-4-1013(F) method and submit a notice of destruction under R3-4-1011(E) with supporting evidence, or submit a corrective action plan under R3-4-1013(D) to remediate to compliance. A corrective action plan will only be approved if total delta-9 THC is greater than 0.3% and less than 1.0%. Failure to dispose or comply may result in a notice of violation under R3-4-1012. On notification, the grower shall not move/transport the non-compliant crop from the site unless permitted by the Department to remediate. Non-compliant crops shall not be comingled. Harvest lots with total delta-9 THC greater than 1.0% constitute a violation and must be disposed of by a R3-4-1013(F) method.

Volunteer plants (E): it is the licensee’s responsibility to monitor and destroy volunteer hemp plants.

Performance-based sampling — SP 21-02: the AZDA programme page confirms hemp microgreens and greens are governed by performance-based sampling under Substantive Policy Statement SP 21-02 (verified seed, timely reporting, harvest before flowering, visual verification). Policy document: SP 21-02 (found by search; full text not re-opened in this session — Verify current status and whether SP19-01 remains operative). The earlier extract of SP 21-02 exceptions (microgreens ≤14 days/≤5 inches/not flowering; greens ≤10 inches/not flowering; nursery transplants not standard-sampled; mother plants ≤0.3% exempt from future sampling if compliant) is consistent with the programme page but should be re-checked against the policy PDF itself.

DEA-lab delay (federal baseline, Arizona lockstep): the AZDA programme page states that under the USDA Final Rule (86 FR 5596) and R3-4-1008(C)(6), commercial lots were scheduled to transition to DEA-registered lab testing, but USDA delayed enforcement for inadequate nationwide DEA lab capacity and “the Arizona Department of Agriculture has extended its enforcement discretion” in lockstep (programme page). This sits alongside the given federal baseline (USDA DEA-lab enforcement delayed to 31 December 2026) — no conflict found, but note the rule text’s 31 December 2022 date is superseded in practice by the delay.

7.8 Notifications and reports — R3-4-1011 (post-2021 version)

Source: Cornell, R3-4-1011 (accessed 31 Oct 2026). All notifications/reports are on Department-provided forms unless otherwise indicated or directed by the Associate Director. Note the 2021 changes: the pre-2021 duties (14-day intent-to-harvest notice, 72-hour intent-to-transport notice, 48-hour damage notice, 14-day business-change notice, 7-day planting report) are struck through in the current text and replaced by: (B) Planting Report — within 5 business days after planting a harvest lot: licensee contact information including licence number; unique harvest lot ID assigned by grower/nursery; geospatial location of the site; variety name; actual area planted; estimated harvest/transplant date. (This matches the programme page’s “failure to submit within 5 business days of planting constitutes a direct violation.”) (C) Notice of Intent to Harvest — within 30 calendar days prior to harvest, for each harvest lot to be sampled: grower contact/licence number; harvest lot ID as on the planting report; geospatial location(s); variety name; size of area to be harvested; intended harvest date. (D) Notice of Intent to Transport — within 3 business days prior to transporting a lot of harvested hemp for processing, for each lot: grower contact/licence number; lot ID; geospatial location(s); variety name; amount to be transported; intended transport date; receiver contact information. (E) Notice of Destruction — within 3 calendar days after a grower finds a lot significantly damaged, completely destroyed, or has disposed of a lot: grower contact/licence number; lot ID; geospatial location(s); variety name; size of area subject to damage/destruction/disposal; date damage/destruction discovered or date of disposal. (F) Grower and nursery annual reports — by 31 December each year: sale/distribution of industrial hemp grown under the licence; name/address of recipient; amount sold/distributed. (G) Processor notifications: (1) for importation of hemp material for processing, notify the Department within 3 business days of receipt, including: shipping manifest (shipper name, physical address, phone, total weight); regulatory-official documentation attesting acceptable total delta-9 THC concentration; grower’s certificate/licence/equivalent authorising production in that state; phytosanitary certificate if required, certificate of inspection, or certificate of origin from a plant regulatory official. (2) For processor assessment invoicing (Table 1), file within 30 calendar days of receipt: grower licence number; harvest lot number issued by the Department or authorising state; amount; date received. (H) Other notifications: notify the Department within 3 business days of receipt of results of any third-party analysis determining a crop/plant sample contained total delta-9 THC greater than 1.0%. (Pre-2021 text used a 0.300% trigger and 72 hours; the current text uses 1.0% and 3 business days — do not mix the versions.)

7.9 Seed and propagative material — R3-4-1006

Source: Cornell R3-4-1006 (accessed 31 Oct 2026) and the full-text mirror (accessed 31 Oct 2026); the 2021 amendment eased reporting here (pre-planting report item struck in one extract — the current text below follows the mirror/Cornell substance and the point is flagged). Authorised seed/propagative material is not a guarantee of ≤0.300% total delta-9 THC. Material used to produce a crop/plant shall (1) be produced from an industrial hemp crop/plant and (2) originate from either (a) a person/business/college/university licensed or certified in a state or federal programme authorised to produce industrial hemp, or (b) a foreign source authorised by its country of origin to export hemp seed/propagative material to produce a crop. Grower/nursery is responsible for acquisition and must provide the Department before planting: producer certificate/licence or equivalent authorising production; official analysis of the crop/plant that produced the seed/propagative material showing ≤0.300% total delta-9 THC dry weight; phytosanitary or nursery certificates from a plant regulatory official for propagative materials (A.R.S. §3-211; Title 3, Chapter 4, Article 2). Verify: whether the pre-planting report (variety/strain name, amount/quantity, lot number(s), provider name/address/phone/email) survives the 2021 amendment — it appeared struck through in one Cornell extract, and the 2021 preamble says R3-4-1006 “eases reporting requirements that have been determined to be redundant.” Labelling: all hemp seed/propagative material sold within or into Arizona must be labelled as to variety/strain or hybrid name and origin (hybrid number/designation counts as variety name); labelers must provide the Department breeder descriptions and variety release information including updates. All hemp seed for planting sold within or into Arizona is subject to Arizona seed laws, A.R.S. §§3-231 et seq. and Title 3, Chapter 4, Article 4. Restrictions: a person receiving non-compliant seed/propagative material shall immediately notify the Department and hold it until disposition is provided; the Department may direct a shipment to be held for compliance. Hemp plants for planting produced by a licensed nursery for intrastate/interstate shipment must have been produced from authorised material under R3-4-1006(A) in compliance with Director laws/rules/orders. Seed dealer/labeler overlay (programme page): entities packaging/labelling hemp seeds for sale in Arizona must secure an active seed labeler licence and conform to state seed labelling laws; anyone selling seeds for propagation/planting must hold a valid state Seed Dealer Licence (programme page). This is in addition to, not instead of, the hemp licence.

7.10 Import, export, and interstate movement

Arizona import/export rule — R3-4-1012(D)(3) (current text, Cornell, R3-4-1012, accessed 31 Oct 2026): no authorised licensee shall import or export industrial hemp plants or plant parts for processing, or seed/propagative material for planting, without notifying the Department and complying with all import or export regulatory requirements. The pre-2021 standalone transport-notification violation (former (D)(3)) was removed in 2021; intrastate movement to a processor is now governed by the R3-4-1011(D) Notice of Intent to Transport (3 business days) and the processor’s receipt notifications (§7.8). No separate Arizona manifest-carry rule was found — do not invent one; the processor import notification requires a shipping manifest copy (shipper name/address/phone, total weight) plus compliance documentation, grower licence, and phytosanitary/inspection/origin certificate (§7.8(G)(1)).

Federal through-transport protection (given framework, not an Arizona rule): 7 U.S.C. §1621 / Farm Bill §10114(b) and 7 CFR §990.63: “No State or Indian Tribe may prohibit the transportation or shipment of hemp lawfully produced under a State or Tribal plan approved under subpart B, under a licence issued under subpart C, or under 7 U.S.C. 5940 through the State or territory of the Indian Tribe, as applicable.” Source: 7 CFR 990.63 (accessed 31 Oct 2026). Secondary analyses stress the distinction between transport through a state (federally protected for lawfully produced hemp) and sale into a destination state (destination retail restrictions still apply); after the federal total-THC change, a product failing the new definition/cap would lose “compliant hemp” status and therefore its transport protection. These are secondary federal analyses, not Arizona transport rules.

Practical consequence for Arizona operators: an Arizona licensee shipping out of state still needs the destination state’s permission for sale there, and an out-of-state shipment into an Arizona processor triggers the R3-4-1011(G) 3-business-day import notification with the four documents listed in §7.8. Verify with AZDA whether an in-state grower-to-processor shipment needs anything beyond the (D) notice — the rule text read does not impose a separate in-state manifest.

7.11 Violations, corrective actions, penalties — R3-4-1012, R3-4-1013, R3-4-1014

Violations — R3-4-1012 (current text, accessed 31 Oct 2026): (A) a licensee commits a violation by: (1) failing to provide a legal description of land where the licensee grows, processes, stores, or researches hemp/hemp seed; (2) failing to obtain the proper licence; (3) producing or distributing Cannabis sativa with total delta-9 THC greater than 1.0% dry weight, unless otherwise permitted by state/federal law, rule, or order (note: the pre-2021 threshold in this paragraph was 0.300%; the 0.3%–1.0% band is now handled by corrective action, not as a paragraph (A)(3) violation); (4) violating a term/condition of the signed licensing agreement or corrective action plan; or (5) violating any law, rule, or order in the regulation of industrial hemp. (B) False statement: any person who materially falsifies application information is ineligible to participate in the programme. (C) No unauthorised person shall grow/cultivate/handle/store/harvest/transport/import/process hemp; trespass on a registered site; disturb/damage/destroy a crop; or tamper with R3-4-1007(B) signage. (D) No authorised licensee shall: (1) transfer germinable hemp plants/parts/seed to an unauthorised person; (2) destroy a crop/stored hemp/seed without prior Department notification; or (3) import/export as in §7.10. (E) Intentional or knowing violations (“culpable mental state greater than negligence”) shall be reported to the State Attorney General, the U.S. Attorney General, and relevant state/local law enforcement. Negligent violations are not subject to federal, state, tribal, or local criminal enforcement action.

Corrective actions — R3-4-1013 (Cornell, R3-4-1013, accessed 31 Oct 2026): (A)–(B) in addition to suspension, revocation, and civil-penalty procedures under R3-4-1014, a violator is subject to a corrective action plan; the Associate Director may request the licensee submit one or impose a written, dated plan for a negligent violation/non-compliance. (C) A plan must include: requirements to correct the violation under (D); a reasonable completion date; and, for violations pursued under A.R.S. §3-319, periodic compliance reports for not less than two years from the date of the violation. (Note: the pre-2021 text required reports for at least three years from the plan date; the current text ties the two-year period to §3-319 violations and the violation date. The USDA plan document still cites a three-year figure in one passage — flag as a version difference, and follow the current rule text.) (D) The plan may prescribe, in addition to A.R.S. §3-317 components: (a) stripping stalks and disposal of floral material; (b) sterilisation of seed and disposal of floral material; (c) THC remediation of leaf/floral material as prescribed by the Associate Director; (d) blending and milling of the entire plant/crop to a homogenised state, then resampling for compliance; (e) education and training; (f) other measures prescribed by the Associate Director. Crops/plants in the >0.300%–<1.0% band shall not be removed from the registered site unless authorised by the Associate Director to complete an approved plan. Failure to complete a prescribed measure within the plan timeframe, or to complete any component, constitutes a second violation. The cost of implementing a corrective action plan is the licensee’s burden. (E) Repeat negligent violations: three violations in a five-year period makes a person ineligible for an industrial hemp licence for five years from the third violation; all negligent violations within one year count as one negligent violation. (F) Disposal methods (voluntary or ordered): plowing under; mulching or composting; disking; bush mower or chopper; deep burial; burning or incinerating. (The R3-4-1001 “Disposal” definition adds tilling, chopping into green manure, and burying/covering with soil — consistent in substance.)

Penalties — R3-4-1014 (Cornell, R3-4-1014, accessed 31 Oct 2026): (A) civil penalties “shall be imposed under A.R.S. §3-319.” The former dollar tiers ($1,000 first offense / $2,500 second / $5,000 third) are struck through in the current Cornell text — do not quote them as current. The current dollar amounts, if any, in §3-319 were not opened for this part — Verify §3-319 before stating a civil-penalty figure. (B) Licence suspension: a violator’s licensing privileges may be suspended until completion of corrective actions prescribed in R3-4-1013. (C) Licence revocation: a person who intentionally violates the article/licence terms/Department rules or orders, or commits a third negligent offense within five years, may be subject to: (1) revocation of all licences under the article; (2) seizure and destruction of all hemp crops, seed, and harvested hemp at the licensee’s cost; and (3) ineligibility for a licence under the article for not less than five years. (D) Intentional or knowing violations by unlicensed individuals shall be punished according to A.R.S. §§3-319 and 13-3405 (the rule text repeats “13-3405” — sic).

7.12 AZDA response to the federal 12 November / 11 December 2026 hemp dates — NONE FOUND

Re-check performed for this part: the current AZDA FAQ page and programme page opened in full contain no mention of PL 119-37, PL 119-103, a federal total-THC definition, or a 0.4 mg per-container cap (FAQ page and programme page, accessed 31 Oct 2026). A site-scoped web search for agriculture.az.gov hemp pages mentioning “total THC,” “119-37,” or “0.4 mg” returned no AZDA page on the federal change. The FAQ page still states the Arizona definition as total delta-9 THC ≤0.3% dry weight including THCA conversion — which already resembles the incoming federal total-THC test for production, but Arizona has no published guidance found on the 0.4 mg per-container finished-product cap, the synthetic/non-plant cannabinoid exclusions, or the seed exclusion. Finding: no Arizona statute, rule, or AZDA guidance responding to PL 119-37 §781 / PL 119-103 §2019 was found as of 31 October 2026. This confirms the Run 1 finding. Practical note for the audit (not an Arizona rule): Arizona’s production-side total delta-9 test means Arizona growers already test on a THCA-inclusive basis, but Arizona finished-product sellers remain exposed to the federal cap change and to the AG’s existing position (I24-005, 11 Mar 2024, and 24 Mar 2025 letters) that unlicensed THC edibles/beverages are unlawful — neither of which is a response to the 2026 federal dates.

7.13 Roster gap — still no public roster; routes to the data

AZDA site: no public hemp licensee roster was found on the AZDA FAQ page, programme page, research page, or application/form pages opened or searched for this part. This confirms the Run 1 roster gap. USDA HeMP public search: the tool at USDA HeMP Public Search Tool could not be read by text fetch in this session — it returns only a JavaScript “Loading” shell, so no Arizona producer result could be verified through it. Do not cite HeMP as a verified roster source for Arizona on the basis of this session; the check is Unavailable via text fetch and needs a live-browser check. What exists instead: under the USDA-approved plan, AZDA must collect and maintain producer contact information, legal descriptions, licence numbers, and status changes and report them to USDA-AMS (plan Exhibit A) — but that reporting is not published as a public roster. Routes to obtain the list: (1) Arizona public-records request to AZDA for the current industrial hemp licensee list (programme hotline (602) 542-0955; intake azhemp@azda.gov — official public contacts, FAQ page); (2) USDA-AMS public-records/FOIA request for Arizona state-plan producer data reported under the plan; (3) FSA county-office acreage data is producer-specific and is not a substitute public roster. Until one of these returns, the correct statement is: no public Arizona hemp licensee roster was found as of 31 October 2026.

PART 8 — CANNABIS-ZONED PROPERTIES

8.0 Verdict: ZERO properties meet the strict verified standard

No property found in this research meets all three limbs of the strict standard as of 31 October 2026: (a) currently listed for sale/lease, and (b) cannabis use verified by a city/town zoning document, use permit/CUP, or ADHS licence record — not by listing copy alone, and (c) with a confirmed current asking price/rent. Every candidate below fails at least one limb, and the failures are stated per property. This is not a finding that no cannabis-zoned property exists in Arizona; it is a finding that none could be verified to the required standard from the sources checked. The two closest candidates — 2220 & 2222 West McDowell Road, Phoenix, and 1655 South Sullivan Lane, Camp Verde — carry explicit broker claims of an approved cannabis use, but in both cases the underlying city/town permit document was not produced and current availability/price is unconfirmed or conflicting (see §§8.3–8.4). They are classified CONDITIONAL-STRONG, not verified.

8.1 How to verify cannabis zoning in Arizona (in order)

  1. City/town zoning code first. Arizona has no statewide cannabis zoning map. Each city/town sets its own districts, and many restrict marijuana establishments to industrial/commercial districts with buffers. Confirm the parcel’s zoning district in the city’s zoning map/code, then confirm marijuana use is allowed in that district (by right, by use permit, or by special exception/CUP) — a generic industrial zoning (e.g., A-2, M1) does not by itself prove cannabis is allowed.
  2. Use permit / CUP / registration second. In most Arizona cities the operative permission is a discretionary approval, not the zoning label: Phoenix uses a use-permit model; Tucson uses a special exception in C-2/C-3; Mesa uses a registration model in LI/GI; Chandler uses administrative clearance. Ask for the permit number, approval date, approved use (dispensary / cultivation / manufacturing / infusion-extraction), and whether it runs with the land or is personal to the licensee and whether it survives a sale/lease. A lapsed, revoked, or licensee-personal permit does not transfer with the real estate.
  3. ADHS licence third. Confirm the marijuana establishment / nonprofit medical dispensary licence (and any off-site cultivation/manufacturing approval) on ADHS records, and confirm it is attached to that address and transferable or re-issuable to a buyer/tenant. Arizona licences are scarce and generally do not transfer automatically with a property sale; a property that “comes with” a licence claim needs the ADHS paper, not the broker’s word.
  4. Buffers and caps last. Even a permitted district can fail on buffers (schools, churches, parks, childcare, other dispensaries — distances vary by city; e.g., Phoenix’s large buffers, Lake Havasu City’s 500-foot buffers) and on population caps (e.g., Yuma’s 1-per-50,000 rule). Re-measure buffers from the parcel, not from the listing’s assurances.
  5. County islands are a trap. Property on a Maricopa County island (as claimed for #1880) is outside city jurisdiction: city taxes and city zoning do not apply, but county zoning and ADHS licensing still do. Verify county zoning, not the nearest city’s code.

Cities whose zoning can make a property genuinely cannabis-zoned (cross-reference to Run 1 Part 3 — districts/buffers as established in Run 1, not re-verified here): Phoenix (use-permit model, large buffers); Tucson (UDC C-2/C-3 special exception); Mesa (LI/GI registration); Chandler (administrative clearance); Gilbert, Scottsdale, Peoria, Flagstaff (dual medical+adult-use pattern); Yuma (dual-purpose B-2/L-I, 1 per 50,000 cap); Prescott Valley (dual-only in CG/CI/PM/IG/IH); Lake Havasu City (500-foot buffers); Casa Grande (overlay); City of Maricopa (GC district, secondary source). Do not copy one city’s districts onto another city’s property — Chino Valley, Camp Verde, and county-island parcels each need their own town/county check. Note the same-name warnings: the Chino Valley, Camp Verde, and Phoenix properties below are all in Arizona (Chino Valley and Camp Verde in Yavapai County; Phoenix in Maricopa County); they are not the same-name places in other states.

8.4 Why the strongest candidates still fail the strict standard

  • West McDowell (candidate 3) is the only candidate with an explicit “Use Permit approved for: Cultivation, Infusion & Extraction” statement tied to a stated zoning district (A-2). It fails only on currency and documentation: a March 2024 broker brochure, no permit number, no price, no current listing found. A Phoenix permit-portal check for 2220/2222 W McDowell Rd is the single check most likely to convert a CONDITIONAL to verified.
  • 1655 Sullivan (candidate 2) is the only lease candidate with an explicit “verified cannabis cultivation approval” claim tied to stated districts (M1/R1-35/C3) and corroboration across four commercial sources. It fails on documentation (no Town of Camp Verde permit produced) and on rent (two conflicting rates, $10 vs. $12/SF/YR) plus a built-year conflict.
  • Chino Valley Farms (candidate 1) has the cleanest current commercial record (May 2026 listing + Colliers OM, firm $2.8M sale price, 12+ years’ licensed cultivation use) but no zoning district or permit anywhere in the materials — the listing expressly pushes verification onto the buyer. A Town of Chino Valley zoning confirmation is the required check.
  • Metro South/North (candidates 4–5) use the strongest listing language (“fully permitted”) but supply the least corroboration: no rent, no permit number, no address-level licence evidence, and stale/conflicting listing dates.

PART 9 — REGULATORS AND HISTORY

Method note — read before using this part. In the Part 9–10 research session the following pages were directly opened: USDA AMS List of USDA-approved Hemp Plans (updated 17 February 2026); A.R.S. §36-2854 (azleg.gov). The following were found in search but not directly opened in that session, so their contents are reported as search/secondary findings and marked Verify where direct opening is required: ADHS licensing hub and Director’s Blog recall explainer; ADHS Third Annual Medical Marijuana Report 2014; Saguaro Healing and White Mountain opinions on Justia; Dobson case notes; Whitmire summary; SB 1494 House summary; §32-1929; SRPMIC Ordinance SRO-576-2024 and SRPMIC marijuana FAQs/guidelines; GRIC LUPZ adult-use cultivation permit packet and GRIC News 22 February 2022 article; Navajo Nation DOJ cannabis overview (New Mexico Legislature handout); USDA San Carlos Apache and Tonto Apache hemp plan PDFs; RCH Wellness opinion (Justia, filed 30 April 2026); Acre 41 dismissal order (MJBizDaily-hosted court PDF); Vasquez/Juicy Joint reporting; Cave/Sherri Dunn/Trulieve reporting and dismissal reporting; HB 2179 chapter text and fact sheets; 2026 sine-die and repeal-initiative reporting. Do not present search-only items as directly read.

9(a) Every regulator with a role

1. Arizona Department of Health Services (ADHS) — Bureau of Marijuana Licensing / Division of Licensing Services — the cannabis regulator

Statutory authority. Medical: A.R.S. Title 36, Chapter 28.1 (Prop 203, AMMA), §§36-2801 et seq. Adult-use: Title 36, Chapter 28.2 (Prop 207, Smart and Safe Arizona Act), §§36-2850 et seq. ADHS (called “the department” in both chapters) licenses nonprofit medical marijuana dispensaries, marijuana establishments, marijuana testing facilities, and dual licensees, and adopts the implementing rules (A.A.C. Title 9, Chapter 17 medical; Chapter 18 adult-use).

Structure — what was found. ADHS’s Public Health Licensing page describes the Division of Licensing Services as licensing and monitoring health and child care facilities and providers throughout Arizona, with licensing inspections, on-site surveys, and complaint investigations, and lists “Marijuana Licensing” as the bureau responsible for licensing marijuana qualified patients, caregivers, agents, and facilities. Source: ADHS Licensing — search result only, Verify against the live ADHS licensing hub before republication. Official hub (Run 1): ADHS Marijuana Licensing.

Director — 2025–2026 leadership change, successor Verify. Jennie Cunico was ADHS Director in January 2025 (ADHS news release, 2 January 2025, headed “Katie Hobbs | Governor / Jennie Cunico | Director”). Source: ADHS news release, 2 Jan 2025 — search result only. In May 2025 Cunico resigned after it became clear the Senate would not confirm her nomination; Governor Hobbs accepted the resignation. Sources: Rose Law Group Reporter, May 2025 (secondary). Audit correction: Jennie Cunico’s resignation was announced 30 April 2025. Sheila Sjolander served as interim Director from April 2025. Governor Hobbs appointed Debbie Johnston to lead ADHS on 24 November 2025, succeeding Sjolander; Johnston signed as ADHS Director on ADHS Governing Body Bylaws dated 27 March 2026. Full Senate confirmation status — Verify. Do not name Cunico, Will Humble (2011–2014 materials), or Cara Christ (former director in audit coverage) as the current director. Bureau-level leadership (Bureau of Marijuana Licensing chief) was also not confirmed — Verify.

How to contact officially. Via the ADHS Marijuana Licensing hub and AZCareCheck search (Run 1): ADHS Marijuana Licensing and AZCareCheck. No individual phone/email is reproduced here, per task rules. Verify the current complaint-portal URL on the live hub before publication; the complaint process itself was not directly opened in this session.

Enforcement powers — directly opened statute. §36-2854(B) (azleg.gov, §36-2854, opened in the Part 9–10 session):

“B. The department may: 1. Subject to title 41, chapter 6, article 10, deny any application submitted or deny, suspend or revoke, in whole or in part, any registration or license issued under this chapter if the registered or licensed party or an officer, agent or employee of the registered or licensed party does any of the following: (a) Violates this chapter or any rule adopted pursuant to this chapter. (b) Has been, is or may continue to be in substantial violation of the requirements for licensing or registration and, as a result, the health or safety of the general public is in immediate danger. 2. Subject to title 41, chapter 6, article 10, and unless another penalty is provided elsewhere in this chapter, assess a civil penalty against a person that violates this chapter or any rule adopted pursuant to this chapter in an amount not to exceed $2,000 for each violation. Each day a violation occurs constitutes a separate violation.”

Unauthorised delivery carries a separate $20,000-per-violation civil penalty to the Smart and Safe Arizona Fund under §36-2854(E), enforceable by the Attorney General (same page, directly opened).

Inspection frequency — directly opened statute. §36-2854(B)(3) (same page):

“3. At any time during regular hours of operation, visit and inspect a marijuana establishment, marijuana testing facility or dual licensee to determine if it complies with this chapter and rules adopted pursuant to this chapter. The department shall make at least one unannounced visit annually to each facility licensed pursuant to this chapter.”

That is the statutory floor. Historical inspection volume (for context only, not a current frequency): ADHS Third Annual Medical Marijuana Report, A.R.S. §36-2809, 13 November 2014, for 1 July 2013–30 June 2014: 45 Approval to Operate certificates issued, 38 operational; 81 Approval to Operate inspections at 81 facilities; 113 compliance inspections at 76 dispensaries. Source: ADHS Third Annual Report 2014 (PDF) — search result only, Verify. Recall process (secondary ADHS blog summary, not a rule): Arizona State Health Laboratory Compliance Team checks products for Table 3.1 contaminants; inspections/audits are scheduled annually with laboratories plus unannounced inspections on complaint; on recall, the ADHS Licensing team contacts the facility, uses a questionnaire, and repeat-tests at two different laboratories. Source: ADHS Director’s Blog, recalls — search result only, Verify against A.A.C. Title 9, Chapter 18 recall rules before citing as law.

What ADHS does NOT do. It does not administer TPT/excise (ADOR), hemp production licensing (AZDA, except that ADHS-licensed establishments may handle marijuana, not hemp, under their cannabis licences), tribal cannabis programmes, or local zoning (cities/counties). It does not operate a single state-contracted seed-to-sale platform: §36-2854(A)(4) (directly opened) requires the licensee to “procure, develop, acquire and maintain a system to track marijuana and marijuana products” meeting the listed accounting, chain-of-custody and transactional-stamp requirements. This confirms Run 1’s correction: Arizona does NOT use Metrc/BioTrack as a single state platform.

2025–2026 changes found. Director resignation May 2025 (above). Delivery rules reported as live since 1 November 2024 under R9-18-312.01 in secondary summaries (Run 1) — AUDIT: rule text not opened from a primary source; Verify against the official A.A.C. R9-18-312.01 text and the 30 A.A.R. 3451 rulemaking notice before quoting the live date or the $10,000 trip cap as a rule. HB 2179 advertising rewrite effective from and after June 30, 2026 per chapter text (paraphrased as 1 July 2026 in azleg summaries) — AUDIT RESOLVED, see audit table. No ADHS “fronting” enforcement order for 2024–2026 was located in Run 1 or this session — not found, not proof none exists in an unpublished file.

2. Arizona Department of Revenue (ADOR) — tax administration

Authority. Transaction privilege tax (TPT) under Title 42; adult-use marijuana excise (16%) under §42-5452; Smart and Safe Arizona Fund distribution under §36-2856 (Run 1). ADHS shares licensee name, physical address, cultivation site and TPT licence number with ADOR on request under §36-2854(J) (directly opened). What it does / does not do. ADOR collects and reports marijuana tax; it does not license establishments, set potency/packaging rules, or inspect facilities. Official sources (Run 1, re-verify before republication): ADOR Marijuana Tax Collection; MJ by Total Collections Category (PDF); MJ by Period Covered (PDF); MJ by Jurisdiction Type (PDF). 2025–2026 change found in this session: none specific to ADOR administration was opened. Verify any ADOR leadership or reporting-format change on the live ADOR page before publication.

3. Arizona Department of Agriculture (AZDA) — hemp

Authority. A.R.S. Title 3, Chapter 2, Article 4.1 (§§3-311 et seq., added by SB 1098, Chapter 287, 2018); A.A.C. Title 3, Chapter 4, Article 10. Arizona runs its own USDA-approved state hemp plan effective 1 January 2022 (Run 1; USDA list below confirms the state plan remains “Approved *” under the final rule effective 22 March 2021). What it does / does not do. AZDA licenses hemp growers/processors, administers the state plan, sampling/testing and compliance for hemp production. It does not license marijuana establishments, regulate hemp-derived THC retail products as marijuana, or set a hemp retail age/testing/labelling regime — no such Arizona rule was located in Run 1 or this session (Unavailable). 2025–2026 change: none confirmed in this session. AZDA FAQ and rules (Run 1): AZDA Hemp FAQ; AZDA Title 3 Ch 4 Art 10 (PDF) — Verify current text before republication. Note the statute-vs-FAQ tension carried from Run 1: §3-311 defines industrial hemp by delta-9 ≤0.3% dry weight, while the AZDA FAQ gloss uses total delta-9 including THCA conversion — flag, do not merge.

4. Attorney General (AG) — advertising and THC enforcement

Authority. §36-2859 (advertising, as rewritten by HB 2179, Chapter 166, 2025); §36-2854(E) (unauthorised-delivery penalty enforcement); general consumer-protection and controlled-substance enforcement. THC enforcement (Run 1, carried forward). AG Opinion I24-005 (R24-001) — AUDIT RESOLVED, I24-003 variant deleted — 11 March 2024 (date Verify against the opinion PDF/metadata): Arizona law does not permit sale of delta-8/other hemp-synthesised intoxicants by non-ADHS-licensed entities. AG letters 24 March 2025 extended this to all THC edibles/beverages by unlicensed entities including delta-9, enforcement from 24 April 2025. No AG advertising-enforcement action under the rewritten §36-2859 was located in this session — Verify against AG press releases before claiming any 2026 enforcement count. The AG does not license establishments or set ADHS rules. 2026 social-equity item (new in this session, not law). SCR 1047 (2026), as passed by Committee of the Whole, would have allowed the AG, in consultation with ADHS, to investigate predatory agreements/fraudulent acquisition of social-equity licences and to seek revocation, card denial/revocation, receivership and permanent disqualification; the floor amendment removed receivership/permanent-disqualification provisions. Source: SCR 1047 summary (PDF) — search result only; SCR 1047 is a proposed referral, NOT current law, and its final 2026 status must be confirmed on azleg.gov before any further statement. Do not present it as an AG power.

5. Department of Liquor Licences and Control (DLLC) — THC beverages, advisory role ONLY

Role (Run 1, carried forward). DLLC issued a THC-beverage advisory dated 24 April 2025. It is not a cannabis licensing authority and does not license THC beverage manufacturers, retailers, or establishments. Verify the advisory text and any 2025–2026 update on the DLLC site before republication; no new DLLC action was opened in this session.

6. Department of Public Safety (DPS) / Arizona Supreme Court — expungement under §36-2862

Authority and procedure (Run 1 + secondary guide, Verify against statute/forms). Effective / petition start 12 July 2021. Eligible pre-effective-date conduct: possessing/consuming/transporting ≤2.5 oz marijuana (≤12.5 g concentrate); possessing/transporting/cultivating/processing ≤6 plants at the individual’s primary residence for personal use; possessing/using/transporting related paraphernalia. Petition in the court where the case was resolved, or superior court of the county of arrest if only law-enforcement arrest records are sought; separate petition per case number; prosecuting agency has 30 days to respond; court shall grant unless the prosecutor establishes by clear and convincing evidence the petitioner is ineligible; granting order vacates the adjudication/conviction, expunges records, restores civil rights including firearms rights unless ineligible on other grounds, and requires the clerk to notify DPS, the prosecuting agency, and the arresting agency; denial is directly appealable under §13-4033(A)(3). Supreme Court resource cited in secondary guide: azcourts.gov Prop 207 — not directly opened, Verify. Forms: AOC CREM1I-071221 (instructions), CREM2F-071221 (petition) — Mohave Courts PDF copy found in search: Mohave Courts expungement instructions (PDF) — search result only, Verify against azcourts.gov. What DPS does / does not do. DPS clears/updates criminal-history records on court order; it does not decide eligibility, license establishments, or administer the cannabis programme. Expungement counts / DPS processing totals: not found in this session — Unavailable. Do not invent a number. County practice example in secondary guide (Pima County Attorney filing petitions on behalf of eligible applicants via web form) is secondary only, Verify.

7. Arizona State Board of Pharmacy — pharmacy-count denominator ONLY

Authority. §32-1904 (board composition and powers); §32-1929 (biennial registration of pharmacies, wholesalers, third-party logistics providers, manufacturers). §32-1929 (Justia, search result): “Board shall require/provide biennial registration of every pharmacy…” Source: Justia, §32-1929 — Verify against azleg.gov. Composition (secondary summaries of §32-1904, Verify). 9 members: 6 pharmacists (at least one hospital-employed, at least one community-pharmacy-employed; pharmacist members licensed ≥10 years in any jurisdiction and ≥5 years immediately preceding appointment in Arizona), 1 pharmacy technician, 2 public members. §32-1905 requires at least four meetings per fiscal year and an annual written report to the Governor and Arizona Pharmacy Association listing authorised pharmacies etc. — that annual report is the logical route to a current count. Cannabis role — strictly the denominator. §36-2854(A)(1)(b) (directly opened): “The department may not issue more than one marijuana establishment license for every ten pharmacies that have registered under section 32-1929, that have obtained a pharmacy permit from the Arizona board of pharmacy and that operate within this state.” The Board does not license marijuana establishments, dispensaries, or testing facilities. Do not overstate its role. Current registered-pharmacy count: Unavailable from sources opened in this session. The only number seen is secondary and rejected: an IndicaOnline 2026 guide claim that the ratio “puts the ceiling somewhere around 143 standard licenses, plus 26 social equity licenses and 12 rural county licenses” — the 12 rural figure conflicts with Run 1’s contemporaneous 13 rural licences and the ~143 figure states no denominator. Re-check the Board annual report / ADHS allocation notice; do not publish a pharmacy count or a licence ceiling derived from it.

8. Local zoning / planning authorities — summary cross-reference to Part 3 (do not redo Part 3)

Authority under §36-2857 (Run 1, verbatim in Run 1): a locality may ban establishments/testing, cap, zone, regulate time/place/manner and signage, and prohibit/restrict delivery; a county only in unincorporated areas; it may not be more restrictive than comparable medical rules, may not interfere with dual-licensee co-location, may not block transport on public roads, and may not allow an establishment within 500 ft of a public/private school, childcare, or preschool. Recurring pattern (Run 1): many cities prohibit standalone adult-use except a dual licensee at a shared location. No official statewide ban/allow list was located in Run 1 or this session. The Flagstaff 500-ft daycare dispute is now decided on standing grounds — see RCH Wellness below; it does not change §36-2857.

9. USDA Agricultural Marketing Service (AMS) — state/tribal hemp plan approval

Authority. Agriculture Improvement Act of 2018 (2018 Farm Bill); USDA Domestic Hemp Production Program, interim final rule 31 October 2019, final rule effective 22 March 2021. Once approved, a plan remains in effect unless withdrawn or revoked, or substantively revised and resubmitted. Directly opened list (this session). USDA AMS, “List of USDA-approved Hemp Plans,” stated “Updated February 17, 2026”: Arizona state plan = “AZ Approved Plan (pdf)*” (* = approved under the final rule effective 22 March 2021). Source: USDA AMS, state and tribal plan review — directly opened 31 October 2026. USDA does not license Arizona marijuana establishments, regulate Arizona retail THC products, or approve tribal cannabis (marijuana) programmes; its tribal approvals here are hemp-production plans only. See Part 10 for the tribal-plan correction.

10. Testing-accreditation bodies

What Run 1 established (secondary summaries, Verify). SB 1494 (2019) required ADHS-certified independent third-party laboratories with accreditation by a national/international accreditation association or similar entity; secondary summaries name A2LA, PJLA, ANAB, IAS, COLA as accreditors in this space. Which bodies ADHS currently recognises was not confirmed against an ADHS list in this session — Verify. Do not publish an ADHS-approved accreditor list from secondary summaries alone. ADHS certifies the laboratories; the accreditors do not license them. Lab-count conflict carried from Run 1: ADHS July 2026 report says 8 labs; CannBus Q3 2026 says 7 certified labs — unresolved, do not pick one silently.
