SB 1494 (2019) — testing, in detail

Per the azleg House Bill Summary (transmitted version, search result only — SB 1494 House summary) and contemporaneous reporting: established an order of priority for medical dispensary registration certificates; required independent third-party laboratories to be certified by ADHS, with application/renewal fees, reporting and health/safety requirements, no direct/indirect familial or financial relationship with a dispensary/related entity/management company or with a designated caregiver for whom the lab tests, a quality assurance programme, and accurate/precise/scientifically valid results procedures, plus accreditation by a national/international accreditation association or similar entity; created the Medical Marijuana Testing Advisory Council (membership in the summary includes an Arizona-based nonprofit dispensary concentrates producer with ≥3 years extraction experience, an Arizona cannabis testing laboratory owner, a laboratory scientist with doctorate or B.S. and ≥3 years cannabis lab testing experience, a registered qualifying patient, a registered designated caregiver, a Department of Public Safety representative, and a licensed health care provider specialising in substance use disorders with ≥5 years experience, among others); mandatory testing by 1 November 2020 for microbial contamination, heavy metals, pesticides, herbicides, fungicides, growth regulators, residual solvents, and potency confirmation; extended medical card validity from one year to two years (annualised cost halved at the time: $150 standard / $75 SNAP; physician certification fees $50–$150 separately, per 2019 reporting). Verify: exact chapter number and three-fourths vote counts (Snell & Wilmer reporting describes unanimous / veto-proof support; the summary notes voter-approved AMMA revisions require three-fourths of each chamber — vote counts not confirmed in this session). Secondary testing detail (ArizonaStateCannabis, undated in extract, Verify against A.A.C. Title 9, Chapter 17 testing rules): Salmonella — no remediation if detectable; E. coli — ≤100 CFU/g passes, >100 CFU/g fails, must be remediated/reprocessed and retested; inhaled products — Aspergillus flavus, niger, fumigatus, terreus by molecular methods, ≤1 CFU passes, >1 CFU triggers mycotoxin testing, ≥20 µg/kg mycotoxins fails and cannot be remediated. Secondary 2022 Council figure: 294 citations to 87 dispensary licensees involving 91 cases of testing deficiencies — Verify against Council/ADHS records. As of 15 September 2021, 16 certified independent testing laboratories, 5 with approval for the full scope of seven test parameters (secondary — do not use as a 2026 count).

9(b) Court cases — holdings ONLY as far as read; search-only holdings are labelled as such

1. Dobson v. McClennen, 238 Ariz. 389, 361 P.3d 374 (Ariz. 2015) — DUI metabolite. Facts as summarised in secondary case notes (full opinion not directly opened): Kristina Dobson (Oregon card) and Marvelle Anderson (Arizona card) were charged under §28-1381(A)(1) (impaired to the slightest degree) and (A)(3) (any drug defined in §13-3401 or its metabolite in the body). (A)(1) charges were dismissed; they were convicted on stipulated records under (A)(3). Superior Court affirmed; Court of Appeals denied special-action relief. Holding as summarised: AMMA does not immunise a registered qualifying patient from an (A)(3) prosecution, but provides an affirmative defence under §36-2802(D) if the patient proves by a preponderance that use was AMMA-authorised (subject to the §36-2811(A) rebuttable presumption) and that the marijuana/metabolite concentration was insufficient to cause impairment. Convictions affirmed; Court of Appeals opinion vacated. The defence applies to (A)(3) only, not to (A)(1) slightest-degree impairment, which remains a jury question. Sources: AZ App blog, Dobson; Neff Sedacca, Dobson — secondary case notes only; open the opinion before quoting its holding as directly read. No per-se THC limit in Arizona (Run 1).

2. Whitmire v. Wal-Mart Stores Inc., 359 F. Supp. 3d 761, 774 (D. Ariz. 2019) — employment. Citation/status Verify. Facts as reported: Carol Whitmire, Walmart employee since 2008 (Cashier, then Customer Service Supervisor), registered medical marijuana patient; workplace injury 21 May 2016 (bag of ice fell on wrist); post-injury drug test showed marijuana metabolites at the highest level the test could record; suspended then terminated. Holding as reported: AMMA §36-2813(B) creates an implied private cause of action because no other remedy was available; Walmart discriminated in violation of AMMA by terminating solely on the positive screen without expert evidence she used, possessed, or was impaired by marijuana on the premises/during hours; high metabolite levels alone are insufficient to show impairment; Walmart’s DTEA §23-493.06 good-faith-belief defence failed on that record. The court also noted a registered qualifying patient shall not be considered under the influence solely because metabolites/components appear in insufficient concentration to cause impairment. Date/status discrepancy: one secondary source dates the decision to 2019, another describes a 2020 judgment/summary-judgment context — Verify against the district court order. Appeal/current status not confirmed — Verify. It is a federal district court decision, not appellate precedent. Source: Legal 500, Whitmire — secondary only.

3. White Mountain Health Center, Inc. v. Maricopa County, 241 Ariz. 230, 386 P.3d 416 (Ariz. App. 2016), Nos. 1 CA-CV 12-0831 / 13-0697 / 14-0372 (consolidated), filed 20 December 2016, Presiding Judge Kessler, joined by Judge Swann and Chief Judge Brown, appeal from Maricopa County Superior Court No. CV2012-053585, Judge Michael D. Gordon — zoning / federal preemption. White Mountain sought county zoning approval in 2012 for a medical marijuana dispensary under AMMA §§36-2801 to -2819. Maricopa County refused zoning documentation, arguing CSA preemption and risk of federal prosecution for county employees. Holdings as summarised in Osborn Maledon / Justia material (opinion not directly opened): (1) CSA does not preempt the AMMA provisions at issue — no express preemption, no field preemption (agreed), no obstacle preemption (following Reed-Kaliher v. Hoggatt, 237 Ariz. 119 (2015)), no impossibility preemption (citing United States v. Rosenthal, 454 F.3d 943 (9th Cir. 2006) on official immunity); (2) trial court properly struck the Maricopa County Zoning Ordinance “Second Text Amendment” as applied to medical marijuana dispensaries because, although it purported to allow dispensaries in limited areas, its “poison pill” prohibiting any act in violation of federal law effectively banned all dispensaries, and AMMA allows only “reasonable zoning regulation that limit [dispensaries] to specified areas,” not a complete ban. Affirmed in part, reversed in part. The attorneys’-fees / §12-349 sanction portion disposition was truncated in search — Verify before stating the sanction outcome. Source: Justia, White Mountain — search result only.

4. Saguaro Healing LLC v. State, 249 Ariz. 362 (2020), No. CV-19-0129-PR, filed 20 August 2020, Justice Beene — county floor. ADHS announced 16 June 2016 it could issue new certificates and would accept applications 18–29 July 2016. Every county had a dispensary at annual review, so ADHS planned to use only the CHAA secondary criterion. During the application period, La Paz County’s only dispensary relocated out of the county. Saguaro Healing LLC, a qualified La Paz applicant, was denied because its CHAA priority was insufficient. Holding as summarised (opinion not directly opened): §36-2804(C) requires ADHS to issue at least one medical marijuana dispensary registration certificate in each county with a qualified applicant; ADHS’s interpretation of R9-17-303 contrary to that mandate violates §36-2804(C). ADHS must prioritise an approved application from a county without a dispensary during the application process, even if it had a dispensary at the start of the process, and must open the application process whenever there is a county without a dispensary during annual review. Court of Appeals decision vacated (No. 1 CA-CV 18-0242, filed 28 March 2019); trial court dismissal reversed and remanded; attorney fees granted to Saguaro. Justice Montgomery dissented on granting review. Source: Justia, Saguaro Healing — search result only. Follow-up, Court of Appeals Division One, Saguaro v. Hon. Bachus / State, No. 1 CA-SA 23-0066, filed 13 July 2023: superior court had remanded to ADHS to issue a certificate consistent with the Supreme Court opinion under statutes/rules in effect on 6 October 2016, considering La Paz applications from 18–29 July 2016; in February 2022 ADHS filed a notice of compliance stating it had awarded the La Paz certificate to an applicant other than Saguaro. Source: Justia, Saguaro 2023 — search result truncated; do not state the awardee or final outcome without opening the full 2023 opinion.

5. Acre 41 Enterprises LLC et al. v. State of Arizona et al., Maricopa County Superior Court No. CV2021-017808, Judge Randall H. Warner — social-equity dismissal, 1 February 2022. Filed November 2021 by Greater Phoenix Urban League and Acre 41, challenging ADHS’s social-equity ownership programme rules for the 26 licences (argument: the rules reduced equity licences to “lottery tickets” and allowed multi-state operators to bankroll/control qualified applicants). Order (court PDF hosted by MJBizDaily, found in search): “Because the rules satisfy the broad requirements of the statute that mandates them, the Motion to Dismiss is granted. The Application for Preliminary Injunction, which Plaintiffs asked to be treated as a motion for judgment on the pleadings, is denied.” Warner’s reasoning as quoted in contemporaneous reporting: “The voters left to ADHS how the social equity ownership program should work, and the rules [the agency] developed comply with the law and are reasonably designed to meet its objectives.” Plaintiffs vowed a further suit focused on specific applicants; whether any second suit was filed and its outcome was not confirmed in this session — Verify. Appeal status: Verify. Sources: Acre 41 dismissal order (PDF); MJBizDaily, Acre 41 dismissal — search results only; the order itself was not directly opened.

6. RCH Wellness Center v. City of Flagstaff, Arizona Court of Appeals Division One No. 1 CA-CV 25-0557, filed 30 April 2026 — DISPOSITION NOW RESOLVED (corrects Run 1’s “Verify” flag). RCH operates Noble Herb, a licensed Flagstaff dispensary since 2021. In February 2024 Yavapai Herbal Services, Inc. d/b/a Nirvana Center submitted a concept-plan application for a new Flagstaff dispensary. Flagstaff Zoning Code requires a minimum 500-ft separation from child daycare centers; Nirvana’s application contained an affidavit stating the location met the separation requirements. The dispute turned on the City/Board of Adjustment’s interpretation of when separation is measured (concept-plan submission vs certificate of occupancy) and on RCH’s standing. Superior Court (Coconino County No. S0300CV202400597, Judge Pro Tempore Brent Davidson Harris) dismissed RCH’s claims for lack of standing. Court of Appeals, Presiding Judge Daniel J. Kiley (joined by Judge D. Steven Williams and Judge Cynthia J. Bailey): AFFIRMED. The only injury RCH claimed was the threatened loss of its dominant market position from Board approval of a competitor. A related FLG Daycare suit, as far as the record in the opinion extract shows, remained pending. This decision resolves RCH’s standing appeal; it is not a merits ruling that the 500-ft measurement interpretation was correct, and it does not decide the daycare’s separate suit — do not cite it as a winner on the 500-ft timing question. AUDIT CORRECTION: delete the “case closed 4 June 2026” docket claim (search-result only, not verified from the azcourts docket). RCH was affirmed on standing only, 30 April 2026, and is not a merits ruling on the 500-ft measurement-timing interpretation. Sources: Justia, RCH Wellness (PDF); azcourts docket, RCH (PDF) — search results only; open the full opinion before quoting further holdings. A related 2026 Division Two decision, RAOF Management Co. v. Town of Springerville, No. 2 CA-CV 2025-0066 (filed 6 April 2026, unpublished, no precedent), concerning a Springerville dispensary business-licence denial and a 200-ft park setback, was noted in search but is outside the tasked case list and is not relied on here.

7. Vasquez / Juicy Joint — Maricopa County Superior Court, Judge Christopher Coury, 4 October 2024 — declined to void/reassign. Anavel Vasquez, qualified as majority owner for a social-equity application, worked with investors Helping Handz, LLC and Investing in the Future, LLC, which paid the $4,000 application fee and handled logistics in return for a minority stake in Juicy Joint I LLC, with Vasquez to hold 51%. After award, Vasquez entered a side agreement transferring the licence to MENVAS22 LLC for $2.7 million and a 51% interest, without her original partners’ consent/authorisation. Arbitration expelled Vasquez from Juicy Joint for breaching contractual/fiduciary duties; ADHS restored the licence in Juicy Joint’s name. Coury’s 4 October 2024 ruling, as quoted in contemporaneous reporting: “…because Ms. Vasquez breached her contractual and fiduciary duties owed to one or more of the Non-State Defendants relative to the License, and because Ms. Vasquez’ knowledge was imputed to Menvas at the time of the breach, Menvas has no rights to the License”; “There is no genuine issue of material fact that AZDHS did not act arbitrarily or capriciously, and a writ of mandamus cannot issue based on these theories.” The ruling rejected Vasquez’s request to void the licence her former partners hold. A separate Vasquez claim that ADHS should never have granted the licence in the first place was reported as still pending in October 2024 — its later disposition was not confirmed in this session — Verify. Sources: MJBizDaily, Vasquez; Marijuana Herald, Vasquez — secondary reporting only; the ruling itself was not directly opened.

8. Cave / Sherri Dunn, LLC (Trulieve) / ADHS — settlement and dismissal, dates now resolved. Chain (secondary reporting + administrative decision, all search results): Sherri Dunn, LLC applied for a La Paz County medical dispensary certificate in 2016 and was denied because La Paz already had a dispensary; after Saguaro Healing litigation, judgment entered 18 November 2021 remanding for issuance under statutes/rules in effect 6 October 2016 considering 18–29 July 2016 La Paz applications; ADHS issued DRC to Sherri Dunn on 28 December 2021 (ALJ decision, Office of Administrative Hearings No. 2022-MMR-0160-DHS, Judge Tammy L. Eigenheer, findings of fact). Sherri Dunn’s dual-licence early application fell outside the 19 January–9 March 2021 window; ADHS initially rejected it, a rejection affirmed by an administrative law judge and then a superior court judge (2022–2023); ADHS then settled and issued the licence (reported February 2024 in Run 1 — exact settlement date Verify). Mason Cave (Arizona Wellness Center Springerville) sued ADHS, Governor Katie Hobbs, Chief of Staff Chad Campbell, and Sherri Dunn, LLC (filed 13 April 2025, Maricopa County Superior Court), alleging Gift Clause and ultra vires violations and political influence via lobbyist contact with Campbell. Maricopa County Superior Court Judge Joseph Kreamer, ruling from the bench (reported 14–15 October 2025), granted all motions to dismiss: Cave and his company lacked standing (no concrete harm / taxpayer claim); even if standing existed, the Gift Clause claim failed because professional/regulatory licences do not fall under its provisions and the language contains no explicit mention of regulatory licences; no ultra vires claim exists in Arizona law; settlements are a routine part of litigation. Appeal not ruled out by Cave’s counsel at the time — later appeal status Verify. Sources: Marijuana Herald, Cave dismissal; ALJ decision (PDF) — secondary reporting only; the dismissal order itself was not directly opened. Note: a separate 2023 unpublished Court of Appeals memorandum decision, Dunn v. State, No. 1 CA-CV 23-0067 (filed 7 September 2023), is a public-records dispute (Sherri Dunn seeking disclosure of November/December 2021 dispensary registration certificates under §36-2810(A); superior court ordered disclosure, affirmed, $12,316.50 fees + $387.27 costs) — do not conflate it with the licence-settlement case. Source: Justia, Dunn public-records

PART 10 — TRIBAL AND INTERSTATE

10(a) Tribal

Governing principle. Arizona’s tribes are sovereign governments; Arizona’s medical and adult-use cannabis laws do not automatically apply on tribal land, and a state licence does not authorise conduct on a reservation that the tribe prohibits. Federal law (CSA) also applies on tribal land, subject to federal enforcement discretion. Each tribe decides independently. No tribal dispensary is listed here from any commercial directory, per task rules.

The “22 tribes” enumeration — flag. The Governor’s Office and Arizona Department of Education confirm “22 federally-recognised Tribes in Arizona” (e.g. Governor’s Office, June 2026; AZED, 22 tribes). The EPA Region 9 Arizona list opened in search names 21 (Ak-Chin, Cocopah, Colorado River, Fort McDowell Yavapai, Fort Mojave, Gila River, Havasupai, Hopi, Hualapai, Kaibab Band of Paiute, Navajo Nation, Pascua Yaqui, Quechan, Salt River Pima-Maricopa, San Carlos Apache, San Juan Southern Paiute, Tohono O’odham, Tonto Apache, White Mountain Apache, Yavapai-Apache, Yavapai-Prescott — EPA Region 9 tribes). The 22nd name was not confirmed from an opened authoritative list in this session — Verify the certified 22 against the AZED/Governor’s Office list before publishing an enumeration. The table below therefore gives a position only where a tribal-government primary source or official record was found, and marks every other tribe as “no tribal cannabis position located in a tribal primary source” — it does not claim the grouped row is a certified enumeration.

Tribal-state compacts: none found — stated as a finding, not a certified fact. A third-party summary states “No tribal-state cannabis compacts exist in Arizona” (Cannabis in Arizona, tribal cannabis, last verified March 2026) and an ASU Corporate & Business Law Journal commentary (September 2024/2025) states: “To date, there exists no formal record of negotiations of a similar marijuana compact between the State of Arizona and any of the local Tribes” (contrasting Michigan, Nevada, and Washington compact statutes). Sources: ASU CABLJ commentary; ASU CABLJ (PDF) — both secondary. No compact was found in Governor’s Office, ADHS, AG, or tribal-government sources opened in this session. Per task rules, report “none found,” NOT “none exist” as a certified fact.

State-licensed vs tribal-licensed programmes: none found. No tribe was found in this session operating a state-licensed medical or adult-use programme, a tribal-licensed commercial dispensary, or a tribal-licensed cultivation/manufacturing programme for marijuana. The only tribal commercial-cannabis-adjacent activity found is hemp production under USDA-approved tribal hemp plans (below) and personal-use regimes (SRPMIC, GRIC). If any tribe operates a programme not found here, it is Unavailable in this research, not disproven.

1. Salt River Pima-Maricopa Indian Community (SRPMIC) — personal possession/use legalised; commercial sale/production prohibited; no tribal dispensary

Tribal-government primary source: SRPMIC Office of the General Counsel / Community website marijuana FAQs and 2023 guidelines. FAQ: “When was the legalization of medical marijuana enacted: October 19, 2022. When was the legalization of recreational marijuana enacted: Approved on March 29, 2023 with an enactment date of May 1, 2023. Are the laws surrounding personal possession and use of marijuana consistent with the State Laws of Arizona: Yes. Is personal cultivation of marijuana allowed within the SRPMIC: Yes under certain conditions and guidelines. Is the commercial growing, manufacturing or selling of marijuana allowed in the SRPMIC: No. At this time, the marijuana business industry aspects are not permitted within the SRPMIC.” Recreational: 21+, ≤1 oz (≤5 g concentrate). Medical: 18+, ≤2.5 oz (≤12.5 g concentrate), valid unexpired Arizona registry identification card required. “Marijuana must be purchased by a marijuana establishment (dispensary) licensed by the State of Arizona” — i.e. from off-reservation state licensees; use prohibited in any public place, Community building/facility, or at any Community event. Over-limit possession: Class C offence, max 6 months jail and/or $1,000 fine. Source: SRPMIC Marijuana FAQs (PDF) — search result only; open the live SRPMIC page and the Code §6-124 text before quoting.

2023 guidelines (same tribal source): Code of Ordinances §6-124(a)(1) recreational, (a)(2) medical, (a)(3) personal cultivation (21+, primary residence, ≤6 plants per individual / ≤12 per residence where ≥2 adults 21+ reside, enclosed locked area not visible from public view without optical aids, manual/mechanical processing only, no chemical extraction/synthesis, product of plants grown on the premises). Source: SRPMIC Guidelines (PDF) — search result only.

Sale/manufacture prohibition clarified by tribal ordinance: Ordinance SRO-576-2024, adopted 1 May 2024 (9 for, 0 opposed), amending Code §6-128 “to clarify that the sale of marijuana is prohibited in any quantity within the exterior boundaries of the Community,” including sale, transfer, transport for sale, import for sale, or possession with intent to sell/transfer/transport for sale/import for sale. Source: SRPMIC Ordinance SRO-576-2024 (PDF) — search result only.

CONFLICT FLAGGED against Run 1 / third-party summary. Run 1’s third-party summary said SRPMIC “legalised adult possession in 2022 by a 4–3 tribal council vote.” The tribal FAQ says medical 19 October 2022 and recreational approved 29 March 2023, enacted 1 May 2023. Do not merge these into a single “2022 legalisation” date; the council vote count (4–3), the 2022 vs 2023 dates, and which action legalised what must be verified against the SRPMIC Council minutes/ordinance. The consistent core across sources is: personal possession/use is now permitted within SRPMIC limits, commercial sale is prohibited, and no tribal dispensary has opened. Third-party summary (last verified March 2026) also states no tribal dispensary as of March 2026: Cannabis in Arizona, tribal cannabis — no tribal dispensary opening between March and 31 October 2026 was confirmed in this session — Verify.

Historical note (secondary, pre-legalisation): SRPMIC previously stated Arizona medical cards “don’t apply” on tribal lands, including the Loop 101 strip, and enforced tribal/federal drug law there (U.S. Attorney letter to ADHS, 2 May 2011, cited in tribal statement). Source: The Weed Blog, SRPMIC (pre-2022) — secondary and pre-dates the 2022/2023 SRPMIC regime; do not use it to describe current SRPMIC law.

2. Gila River Indian Community (GRIC) — CORRECTION to Run 1’s “prohibits” summary: personal adult-use cultivation is now permitted by tribal permit; commercial position Verify

Tribal-government primary source: GRIC Department of Land Use Planning & Zoning (LUPZ) Adult Use Marijuana Cultivation Permit packet: applications accepted starting 1 March 2023 by appointment only (not for sites on allotted lands); only enrolled Community members may apply; 6 plants max per homesite; $50 fee per plant; permit valid one year (365 days) on signature of the LUPZ Director; cultivation in an enclosed area (closet, room, greenhouse or other enclosed area); applicant must complete a GRIC Adult Use Marijuana Cultivation Class; cultivation tags issued on completion. Source: GRIC LUPZ permit packet — search result only; open the live GRIC LUPZ page before quoting. The GRIC LUPZ Subdivision Administration page still links an “Adult Use Marijuana Cultivation Permit Application Packet with Guidelines”: GRIC LUPZ, Subdivision Administration — search result only.

History: GRIC News, 22 February 2022 (tribal newspaper): after Prop 207, the Office of General Counsel presented a memorandum to Council in December 2020; Council referred it to the Legislative Standing Committee; draft Title 5 revisions approved 9 November 2021 and presented for public comment 5 February 2022 would have allowed medical and recreational marijuana “in conformance with Arizona law” (adults 21+: ≤1 oz / ≤5 g concentrate; ≤6 plants per homesite, enclosed, not accessible/visible to the public, registered with LUPZ). The article states: “Until the proposed revisions to GRIC Code Title 5 are approved by the Community Council, marijuana remains illegal in the Community.” Source: GRIC News, 22 Feb 2022 — search result only.

Correction: the existence of a 2023 tribal permit process for enrolled members’ personal cultivation is inconsistent with a flat present-tense statement that “Gila River prohibits” all marijuana. What is established is: GRIC operates a tribal personal-cultivation permit regime from March 2023; whether the full Title 5 decriminalisation (possession/use by non-members, medical use, commercial sale) was enacted, and the current text of GRIC Code Title 5, were NOT confirmed in this session — Verify against the current GRIC Code. The ASU commentary’s statement that GRIC “flirted with legalization in 2022 but ultimately decided against it on a recreational level” (ASU CABLJ) is superseded in part by the tribal permit packet and must not be quoted without the permit qualification. GRIC’s earlier opposition to dispensary placement near its borders (asking Pinal County supervisors not to locate dispensaries within a mile of its borders, secondary reporting) concerns off-reservation siting, not the tribal personal-cultivation regime — do not conflate.

3. Navajo Nation — strict prohibition (tribal code, via official tribal DOJ presentation)

Navajo Nation Department of Justice cannabis overview (presented by Deputy Attorney General Kimberly A. Dutcher, New Mexico Legislature handout, 3–4 August 2021): “Under Navajo Law, Cannabis is Illegal.” Resolution No. CO-75-18 amended Title 17 of the Navajo Criminal Code to define “marijuana” as the plant Cannabis sativa L. with a THC level of more than 0.3% dry weight, and emphasised the resolution did “not authorize the cultivation, growth, possession, development or propagation of industrial hemp until the Navajo Nation creates a regulatory system for industrial hemp and obtains the necessary and applicable permits.” Resolution No. CJN-24-19 authorised a hemp pilot research project (Native American Products / Navajo Agricultural Products Industry with New Mexico State University) while reiterating industrial hemp remained illegal. Source: Navajo Nation DOJ overview (PDF) — tribal DOJ presentation hosted by a state legislature; the Navajo Code text itself was not directly opened — Verify against the current Navajo Nation Code Title 17.

2020 expansion (secondary, consistent): Navajo Nation Council amended the criminal code to include the entire cannabis plant including hemp, with civil forfeiture for cannabis offences and forfeiture of elected office/employment for officials convicted; President Jonathan Nez stated possessors/manufacturers/sellers “will be held accountable.” Source: Ganjapreneur, 7 Oct 2020 (updated 22 June 2025) — secondary; Verify resolution number/date against Council records. Enforcement example: Navajo Nation v. Dineh Benally, No. SR-CV-014-2020 (Shiprock District, Judge Genevieve Woody, temporary restraining order September 2020; ~400 acres near Shiprock) — secondary summary only.

Navajo hemp status: no USDA-approved Navajo hemp plan appears on the USDA list directly opened (see below); the 2019 pilot does not equal an approved plan. Do not list the Navajo Nation as having a USDA-approved hemp plan.

The Schedule III “state-licensed” question — Verify, and why it matters

Given federal baseline: state-licensed medical marijuana moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026; adult-use remains Schedule I; 280E generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity only. Question: whether a tribal-licensed medical programme (if one existed, or if a tribe licensed medical activity under tribal law) counts as “state-licensed” for the federal Schedule III / 280E purpose. Mark Verify — no federal guidance, IRS guidance, or DEA rule answering this question for tribal programmes was located in this session, and no Arizona tribe currently operates a tribal-licensed medical programme against which to test it. Why it matters for tribal operators: if tribal medical activity does not count as state-licensed, a tribal medical operator would remain Schedule I for federal purposes and fully subject to 280E (no deduction of ordinary/necessary business expenses), even though an Arizona state-licensed medical operator’s medical activity is Schedule III / 280E-relieved; dual-activity splitting (medical vs adult-use) that Arizona dual licensees must perform would have no tribal analogue unless the tribal programme is recognised. If it does count, tribal medical operators would share the 280E relief and the Schedule III handling, banking, and research consequences. The same question affects whether tribal hemp-plan holders’ hemp activity is affected at all — hemp (≤0.3% delta-9 / post-11 December 2026 total-THC definition) is already excluded from the CSA marijuana definition and is not the Schedule III question; do not conflate hemp plans with marijuana Schedule III status. Arizona’s own §43-108 decoupling (Run 1) is a state income-tax subtraction for marijuana establishments/testing facilities/for-profit dual licensees; whether it would extend to a tribal operator’s Arizona-source income is a separate state-tax question — Verify, do not assume.

10(b) Interstate

Arizona law on transporting marijuana across state lines

State criminal law (secondary summaries, Verify against §§13-3405, 36-2852). Arizona law allows possession/use/purchase only within Arizona’s licensed system. Transporting marijuana for sale is unlawful under §13-3405; possession not protected by Prop 207 / AMMA is a criminal offence; secondary law-firm summaries state transporting marijuana into Arizona from another state — even if legally purchased there — can result in charges under §13-3405, and that AMMA requires a cardholder to purchase from an Arizona-licensed dispensary, so out-of-state product cannot be brought in to satisfy AMMA. Sources: Kolsrud Law, traveling with marijuana; Jaburg Wilk, traveling with medical marijuana — both secondary law-firm summaries; open §§13-3405, 36-2804, 36-2852 before quoting elements or penalties. Do not state a specific trafficking penalty or “trafficking” classification from these summaries alone.

Federal law. Interstate transport of marijuana remains a federal offence (21 U.S.C. §841) regardless of both states’ laws; adult-use remains Schedule I federally (given baseline); state-licensed medical Schedule III status from 28 April 2026 does not create an interstate-commerce right — no federal interstate medical-commerce authorisation was located. Secondary summaries agree: “Your Arizona dispensary purchase becomes contraband the moment you cross into New Mexico, Nevada, or any other state” (driving, flying, bus, or mailing it to yourself); TSA officers do not specifically search for drugs but must report illegal substances discovered during screening. Sources: LegalClarity, Prop 207 rules; LegalClarity, AZ cannabis laws; Super Lawyers, selling across state lines — secondary only.

Federal-land / checkpoint overlay (secondary). Possession on federal land inside Arizona (national parks/forests, military installations) can be federally prosecuted regardless of amount; U.S. Border Patrol checkpoints on highways (including Interstate 8, per Jaburg Wilk secondary summary) inspect vehicles and seize marijuana under federal law. Source: Jaburg Wilk — secondary; Verify checkpoint practice against CBP guidance before publishing as current practice.

No Arizona interstate-commerce authorisation found. Arizona has no statute authorising export of marijuana to another state, no interstate cannabis compact, and no state-to-state reciprocity agreement for purchase (see below). Licensed activity is intrastate by design: §36-2857 prohibits localities from blocking transport on public roads within Arizona (Run 1), which is not an interstate right.

Hemp interstate commerce — 2018 Farm Bill and §3-320 affirmative defence (scope, with limits)

Federal hemp interstate commerce. Under the 2018 Farm Bill, hemp (as federally defined) is not a controlled substance and states/tribes may not prohibit its interstate transportation/shipment — the standard federal position, stated here as background; the federal provision (7 U.S.C. §1639o note / §10114) was not directly opened in this session — Verify before quoting it. This does not authorise interstate shipment of marijuana, of hemp that exceeds the THC threshold, or — after 11 December 2026 under the given baseline — of product exceeding the full total-THC definition / 0.4 mg per container cap. No Arizona statute/rule responding to PL 119-37 / PL 119-103 was found as of 31 October 2026 (Run 1, carried forward).

Arizona §3-320 — text from Justia mirror (Verify against azleg.gov). §3-320 (2025), as reproduced:

“A. It is an affirmative defense to any prosecution for the possession or cultivation of marijuana pursuant to title 13, chapter 34 that the defendant is a licensee, or a designee or agent of a licensee, who is in compliance with this article. B. This section is not a defense to a charge of possession, sale, transportation or distribution of marijuana pursuant to title 13, chapter 34 that is not industrial hemp.”

Source: Justia, §3-320 — mirror, not azleg.gov; Verify current text.

Scope — read literally, do not over-read. Subsection A names possession or cultivation only; it does not name transportation, sale, or distribution as a defended charge. Subsection B withdraws the defence entirely for marijuana that is not industrial hemp. The broader transport protection for compliant hemp comes instead from §13-3405(I) as added by SB 1098 (Chapter 287, 2018, session-law text found in search): §13-3405 “DOES NOT APPLY TO” (1) a person licensed under Title 3, Chapter 2, Article 4.1 who possesses, uses, sells, produces, manufactures or transports industrial hemp as defined in §3-311, and (2) a person who engages in commercial production/processing/manufacturing/distribution/commerce of industrial hemp as defined in §3-311 in Arizona outside the pilot programme if authorised under federal law. Source: Laws 2018, Ch. 287 (SB 1098) — session-law text, search result only; Verify current §§13-3405, 3-311, 3-312, 3-314, 3-316–3-318 on azleg.gov. A proposed 2024 amendment (SB 1186, which would have added “narcotic drugs” language to §3-320 and a licensed “transporter” definition) is not law — do not cite it as current §3-320. Source for the proposal only: SB 1186 (2024), proposed.

Practical scope statement (flagged as interpretation, not statute text). On the texts above, a compliant Arizona hemp licensee (or designee/agent) transporting industrial hemp as defined in §3-311 has a statutory carve-out from §13-3405 and an affirmative defence for possession/cultivation; the §3-320 defence by its terms does not cover a transportation charge for non-hemp marijuana, and it does not authorise shipment into a state that prohibits that hemp product, nor shipment of product that fails the THC definition. Whether §3-320 covers interstate shipment (as opposed to intrastate possession/cultivation) is not stated in the section and was not resolved by any case found in this session — Verify; do not state that §3-320 “authorises interstate hemp shipment” without that qualification.

Visiting patients, out-of-state cards, and the out-of-state revocation bar

Visiting patients — established, do not contradict (Run 1). §36-2804.03(C): a visiting qualifying patient (valid card/equivalent from another U.S. medical programme) may possess and use marijuana within Arizona’s medical limits but is not authorised to obtain marijuana from a nonprofit medical dispensary. Secondary reciprocity matrix (May 2026) classifies Arizona as “possession-only recognition” for out-of-state cards: MMJ Now, reciprocity explained (May 2026) — secondary; the statute controls. Practical route for a visiting adult 21+: adult-use purchase from a licensed marijuana establishment (Run 1). Arizona does not operate a purchase-reciprocity system for out-of-state medical cards, and no Arizona reciprocity agreement with any state was found in this session — none found.

SRPMIC nuance (tribal, not state). SRPMIC’s own medical regime requires a valid unexpired Arizona registry identification card for medical use within SRPMIC (tribal FAQ, above) — that is a tribal condition for tribal-law medical use, not Arizona state reciprocity, and it does not authorise purchase inside SRPMIC (commercial sale prohibited there).

Out-of-state licence revocation bar — directly opened statute. §36-2854(A)(1)(h) (azleg.gov, §36-2854, opened in the Part 9–10 session):

“(h) The department may not issue a marijuana establishment or marijuana testing facility license to an applicant who has an ownership interest in an out-of-state marijuana establishment or marijuana testing facility, or the other state's equivalent, that has had its license revoked by the other state.”

Scope notes: the bar is on issuance to an applicant with an ownership interest in a revoked out-of-state establishment/facility (or equivalent); it is not stated as a bar on mere out-of-state licensure, on employment, or on a revoked individual card; whether a revocation later reinstated, or a revocation for a non-cannabis reason, lifts the bar is not stated in the subsection — Verify against ADHS licensing rules / practice before advising on any individual case. The 2025/2026 bills that would have re-lettered this paragraph (SB 1713 (2025), SB 1057 (2025), SB 1641 (2026)) did not change the operative bar in the current compilation — SB 1641 is dead at sine die (above); SB 1713/SB 1057 final statuses Verify on azleg.gov before citing them.

PART 11 — OPERATIONS RULES (for licensees)

Corrections/upgrades to the prior session’s open items, established in this session: the $10,000 delivery trip cap is now confirmed in rule text — R9-18-312.01(C)(3) — not secondary; the 5-year retention figure is now confirmed for inventory documentation — R9-18-314(E)(1) — but it is not a blanket rule: other record classes have different periods (see record-types table); the inventory audit interval is 30 calendar days, not 3 months — R9-18-314(D); total CBD formula: Cornell’s R9-18-408(F)(3)(b)(ii) as directly opened reads CBDA × 0.877 + CBD, conflicting with the earlier Justia extract’s 0.8 — Verify against the official AAC before publication; the $20,000 advertising penalty in §36-2859(K) applies to an individual/entity other than a marijuana establishment or nonprofit medical dispensary; establishments/dispensaries face disciplinary action under §36-2854(B)/§36-2816 instead. Run 1’s shorthand “$20,000/violation” for advertising should be corrected to this split.

11.1 Tracking: the no-Metrc position re-confirmed

§36-2854(A)(4) requires the licensee to “procure, develop, acquire and maintain a system to track marijuana and marijuana products at all points of cultivation, manufacturing and sale,” with accurate accounting/reporting, tracking of propagation, processing, licensee-to-licensee sale/purchase, transfer between licensed premises, disposal of marijuana waste, and the identity of the person making each entry plus time, date, location and any corrections — and a “transactional stamp to ensure accuracy, provide for chain of custody… and foreclose tampering.” No state-contracted platform is named. Source: azleg.gov, §36-2854 — accessed 31 Oct 2026. R9-18-314 requires the establishment to “establish and implement an inventory control system” of its own, with a designated marijuana facility agent in writing having oversight (R9-18-314(A)). No rule opened names Metrc, BioTrack, or any other vendor. Source: Cornell, R9-18-314 — accessed 31 Oct 2026. Conclusion: Arizona does NOT use Metrc or any single state platform — re-confirmed. The earlier CannBus/IndicaOnline claims to the contrary remain discarded. This closes Run 1 open item 2 (confirm the no-single-state-platform position against R9-18 inventory rules).

11.2 Security — R9-18-312 (directly opened)

Source for this section: Justia, R9-18-312 (stated current through Register Vol. 31, No. 25, 20 June 2025) — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

  • Cultivation siting: indoors, in a closed, locked room; outdoors, surrounded by solid 10-foot walls of metal, concrete, or stone that prevent viewing of the plants, with a one-inch thick metal gate — R9-18-312(A).
  • Access control: access to a cultivation site, manufacturing site, or the portion of a retail site where marijuana is cultivated, processed, manufactured, or stored is limited to the establishment’s principal officers, board members, and authorized marijuana facility agents, unless the individual is supervised by a marijuana facility agent associated with the establishment — R9-18-312(B).
  • Premises security equipment (R9-18-312(I)(1)): intrusion-detection devices (which may include a signal system interconnected by radio-frequency method such as cellular, private radio signals, or another mechanical/electronic device); exterior lighting to facilitate surveillance; electronic monitoring including at least one 19-inch or greater call-up monitor; a printer capable of immediately producing a clear still photo from any video camera image; video cameras covering all entrances to and exits from limited access areas and all entrances to and exits from the building, capable of identifying any activity occurring in or adjacent to the building, at recording resolution of at least 704 x 480 or equivalent; a video camera at each point-of-sale location allowing identification of any consumer purchasing marijuana or a marijuana product; a video camera in each grow room capable of identifying any activity within the grow room in low light conditions; storage of video recordings for at least 30 calendar days; a failure notification system providing audible and visual notification of any failure in the electronic monitoring system; sufficient battery backup for video cameras and recording equipment to support at least five minutes of recording in a power outage; and panic buttons in the interior of each building.
  • Security policies (R9-18-312(I)(2)): identification of authorized individuals; deterring unauthorized removal (including restricting access to retail cultivation/processing/storage areas and cultivation/manufacturing sites, and supervision of non-officer/board/agent individuals by an associated facility agent); preventing loitering; conducting electronic monitoring; and use of a panic button.
  • Testing-facility visitor rule: a marijuana facility agent must accompany any individual other than another associated facility agent when present in the area where marijuana/products are tested or stored for testing — R9-18-405(8). Source: Cornell, R9-18-405 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

11.3 Physical plant, sanitation, storage separation

Physical plant (R9-18-316, directly opened): licensed premises maintained free from hazards; on-site or adjacent parking for the retail site; at least one toilet room (flushable toilet, mounted tissue, sink with running water, soap dispenser, disposable single-use towels or air dryer); at least one hand-washing sink outside a toilet room with running water, soap, and towels/dryer; designated storage areas for marijuana or materials used in direct contact with marijuana, separate from storage areas for toxic or flammable materials; if preparation/packaging is done in the building, a designated area with sanitizable work space used only for preparation/packaging. Source: Cornell, R9-18-316 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

Commercial devices (R9-18-316(D)): for each commercial device used at a retail, cultivation, or manufacturing site, the establishment shall ensure the device is licensed or certified pursuant to A.R.S. §3-3451, maintain documentation of the licence/certification, and provide a copy to ADHS on request. §3-3451 text itself was not opened in this session — Verify its device classes. Same source.

Cleaning and sanitation (R9-18-315, directly opened): buildings/equipment used for cultivation, harvest, preparation, packaging, storage, infusion, or sale maintained clean and sanitary; marijuana/products protected from flies, dust, dirt, and all other contamination during production, preparation, manufacture, packing, storage, sale, distribution, or transportation; refuse/waste removed from retail/cultivation/manufacturing buildings at least once every 24 hours or more often as necessary; trucks, trays, buckets, receptacles, platforms, racks, tables, shelves, knives, saws, cleavers, utensils, and machinery cleaned daily; product-preparation equipment clean, in good repair, and calibrated per manufacturer recommendations if applicable; supplies including flammable/volatile chemicals stored to avoid a hazardous condition; all stored marijuana products securely covered. Agent hygiene: hand/arm washing in a hand-washing sink before preparing (including working with food, equipment, utensils), during preparation as often as necessary, after handling soiled equipment/utensils, after touching bare body parts other than clean hands/arms, and after using the toilet room; trimmed/filed cleanable nails, no polish/artificial nails unless wearing intact gloves in good repair, protective apparel (coats, aprons, gowns, or gloves) when working directly with preparation or infusion into non-edible products; clean clothing appropriate to tasks; report any health condition that may adversely affect safety/quality per policies, and exclusion from direct contact until the condition will not adversely affect the product. Source: Cornell, R9-18-315 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

Retail operations (R9-18-308(A), directly opened): retail site must be operating and available to provide marijuana/products to consumers at least 30 hours weekly between 7:00 a.m. and 10:00 p.m., and within 18 months after receiving the licence. The establishment must post, where visible to individuals entering the retail site: if applicable, its approval to operate; the establishment licence; a Department-provided-format sign reading verbatim: “WARNING: There may be potential dangers to fetuses caused by smoking or ingesting marijuana while pregnant or to infants while breastfeeding” and “WARNING: Use of marijuana during pregnancy may result in a risk of being reported to the Department of Child Safety during pregnancy or at the birth of the child by persons who are required to report”; and its hours of operation. Source: Cornell, R9-18-308 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

Volatile solvents: §36-2851(10) does not authorize a person to process/manufacture marijuana by means of any liquid or gas, other than alcohol, with a flashpoint below 100°F, unless performed by a marijuana establishment. Source: azleg.gov, §36-2851 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

11.4 Inventory control and recordkeeping — R9-18-314 (directly opened in full)

Source: Cornell, R9-18-314 (adopted 27 A.A.R. 111, eff. 15 Jan 2021; amended 29 A.A.R. 2453, eff. 1 Oct 2023; amended 30 A.A.R. 3451, eff. 1 Nov 2024) — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

  • Acquisition sources only: the establishment’s own cultivation/manufacturing site, another marijuana establishment, or a dispensary with a registration certificate under 9 A.A.C. 17 — R9-18-314(B).
  • Daily inventory record (R9-18-314(C)(1)): each day’s beginning inventory; acquisitions; marijuana harvested; marijuana/products provided to a dispensary or another establishment; sold; submitted to a testing facility under R9-18-311; disposed of; and the day’s ending inventory.
  • Acquisition record (C)(2): description (amount, batch number, strain; ingredients in order of abundance for products; for edibles: manufacture date, total weight, estimated amount and batch number of marijuana/product infused); name and licence number (or registry number) of the providing establishment/dispensary; name and licence/registry number of the providing agent; name and licence number of the receiving agent; date of acquisition.
  • Batch cultivation record (C)(3): batch number; seeds vs cuttings origin; origin and strain of seeds/cuttings; number planted; planting date; list of all chemical additives including nonorganic pesticides, herbicides, and fertilizers; number of plants grown to maturity; harvest date, final yield weight of processed usable marijuana (as defined in §36-2801), and name/licence number of the agent responsible for the harvest.
  • Transfer record (C)(4) and testing-submission record (C)(5) mirror the acquisition fields, plus the testing facility’s name/registry number, receiving agent, and submission date.
  • Disposal record (C)(6): description of and reason for disposal, including if applicable the number of failed or other unusable plants and laboratory testing results; date of disposal; method of disposal; name and licence number of the facility agent responsible for disposal.
  • Audit (D): the designated individual shall conduct and document an audit of inventory accounted for according to generally accepted accounting principles at least once every 30 calendar days. If the audit identifies a reduction not due to documented causes, the establishment shall determine and document where the loss occurred and take and document corrective action. If the reduction is due to suspected criminal activity by a facility agent, the establishment shall report the agent to the Department and to local law enforcement.
  • Retention (E): maintain documentation required in (C) and (D) at the establishment for at least five years after the date on the document, and provide it to the Department on request. (This resolves the prior session’s retention Verify for inventory records.)
  • Loss/theft (R9-18-308(A)(12)): document and report any loss or theft from the retail, cultivation, or manufacturing site to the appropriate law enforcement agency. Same R9-18-308 source as above.

11.5 Waste disposal (adult-use rule text now directly opened)

  • Establishment disposal policies (R9-18-308(A)(2)(f), directly opened): policies must cover disposal including: (i) destroying a batch that does not meet Table 3.1 and documenting the destruction; (ii) submitting marijuana that is not usable marijuana (as defined in §36-2801) to a local law enforcement agency and documenting the submission; or (iii) otherwise disposing of marijuana/product such that it is unrecognizable or cannot otherwise be used, documenting the method of disposal, the facility agent overseeing disposal, and the date of disposal. Source: Cornell, R9-18-308 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • Testing-facility disposal (R9-18-405(5)(c)(vii)): dispose such that the product is unrecognizable or cannot otherwise be used, documenting method, whether tested, if not tested the reason, overseeing agent, and date. Same R9-18-405 source.
  • Statutory hook: §36-2854(A)(4)(c)(v) requires the tracking system to track disposal of marijuana waste. Source: azleg.gov, §36-2854 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

11.6 Testing

Batch, sampling, and failed-test rules — R9-18-311 (directly opened in the prior session; Cornell LII). Source: Cornell, R9-18-311 — accessed 31 Oct 2026 (prior-session open; re-cited, not re-opened this session).

  • Before offering a batch for sale/transfer, the establishment shall ensure each batch of marijuana is tested per R9-18-408 and Table 3.1, except: (a) a product prepared from another compliant product (e.g., concentrate or tincture) using none of: a temperature above which any analyte could decompose/react, a pressure above which any analyte could decompose/react, a process by which any compliant analyte may be further concentrated, or a solvent other than water — then at least potency and microbial contaminants other than mycotoxins; or (b) all analytes except ethanol if the product is intended to contain ethanol, or isopropanol for a topical intended to contain isopropanol.
  • Reduced heavy-metals frequency: if heavy-metals testing shows Table 3.1 compliance for at least six consecutive months, each batch is then tested for all analytes except heavy metals, with heavy-metals testing at least once every three months. Verify: the Cornell text cites “R9-17-408” in (A)(3)(b) — apparent cross-reference error or variant.
  • Until testing is completed and compliant results received, a batch must be stored away from marijuana/products offered for sale or transfer.
  • Sampling: only one sample per batch (except as in (D)), collected per ANSI/ASQ Standard Z1.4 (2018), General Inspection Level II (incorporated by reference, no future editions); methods include top/middle/bottom sampling with a sample thief, star-pattern sampling from top/middle/bottom of each container, discrete incremental units (e.g., every 10th unit or every 20th drop), or quartering; aggregated portions are quartered to obtain the specified sample size. Sample size must suffice for testing and, if necessary, retesting. Sample packaged in the same material as for sale/transfer or another non-reactive, non-leaching material, labelled with licence number, amount, strain, batch number, analytes requested, storage temperature, and sampling date, and submitted to an ADHS-licensed testing facility approved by ADHS for each requested analyte. Only batches with compliant results may be offered for sale/transfer. Any noncompliant batch is remediated, if applicable, or destroyed per policies/procedures.
  • Retest (R9-18-311(C)): on a final testing report under R9-18-410(B)(3) indicating noncompliance, within seven days the establishment may request retesting of the remaining portion of the sample for all noncompliant analytes by no more than two other ADHS-approved facilities independent of the facility that conducted the test in the final report. If a retest report from another independent facility indicates any tested analyte does not comply, the establishment shall remediate, if applicable, or destroy the batch. If final reports from each of the two other independent facilities indicate all tested analytes comply, the batch may be offered for sale/transfer. Do not rely on struck-through Cornell text about a third independent facility retest.
  • Second-sample retest (R9-18-311(D)): permitted if the batch is still in the establishment’s possession; it receives notification from ADHS, another establishment, or a dispensary that the final report may be inaccurate; if the notification is from another establishment/dispensary, it informs ADHS the report may be inaccurate; it collects the second sample per (B)(2)–(3), packages/labels per (B)(4)–(5), submits to a second independent ADHS-approved facility, and follows (C)(1)–(3).
  • Remediation: per policies/procedures, using a method appropriate to the noncompliant analyte, not introducing/producing a substance in a concentration known to be harmful to humans; a remediated batch must be submitted for testing per (B). The establishment shall provide ADHS on request a sample of its inventory sufficient for ADHS analysis.

Method, reporting, and turnaround — R9-18-408 and R9-18-410 (directly opened this session). Sources: Cornell, R9-18-408 and Cornell, R9-18-410 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

  • R9-18-408(A)–(B): definitions in R9-17-404.03(A) apply; the technical laboratory director shall ensure compliance with R9-17-404.03(B)–(O) when using chemical analytical methods for any Table 3.1 analyte.
  • Reporting units (R9-18-408(F)): pesticides, fungicides, growth regulators, heavy metals, residual solvents in ppm; mycotoxins per R9-17-404.04(I)(4); potency in percent (w/w) relative to bulk plant material/product or mg per designated unit; total THC = THCA × 0.877 + delta-9-THC; total CBD = CBDA × 0.877 + CBD (Cornell text; conflicts with prior 0.8 extract — Verify).
  • Microbial reporting (R9-18-408(G)–(J)): methods per R9-17-404.04(A)(1), validated per (A)(2), complying with (A)(3)–(4) and (B)–(G); E. coli in CFU/g; Salmonella reported “Detected” or “Not detected” in one gram; Aspergillus reported “Detected” or “Not detected” in one gram; mycotoxin reporting includes total aflatoxins in µg/kg and ochratoxin A in µg/kg.
  • Data qualifiers (R9-18-408(C)–(D),(I)): B1, B2, D1, I1, L1, M1–M5, N1, R1, R2, V1, Q1, Q2, and Q3 — Q3 means the result is for informational purposes only and cannot be used to satisfy R9-18-311(A) testing or R9-18-310 labelling requirements.
  • Turnaround (R9-18-410(A)(1),(E)): samples analyzed as received within 10 calendar days after receipt (at the facility, or at another ADHS-approved facility for parameters/analytes the facility is not approved to conduct); final report sent to the establishment within 10 calendar days after receipt; inter-facility reports within 7 calendar days; retest reports under R9-18-311(C) within 7 calendar days.
  • Final report contents (R9-18-410(B)(3)): facility name/address/telephone, licence number, actual scientifically valid/defensible results in appropriate units per R9-18-408 and the quality assurance plan, method list, sample information (unique ID, colour picture as submitted, description incl. strain/batch, collection date/time, submitter name/number, changes since submission), date of testing for each parameter, date of final report, and technical laboratory director/designee signature. Testing records must also contain raw data, worksheets, calculations, QC data, and change history.
  • Accreditation (R9-18-405(2)): a testing-facility owner shall “maintain accreditation for each approved parameter and analyte.” The R9-18-101 definition of “accreditation” as being deemed technically competent under ISO 17025 by the American Association of Laboratory Accreditation (and others) appears in the 7 May 2021 Administrative Register extract — Verify the current definition text against the current AAC compilation. Sources: R9-18-405 above; Arizona Memory Project, Administrative Register extract (PDF) — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • Independence (statute + rule): §36-2854(A)(10) requires rules prohibiting a testing facility from having any direct or indirect familial relationship with or financial ownership interest in an establishment or related business entity/management company, and vice versa; §36-2854(N) states the same prohibition until such rules are adopted. R9-18-308(A)(7) requires an establishment to ensure no principal officer or board member has a direct or indirect familial or financial relationship with a testing facility, or had/has an ownership interest in a licensed marijuana business that had its licence revoked in another state. Sources: azleg.gov, §36-2854; R9-18-308 above — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • Medical testing: medical testing is under Title 9, Chapter 17 and SB 1494 (2019) (secondary: ArizonaStateCannabis, testing — accessed 31 Oct 2026). R9-18-408 incorporates R9-17-404.03/404.04 methods, so the adult-use and medical analytical frameworks are linked. Medical lab residual-sample retention of at least 14 days after sending the final report (R9-17-404, prior-session summary) and the R9-18-309(C) cross-reference to R9-17-317.01 are Verify against current Ch. 17 text.

Testing table — what is confirmed vs Verify. Table 3.1’s full per-analyte maximum allowable concentrations were not directly opened in this session (the table is in the AAC compilation, not in the R9-18-408 rule text). Do not publish the table below as Table 3.1 text; per-analyte limits are flagged accordingly.
