11.7 Packaging and labelling in operational detail

Rule — R9-18-310 (directly opened in the prior session; Cornell LII). Source: Cornell, R9-18-310 (adopted 27 A.A.R. 111, eff. 15 Jan 2021; amended 27 A.A.R. 696, eff. 1 May 2021; 29 A.A.R. 2453, eff. 1 Oct 2023; 29 A.A.R. 3532, eff. 18 Oct 2023; 30 A.A.R. 3451, eff. 1 Nov 2024) — accessed 31 Oct 2026 (prior-session open; re-cited).

  • Retail (R9-18-310(A)): product provided by a retail site to a consumer must comply with §§36-2854.01 and 36-2860(A); be labelled with: (a) establishment licence number; (b) amount, strain, batch number; (c) form of product; (d) weight as applicable; (e) potency per Table 3.1 testing results — mg per designated unit or % total THC per R9-18-408(F)(3)(b)(i), total CBD per (F)(3)(b)(ii), and any other cannabinoid for which the establishment makes an effect claim; (f) the verbatim warning: “ARIZONA DEPARTMENT OF HEALTH SERVICES’ WARNING: Marijuana use can be addictive and can impair an individual’s ability to drive a motor vehicle or operate heavy machinery. Marijuana smoke contains carcinogens and can lead to an increased risk for cancer, tachycardia, hypertension, heart attack, and lung infection. Marijuana use may affect the health of a pregnant woman and the unborn child. KEEP OUT OF REACH OF CHILDREN”; (g) for a marijuana product, ingredients in order of abundance; (h) a QR code linking to a webpage containing: the strain; the statement “Using marijuana during pregnancy could cause birth defects or other health issues to your unborn child”; distribution chain information including the establishment name, and if not cultivated by the establishment, the name and licence/registry number of the cultivator, and if not infused/prepared by the establishment, the name and licence/registry number of the infuser/preparer; a link to the final testing report under R9-18-410(B)(3); if applicable, the THC extraction method; and the date of harvest and, if applicable, manufacture. And (3) the product must be placed in child-resistant packaging on exit from the establishment.
  • Business-to-business (R9-18-310(B)): product provided to another establishment/dispensary must be labelled with licence number, amount/strain/batch, and dates of harvest or sale and, if applicable, manufacture; and a copy of testing-facility results must be provided to the receiving establishment/dispensary.
  • QR webpage retention (R9-18-308(A)(13)): maintain the QR link and webpage for at least 30 calendar days after the last date the retail site provides the product to which it pertains.

Statute — §36-2854.01 (directly opened this session). Source: azleg.gov, §36-2854.01 — accessed 31 Oct 2026. Not later than 31 Dec 2023, any marijuana product packaging labelled for sale shall include a consumer-scannable THC QR code or similar technology linking to a webpage displaying for the specific product: (1) harvest date; (2) THC strain; (3) THC extraction method; (4) a laboratory report of impurities containing at least heavy metals and agrochemicals; (5) manufacture date; (6) distribution chain including at least a point of intended sale; (7) the warning “using marijuana during pregnancy could cause birth defects or other health issues to your unborn child.” Note the statute’s list and R9-18-310(A)(2)(h)’s list differ in detail (the rule adds cultivator/infuser names and numbers and the final testing report link; the statute adds the impurities report and point of intended sale) — both must be satisfied; do not present either list as the whole requirement.

Statute — §36-2860 (directly opened this session). Source: azleg.gov, §36-2860 — accessed 31 Oct 2026. An establishment may not: (1) package/label in a false or misleading manner; (2) manufacture or sell products resembling the form of a human, animal, insect, fruit, toy, or cartoon; (3) sell/advertise with names resembling or imitating food or drink brands marketed to children, or otherwise advertise to children. Violation is subject to disciplinary action under §36-2854(B).

What the rule does NOT require (guides vs rule text): Opaque / resealable / tamper-evident: neither R9-18-310, §36-2854.01, nor §36-2860 as opened requires every immediate consumer package to be opaque, resealable, or tamper-evident. The statutory requirements are “clearly and conspicuously labeled containers” (§36-2854(A)(5)(a)) and “child-resistant packaging on exit” (§36-2854(A)(5)(b), R9-18-310(A)(3)). A secondary packaging guide agrees Arizona’s principal adult-use rules do not expressly require opaque/resealable/tamper-evident for every package (Sana Packaging, Arizona guide — accessed 31 Oct 2026). Other guides claiming a universal cannabis symbol, Poison Prevention Packaging Act/CPSC compliance, or additional warnings (intoxicating/habit-forming, impairs concentration/coordination/judgment, 21+ only) go beyond the texts opened — Verify before adopting any of them. “Child-resistant” is defined in §36-2850(2) as designed/constructed to be significantly difficult for children under five to open and not difficult for normal adults to use properly. Source: azleg.gov, §36-2850 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

11.8 Manufacturing / food-establishment rules

  • Edibles (R9-18-313, directly opened via search extract and Cornell): before preparing, selling, or otherwise transferring a marijuana-infused edible food product, obtain a licence or permit as a food establishment under 9 A.A.C. 8, Article 1; if the establishment prepares the edibles, prepare per 9 A.A.C. 8, Article 1; if another establishment/dispensary prepares them, ensure that preparer holds a current 9 A.A.C. 8, Article 1 licence/permit; if the establishment sells/transfers edibles, ensure they are sold/transferred per 9 A.A.C. 8, Article 1, comply with §36-2854(A)(7) (≤10 mg THC per serving, ≤100 mg per package), and if packaged as more than one serving, are scored/delineated into standard serving sizes and of homogeneous consistency. The establishment is responsible for the content and quality of any edible sold/dispensed by it. 9 A.A.C. 8, Article 1 incorporates the FDA Food Code 2017 (no future editions). Sources: Cornell, R9-18-313; Arizona Memory Project, Administrative Register extract (PDF) — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • Commercial devices: see §11.3 (R9-18-316(D), §3-3451).
  • No third-party GMP audit requirement found in the rules opened (secondary audit guide states Arizona requires the food-establishment licence and cleaning/sanitation rule, with ADHS inspection, and no third-party audit — ASI Food Safety, GMP audit for cannabis — accessed 31 Oct 2026; treat as secondary, not rule text).

11.9 Transport between licensee sites

Source: R9-18-312(C)–(H) (Justia, directly opened this session) — Justia, R9-18-312 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

  • A facility agent may transport between: the establishment’s retail/cultivation/manufacturing sites; that site and another establishment; that site and a Ch. 17 dispensary; the retail site and a consumer (per §36-2854(D) and R9-18-312.01, and only if the property owner has not notified the establishment that deliveries are not allowed at that property); and that site and an ADHS-licensed testing facility or Ch. 17 Art. 4 laboratory.
  • Trip plan (the manifest-equivalent; no separate form titled “manifest” was located in the rules opened — Verify): before transport, the agent must complete a trip plan (agent in charge; vehicle licence plate; date and start time; description of marijuana/plants/products; anticipated stops with locations and estimated arrival/departure times; anticipated route) and provide a copy to the establishment.
  • During transport: carry the trip plan; use a vehicle with current Arizona DMV registration (Title 28, Ch. 7, Art. 2), without any marijuana identification, equipped with GPS or another means for the establishment to track current location at any time, capable of providing electronic information about where the vehicle has been during at least the previous 90 days, with operational video surveillance/recording showing the interior including the driver’s seat and the product location, turned on for the duration of a trip while product is in the vehicle, storing the recording at least 30 calendar days or transmitting it at recording to a location storing it at least 30 calendar days, and with a locked compartment; have a means of communication with the establishment; notate arrival/departure times per stop; store product in the locked compartment, not visible.
  • After transport: enter end time and any trip-plan changes on the plan and provide the updated plan to the establishment. The establishment must maintain (D)(2) and (F) documents at least two years after the documentation date, provide a copy of the trip plan to a testing facility when transporting a sample there, and provide copies to ADHS on request. An establishment shall not transport to a consumer except as specified in R9-18-312.01.

11.10 Delivery operations in detail — R9-18-312.01 (directly opened in full this session)

Source: Cornell, R9-18-312.01 (new section, exempt rulemaking 30 A.A.R. 3451, effective 1 Nov 2024) — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

  • Vehicle administration (A): maintain a list of delivery vehicles (make, model, licence plate); keep a daily log of vehicle usage including date, time period of usage, and retail price of marijuana/plants/products transported during a trip; make a delivery vehicle available at the retail location for Department inspection within two hours after a Department request. (Plus R9-18-312(E)(2) vehicle requirements in §11.9.)
  • Order conditions (B): no transport to a consumer unless: (1) the retail site received the order from the consumer during the retail site’s regular hours of operation as posted per R9-18-308(A)(15); (2) the consumer provides name and date of birth, the identifying number on the document to be used to verify age on delivery, and the property address (plus apartment number if applicable); (3) a facility agent at the retail site documents the order (date/time; agent taking the order; consumer name, date of birth, ID number, delivery address; amount and retail price ordered; total retail price); (4) a copy of the order is attached to the R9-18-312(D)(1) trip plan; (5) delivery originates at the retail site; (6) before transfer, the delivering agent ensures the individual accepting delivery is the ordering consumer, verifies identity and age per R9-18-309(A)(1), complies with R9-18-309(A)(2)–(3) (testing results available if requested; ≤1 oz / ≤5 g concentrate), and obtains the consumer’s hand-written signature on the order.
  • Transport conditions (C): no delivery to property owned or leased by the United States, this state, a political subdivision of this state, or the Arizona Board of Regents; no delivery to an address whose owner has informed the establishment delivery is not permitted there; no more than a total retail price of $10,000 of marijuana/plants/products for delivery to a consumer in a vehicle; only product associated with orders made per (B)(1) may be in the vehicle (no extra/unallocated inventory — matching §36-2854(D)(4)).
  • Undelivered product (D): the delivering agent must return to the retail site at completion of the trip plan if any ordered product has not been transferred and remains in the vehicle; and must not pick up or otherwise receive product from a consumer for transport to the establishment once a delivery has been completed according to subsection (B)(7). Anomaly flagged: the Cornell rendering of (B) contains only (B)(1)–(6); the (B)(7) cross-reference in (D)(2) does not resolve — Verify against the official AAC.
  • Statutory frame (§36-2854(D), directly opened): delivery rules may not limit the distance between delivery location and originating retail location; may not limit the number of delivery vehicles; must require delivery logs and inspections; dual licensees must comply. Delivery was unlawful until rules were adopted; rules were due not later than 1 Jan 2025 and took effect 1 Nov 2024. Source: azleg.gov, §36-2854 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • Medical delivery (secondary): ADHS medical delivery guidelines (GD-107-PHS-EDC, cited by LegalClarity) require verification of the registry card through the electronic verification system before approving delivery and again on arrival; a designated caregiver (21+) may receive on a patient’s behalf. The guideline document itself was not opened — Verify. Source: LegalClarity, Arizona dispensary delivery — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

11.11 Inspections and enforcement

  • Inspection power (§36-2854(B)(3), directly opened): ADHS may, at any time during regular hours of operation, visit and inspect a marijuana establishment, testing facility, or dual licensee to determine compliance; ADHS shall make at least one unannounced visit annually to each facility licensed under the chapter. Source: azleg.gov, §36-2854 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • Denial/suspension/revocation (§36-2854(B)(1)): subject to Title 41, Ch. 6, Art. 10, ADHS may deny an application or deny/suspend/revoke in whole or part any registration/licence if the party (or its officer, agent, or employee) violates the chapter or a rule, or has been/is/may continue to be in substantial violation of licensing/registration requirements such that public health or safety is in immediate danger.
  • Civil penalty (§36-2854(B)(2)): subject to Title 41, Ch. 6, Art. 10, and unless another penalty is provided elsewhere in the chapter, up to $2,000 for each violation; each day a violation occurs is a separate violation; in determining the amount ADHS shall consider the factors in §36-2816(H) (factors not opened — Verify); penalties deposited in the Smart and Safe Arizona Fund (§36-2856).
  • Establishment licence (R9-18-317, directly opened): ADHS shall deny if a principal officer/board member has been convicted of an excluded felony or is under 21, or the application/establishment does not comply with Ch. 28.2 and Ch. 18; may deny if a PO/BM did not obtain approval to operate within 18 months after issuance, served as PO/BM for a dispensary/establishment that had its certificate/licence revoked, or provides false/misleading information; may suspend/revoke if the establishment provides false/misleading information, operates before obtaining approval to operate, diverts to a person/entity not allowed to possess under Ch. 28.1/28.2, acquires from an entity not allowed to possess, if a PO/BM has an excluded-felony conviction or provides false/misleading information, or if the establishment does not comply with Ch. 28.2/Ch. 18 or does not implement its application policies/procedures/statements. Denial/suspension/revocation notice must state specific reasons and (for suspension/revocation) the Title 41, Ch. 6, Art. 10 review process. Source: Cornell, R9-18-317 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • Testing-facility licence (R9-18-415, search extract): shall deny if an owner has an excluded-felony conviction or is under 21, or the application/facility does not comply with Ch. 28.2/Ch. 18; may deny for false/misleading information by an owner, or for failure to demonstrate compliance for a testing parameter/analyte; may suspend/revoke if the facility provides false/misleading information, begins R9-18-311 testing before obtaining Department approval, diverts to or acquires from a person/entity not allowed to possess, if an owner has an excluded-felony conviction or provides false/misleading information, or if the facility does not comply with Ch. 28.2/Ch. 18, does not comply with a corrective action plan submitted under R9-18-406(F)(6)(b), or does not implement its application policies/procedures/statements; may revoke approval of a testing parameter for continued non-demonstration of compliance. Source: Justia, R9-18-415 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • Facility-agent licence (R9-18-205, directly opened): shall deny if the agent does not meet the §36-2850 “marijuana facility agent” definition; may deny for prior revoked registry card or agent licence, or false/misleading information; may suspend/revoke and assess a civil penalty for diversion, excluded-felony conviction, false/misleading information, or knowing violation of Ch. 28.2/Ch. 18 (or Ch. 28.1/Ch. 17 if acting as a dual-licensee dispensary agent). Notice with specific reasons and Title 41, Ch. 6, Art. 10 review process required. Source: Cornell, R9-18-205 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • Citation process / plans of correction: no marijuana-specific citation or plan-of-correction rule was located in Ch. 18 in this session — Verify. ADHS’s general licensing complaint-investigation process (official ADHS document, not marijuana-specific) states: complaints are triaged and assigned a priority and due date within 1–2 business days; depending on priority, complaints are due between 2 business days and the next on-site visit (which may be the next annual compliance inspection); complaint investigations are unannounced; citations are documented in a Statement of Deficiencies sent to the licensee within 30 business days after investigation; if enforcement is necessary, the SOD is issued with a Notice of Enforcement and the case is referred to the Enforcement Team (fines, agreement terms, or legal orders such as intent to revoke or assess civil money penalties). Source: ADHS complaint investigation process (PDF) — accessed 31 Oct 2026. Run 1 Part 5’s 2024 Citation Report figures (603 citations to 188 licences; 68 resulting in enforcement) are carried forward from Run 1, not re-verified here.
  • Unauthorised delivery penalty (§36-2854(E)): in addition to any other penalty, an individual/entity delivering in a manner not authorized by the chapter shall pay $20,000 per violation to the Smart and Safe Arizona Fund; enforceable by the Attorney General. Source: azleg.gov, §36-2854 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

11.12 Recalls

Process (ADHS Directors Blog, official ADHS): if a product has been distributed publicly, the Arizona State Health Laboratory Compliance Team checks it for Table 3.1 contaminants relevant to the product (e.g., inhalable products tested for Aspergillus, edibles not); lab inspections/audits are scheduled annually, with an unannounced inspection if a complaint is received; if discrepancies appear between test results and equipment documentation, inspectors investigate, including checking instrument logs of failed tests; once a recall is determined to be required, ADHS Licensing contacts the facility to determine whether product was sold to the public or remains to be quarantined; the facility answers a questionnaire (product name, harvest date, products used to grow it); typically the original sample is sent to two different laboratories for repeat testing — if negative, the product can be sold and no recall is necessary. Source: ADHS Director’s Blog, recalls — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

Bulletin fields and the dispensary-name rule (same source): ADHS includes a brief explanation of how contamination was discovered, the cultivator’s name, the product name, the product type, and the batch number (which consumers can find on the product label). Dispensary names are NOT included because the list may not be comprehensive and could give consumers a false sense of security. If a recalled product is held, the consumer should dispose of it, try to return it, or hold it pending retest/update; recalls issued in an abundance of caution may later be lifted on repeat testing.

Recent examples (GovDelivery bulletins, official ADHS): 18 Dec 2025 voluntary recall for possible Spinosad pesticide contamination (distillates and products made from them; discovered during a routine Kaycha Labs inspection where failed Spinosad tests had been reported as passing) — ADHS bulletin, 18 Dec 2025 — accessed 31 Oct 2026; 10 Feb 2026 voluntary recall for possible Aspergillus (Legacy & Co Super Yuzu batch K-2025-19-Z1-SYUZ; Nature’s Wonder Trap Queen batch 0701R30TQ) — ADHS bulletin, 10 Feb 2026 — accessed 31 Oct 2026; the Super Yuzu recall was lifted 2 Mar 2026 after two laboratories returned negative results — ADHS bulletin, 2 Mar 2026 (lifted) — accessed 31 Oct 2026. Note the lifted-recall bulletin describes a second-lab-negative → third-lab-decisive sequence under R9-17-317.01/R9-18-311, which conflicts with the current R9-18-311(C) two-facility text as rendered on Cornell — Verify which retest sequence currently governs.

Consumer batch check: match the batch number on the product label against the ADHS bulletin table (Licence Holder/Cultivator, Brand, Product, Batch, Product Type, Contaminant); use the R9-18-310 QR code to reach the product webpage and the linked final testing report. No ADHS batch-lookup database was located — Verify; AZCareCheck is the ADHS licence-search tool (Run 1 Part 5).

11.13 Employee rules

  • Registration (§36-2855, directly opened): a facility agent shall be registered with ADHS before working at an establishment or testing facility; application on a Department form with a reasonable nonrefundable fee, plus either a current Level I fingerprint clearance card (§41-1758.07) or a full fingerprint set for state/federal criminal records check (§41-1750, Pub. L. 92-544; DPS destroys fingerprints after the check); ADHS issues a facility agent card valid for two years if criteria are met; an agent may be employed by/associated with any establishment/testing facility, with prompt mutual notice obligations on association and disassociation; a dual licensee’s dispensary agent who has applied for facility-agent registration may serve as a facility agent until ADHS approves or rejects the application. Source: azleg.gov, §36-2855 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • Definition (§36-2850(22)): “marijuana facility agent” means a principal officer, board member, or employee of an establishment or testing facility who is at least 21 and has not been convicted of an excluded felony offense. Source: azleg.gov, §36-2850 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • Application (R9-18-201, directly opened): applicant must be at least 21; submit name, date of birth, residence and Arizona mailing addresses, county, ID number, signature/date, copy of qualifying ID (Arizona driver’s licence/ID issued on/after 1 Oct 1996, Ch. 17 registry card, facility agent licence, U.S. passport page/card, or pre-Oct 1996 licence/ID plus citizenship proof), current photograph, fingerprints or Level I clearance card, attestation of no excluded-felony conviction, attestation of truth, and the R9-18-102 fee. Source: Cornell, R9-18-201 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • On-person card and linkage (R9-18-308(A)(6),(8)): all principal officers, board members, employees, and volunteers must maintain valid facility agent licences linked to the establishment through the Department’s electronic system; each agent must have the licence in immediate possession when working/volunteering at the retail, cultivation, or manufacturing site or when transporting. Written notice to ADHS (including event date) within 10 working days when an agent ceases to serve as PO/BM, be employed, or volunteer (R9-18-308(A)(10)); same 10-working-day rule for testing facilities (R9-18-405(10)). Sources: R9-18-308 and R9-18-405 above.
  • Principal officer / board member disclosure (≥10%): §36-2854(A)(1)(a) provides an application for an establishment or testing-facility licence may not require disclosure of the identity of any person entitled to less than 10% of the profits of a publicly traded corporate applicant — i.e., the disclosure line is drawn at 10% for publicly traded applicants. Whether a general ≥10% PO/BM disclosure rule applies to all applicants beyond this provision was not separately located — Verify. Source: azleg.gov, §36-2854 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • Training: R9-18-308(A)(2)(b) requires establishments to develop, document, and implement policies/procedures for training of facility agents, including the requirements of Ch. 28.2 and Ch. 18; R9-18-405(5)(a) requires testing facilities to do the same (job descriptions, supervision, ongoing function-specific training, confidentiality training, periodic performance evaluations including rotating proficiency testing, disciplinary actions). No standalone training curriculum, hour count, or certification is stated in the rules opened — Verify; do not invent one. §36-2854(A)(8) requires ADHS rules ensuring the health, safety, and training of establishment and testing-facility employees. Sources: R9-18-308, R9-18-405, §36-2854 above.
  • Social-equity licensee community-benefit policies (R9-18-308(A)(2)(g)): an establishment licensed under §36-2854(A)(1)(f) (the 26 social-equity licences) must document in policies how it will provide a benefit to one or more communities disproportionately affected by enforcement of Arizona’s previous marijuana laws, such as through specific hiring/interning practices or donation of a percentage of gross profits to unaffiliated nonprofit, community-based organizations focusing on social or health inequities in a community. Source: R9-18-308 above.

11.14 Insurance / banking operations constraints

None found in the rules opened. No rule in Title 9, Chapter 18 as opened requires a marijuana establishment or testing facility to hold liability, casualty, or product insurance, or to bank with a particular institution. Do not invent an insurance requirement. (Contrast: some other states’ rules do require insurance; Arizona’s Ch. 18 sections opened — R9-18-308, 312, 314, 315, 316, 317, 405, 415 — contain no such requirement.) Federal baseline carried forward: no federal cannabis banking law; Schedule III/280E treatment applies to state-licensed medical activity only (see Part 12.1). Verify a full-text search of Ch. 18 for “insurance” before final publication, since absence was established by section-by-section reading, not a compiled search.

PART 12 — CONSUMER AND SOCIAL RULES (for consumers and the public)

12.1 Federal baseline as it reaches the consumer (given, not re-researched)

State-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); all adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I; IRS 280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity while adult-use remains fully subject to 280E. For a dual licensee (holding both a nonprofit medical dispensary registration and an establishment licence), Schedule III/280E treatment applies to medical activity only — the licensee must split activity; adult-use sales remain Schedule I and fully 280E-subject. If any tribe operates a medical programme, whether it counts as “state-licensed” is Verify (carried from Run 1). Hemp dates carried forward: PL 119-37 §781 effective 12 Nov 2026, limited by PL 119-103 §2019 until 11 Dec 2026, after which the full total-THC hemp definition and 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply; USDA DEA-lab testing enforcement delayed to 31 Dec 2026; federal medical appropriations rider through 11 Dec 2026; no federal cannabis banking law. No Arizona response to the federal 12 Nov/11 Dec 2026 hemp dates was found in Run 1 — still Verify/not found.

12.2 Consumer purchase rules

  • Adult-use, in store (R9-18-309(A), directly opened): before selling/transferring to a consumer, the facility agent shall: (1) verify the consumer’s age through one of the documents in §4-241(K); (2) make available the results of testing required in R9-18-311, if requested by the consumer; (3) ensure the amount does not exceed one ounce of marijuana, with not more than five grams in the form of a marijuana concentrate. Product must be sold/transferred in a container made of material that will not react with or leach into the product (R9-18-309(B)), and must meet R9-17-317.01 (R9-18-309(C) — the medical labelling/analysis rule cross-applied to adult-use sales as written; Verify the current R9-17-317.01 text). Source: Cornell, R9-18-309 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • What a consumer must show (adult-use): a §4-241(K) document proving age 21+. “Consumer” is defined in §36-2850(4) as an individual at least 21 who purchases marijuana/products. Source: azleg.gov, §36-2850 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • Medical: 2.5 oz per 14 days (Run 1), with the dispensary verifying the registry card through the state electronic verification system; a medical patient must show a valid Arizona registry identification card plus government-issued photo ID (secondary delivery-guideline summary; in-store verification-system mechanics per Run 1 Part 1/Part 5). Visiting patients: §36-2804.03(C) gives possession/use protection but does not authorise obtaining marijuana from an Arizona nonprofit dispensary (Run 1).
  • Delivery purchase limits: same as in-store — adult-use 1 oz / ≤5 g concentrate per transaction (R9-18-312.01(B)(6)(c) incorporates R9-18-309(A)(3)); medical 2.5 oz per 14 days with electronic-verification checks (secondary guideline summary — Verify). Delivery order must be taken during posted retail hours, originate at the retail site, and be handed only to the ordering consumer on ID/age verification plus hand-written signature (R9-18-312.01(B)).
  • Under-21: no purchase by, and no sale/transfer/provision to, a person under 21 — §36-2851(4)–(5). Source: azleg.gov, §36-2851 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • Purchase for resale: no consumer resale right exists in Chapter 28.2; lawful sale requires an establishment licence (§36-2854(A)), and adult-use gifting is allowed only without remuneration and not advertised (§36-2852, Run 1). A standalone “purchase for resale” consumer offence in Ch. 28.2 was not located — Verify; unlicensed sale falls under the criminal code (§13-3405, Run 1 context).

12.3 Consumption rules

  • Private residence lawful: adult-use possession/use and home cultivation are lawful within §36-2852 limits (Run 1); a private residence is not a “public place” or “place of employment” under §36-601.01 unless used as a childcare, adult day care, or health care facility. Source: azleg.gov, §36-601.01 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • Public smoking — petty offence: §36-2853(C) — “A person who smokes marijuana in a public place or open space is guilty of a petty offense.” §36-2851(8)(a) likewise does not allow any person to smoke marijuana in a public place or open space. “Public place” has the §36-601.01 meaning (§36-2850(29)); “open space” means a public park, public sidewalk, public walkway, or public pedestrian thoroughfare (§36-2850(27)). Sources: azleg.gov, §36-2853; azleg.gov, §36-2851; azleg.gov, §36-2850 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • Smoke-Free Arizona Act scope — resolved: §36-601.01(A)(11) defines “smoking” as inhaling, exhaling, burning, or carrying/possessing any lighted tobacco product — so the Smoke-Free Act’s own definition does not cover marijuana smoke, and (per the Attorney General’s 2014 e-cigarette opinion as summarised secondarily) does not cover vaping devices either. Marijuana public smoking is banned independently by §36-2851(8)(a) and §36-2853(C), not by the Smoke-Free Act. Local ordinances may be more restrictive (§36-601.01(M)); e.g., Tempe’s vaping restrictions are secondary claims — Verify locality by locality. Sources: §36-601.01 above; secondary summary LegalClarity, Arizona smoking laws — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • “Smoke” vs vaping — important nuance: §36-2850(31) defines “smoke” for Chapter 28.2 as inhaling, exhaling, burning, carrying, or possessing any lighted marijuana or lighted marijuana products, whether natural or synthetic. Vaping (unlighted, heated) therefore falls outside the Chapter 28.2 “smoke” definition as written. Consequences: (a) public vaping of marijuana is not squarely within the §36-2853(C) petty offence text (“smokes”) — Verify before advising; do not present public vaping as expressly criminalised statewide; (b) vaping is still banned in vehicles by §36-2851(8)(b) (see §12.4), and on any property whose owner/occupier prohibits it (§36-2851(7)), and may be banned by local ordinance. Source: azleg.gov, §36-2850 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • Non-smoked public consumption (e.g., eating an edible in public): no provision in §36-2851/§36-2853 as opened expressly bans eating/drinking a marijuana product in a public place — the bans are framed on “smoke” and on consumption in vehicles. Verify — do not state that public edible consumption is lawful without that check, and note property owners and local rules may still prohibit it.
  • Consumption lounges — no licence class exists: no consumption-lounge licence appears in Chapter 28.2 or in Title 9, Chapter 18, Articles 1–4 (§§R9-18-101 to R9-18-415; the article list contains no on-site consumption section). Secondary sources conflict and are not adopted: MPP’s state-by-state table claims Arizona allows on-site consumption but not sales at the same location (MPP, on-site consumption laws — accessed 31 Oct 2026); a 2026 lounge survey claims no dedicated state licence but private membership-based establishments may allow on-site use under private-property rules (Marijuana Herald, lounges 2026 — accessed 31 Oct 2026); a law-firm Q&A answer claims consumption must occur at home and a retail facility qualifies as a public area (Ask A Lawyer On Call, Arizona Q&A — accessed 31 Oct 2026). Conclusion: no ADHS lounge licence class — confirmed by the statute/rule structure; whether any private-membership consumption model is lawful in Arizona is Verify, and no lounge should be described as licensed. Local illustration (not statewide law): Eloy’s code provides “Marijuana shall not be consumed on the premises, including any accessory structures, parking lot or parking areas” for recreational facilities (Eloy code — accessed 31 Oct 2026).
  • Consumption on licensed premises: no rule opened authorises consumption at a retail site; the retail site’s lawful functions are sale/transfer, cultivation, and manufacture (§36-2850(21)). Combined with the public-place smoking ban (a retail floor open to the public is a public place under §36-601.01(A)(9)), on-premises smoking at a dispensary is not authorised — Verify for a direct rule prohibition, since none was located in Ch. 18.
  • Vehicles: see §12.4.
  • Government property: §36-2851(9) does not prohibit the state or a political subdivision from prohibiting/regulating conduct otherwise allowed by the chapter on property occupied, owned, controlled, or operated by it; delivery to such property is separately banned (R9-18-312.01(C)(1)). Federal property remains subject to federal law (Schedule I for adult-use; Schedule III only for state-licensed medical/FDA-approved products per the federal baseline).
  • Schools, healthcare, daycare: §36-2851(6) does not restrict employers, schools, day care centers, adult day care facilities, health care facilities, or corrections facilities from prohibiting/regulating chapter-allowed conduct on/in their properties. For medical cardholders, §36-2813(A) separately provides no school or landlord may refuse to enroll/lease or penalise a person solely for cardholder status, unless doing so would cause loss of a monetary/licensing benefit under federal law — i.e., status protection is not a right to use on the premises. Sources: azleg.gov, §36-2851; azleg.gov, §36-2813 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

12.4 Driving, vehicles, and in-vehicle storage

DUI statute (§28-1381, directly opened). Source: azleg.gov, §28-1381 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

  • (A)(1): unlawful to drive or be in actual physical control while under the influence of intoxicating liquor, any drug, a vapor-releasing substance containing a toxic substance, or any combination, if impaired to the slightest degree.
  • (A)(3): unlawful to drive or be in actual physical control while there is any drug defined in §13-3401 or its metabolite in the person’s body — the metabolite prong. AUDIT CORRECTION for marijuana: §36-2852(B) provides that a person with metabolites or components of marijuana in the body is guilty of violating §28-1381(A)(3) only if also impaired to the slightest degree — so for marijuana, (A)(3) guilt now requires slightest-degree impairment, and “no impairment proof required” must be deleted for marijuana cases.
  • (B): it is not a defence to an (A)(1) charge that the person is or has been entitled to use the drug under Arizona law.
  • (D): a person using a drug as prescribed by a Title 32-licensed medical practitioner authorised to prescribe is not guilty of an (A)(3) violation — this prescribed-drug defence does not itself cover AMMA certification (see Dobson below).
  • (C): conviction is a class 1 misdemeanor. First-offence sentence (I): not less than 10 consecutive days in jail (judge may suspend all but one day if the person completes a court-ordered screening/education/treatment programme or evidence-based psychotherapy), fine not less than $250, plus $500 prison-construction assessment and $500 public-safety-equipment assessment, traffic survival school, and possible ignition-interlock order.
  • No per-se THC limit appears anywhere in §28-1381 as opened; the 0.08/0.04 figures in the statute are alcohol concentrations only.

Dobson v. McClennen, 238 Ariz. 389, 361 P.3d 374 (Ariz. 2015) — secondary case summaries, Verify against the opinion: the Arizona Supreme Court held the AMMA does not immunise registered qualifying patients from (A)(3) prosecution, but provides an affirmative defence that the marijuana or its impairing metabolite was in a concentration insufficient to cause impairment, proven by the patient by a preponderance of the evidence; the patients in Dobson failed to establish it and their convictions were affirmed. The defence is therefore medical-patient-only, burden-on-defendant, and concentration-based — it is not a card = immunity rule, and it does not help an adult-use consumer. Sources: Studicata, Dobson brief; Phoenix Injury Law, DUI laws — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

Consumption in vehicles (§36-2851(8)(b)): the chapter does not allow any person to consume marijuana/products while driving, operating, or riding in the passenger seat or compartment of an operating motor vehicle, boat, vessel, aircraft, or other vehicle used for transportation. This covers edibles and vaping in a vehicle, not only smoking. Source: azleg.gov, §36-2851 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

Open-container / in-vehicle storage — re-checked, still none found for marijuana: Arizona’s open-container statute, §4-251, covers spirituous liquor only (secondary summary: LegalClarity, open-container law — accessed 31 Oct 2026). No Arizona statute or Ch. 18 rule located in this session imposes a marijuana open-container, sealed-container, trunk-storage, or inaccessible-compartment rule on consumers (contrast the locked-compartment rule for licensee transport/delivery, §11.9–11.10, which binds licensees, not consumers). One secondary law-firm page claims it is illegal to have an open container of marijuana or a smoking device in a vehicle — it cites no marijuana statute for that claim, and it is not adopted; it conflicts with the statute text. Conclusion: no Arizona marijuana open-container statute found — do not import another state’s rule. Prudent-practice advice in secondary sources (sealed container, trunk) is advice, not Arizona law.

12.5 Landlord / tenant and housing

  • Adult-use — property owner may prohibit: §36-2851(7) does not restrict the ability of an individual, partnership, LLC, private corporation, private entity, or private organization of any character that occupies, owns, or controls property to prohibit or regulate conduct otherwise allowed by the chapter on or in such property. A landlord may therefore prohibit smoking, vaping, or growing on the property by lease; breach is a lease matter (secondary summary: LegalClarity, Prop 207 rules — accessed 31 Oct 2026), and delivery can be blocked at an address by owner notice (R9-18-312.01(C)(2)). Source for the statute: azleg.gov, §36-2851 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • Medical — status protection, not use protection: §36-2813(A) — “No school or landlord may refuse to enroll or lease to and may not otherwise penalize a person solely for his status as a cardholder, unless failing to do so would cause the school or landlord to lose a monetary or licensing related benefit under federal law or regulations.” This protects status, not a right to use or cultivate on the premises. Source: azleg.gov, §36-2813 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • Proposed, not law: HB 2904 (57th Legislature, 1st Regular Session, 2025) would have added §33-1317.01 providing “A landlord may not terminate a tenant’s rental agreement because the tenant uses marijuana.” BillTrack50 records it as Dead 27 June 2025; its final status must be confirmed on azleg.gov before any further statement. Do not present HB 2904 as law. Sources: HB 2904 (proposed); BillTrack50, HB 2904 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • Public / federally assisted housing — Verify for Arizona-specific sources: no Arizona statute located in this session addresses marijuana in public housing. The federal position, stated secondarily, is that federal regulations require federally assisted housing providers to prohibit illegal drug use and permit termination for illegal controlled-substance use (24 CFR Part 5, Subpart I; 42 U.S.C. §13662), and marijuana remains federally illegal for adult-use (and, for medical, is Schedule III only as state-licensed medical activity per the federal baseline — whether that changes HUD treatment is Verify). Secondary summary: LegalClarity, Prop 207 rules — accessed 31 Oct 2026. Do not present federal housing rules as Arizona law.

12.6 Schools and youth

  • Under-21 possession tiers (§36-2853(B), directly opened). Source: azleg.gov, §36-2853 — accessed 31 Oct 2026. A person under 21 who possesses, consumes, transports, or transfers without remuneration 1 oz or less (≤5 g concentrate) or consumption paraphernalia: (1) first violation — civil penalty of not more than $100 to the Smart and Safe Arizona Fund, and in the court’s discretion up to 4 hours of drug education/counseling; (2) second violation — petty offense, and in the court’s discretion up to 8 hours of drug education/counseling; (3) third or subsequent violation — class 1 misdemeanor.
  • Fake ID (§36-2853(E)): under-21 misrepresentation of age by a written instrument of identification, or use of a fraudulent/false instrument, with intent to induce sale/transfer to the under-21 person: first violation petty offense; second or subsequent class 1 misdemeanor.
  • Solicitation (§36-2853(F)): under-21 soliciting another person to purchase in violation of the chapter: first violation petty offense; second or subsequent class 3 misdemeanor.
  • Over-limit possession (21+): more than the §36-2852 amount but ≤2.5 oz (≤12.5 g concentrate) is a petty offense (§36-2853(A)); unlicensed cultivation visible from public view without optical aids or outside a locked enclosed area: first violation petty offense, second or subsequent class 3 misdemeanor (§36-2853(D)).
  • School-zone siting floor: a locality cannot allow an establishment within 500 ft of a school/childcare/preschool (§36-2857, Run 1); the 1,000-ft billboard buffer in §36-2859(E) covers childcare centers, churches, substance-abuse recovery facilities, public parks/playgrounds, and preschool–grade 12 schools.
  • Advertising to minors (§36-2859(C)(1), directly opened). Source: azleg.gov, §36-2859 — accessed 31 Oct 2026. Advertising may not target under-21s, including: names resembling/imitating food/drink brands marketed to children; images/likenesses of toys, cartoons, animated/fictional characters including Santa Claus designed to appeal to or encourage under-21 consumption; images/visual representations of consumption; potency/THC levels; or a medium specifically appealing to under-21s with special attractiveness beyond general adult attractiveness. Further: no advertising at/on/within public airports, public transportation shelters, buses, trains, shuttles, or trams (§36-2859(C)(2)); electronic/social-media/website advertising only if at least 73.6% of the audience is expected to be 21+ (§36-2859(C)(3)); no health-related/effects statement known to be untrue (§36-2859(C)(4)); sporting-event sponsorship only if at least 73.6% of the audience is expected to be 21+ or the sponsored organization provides written approval (§36-2859(C)(5)); direct/individualised communication requires an age-affirmation method verifying 21+ before the dialogue (§36-2859(G)); advertising platforms may host only authorised, establishment-identified (name + licence/registration number), warning-bearing advertising (§36-2859(F)); communications targeted only to the established customer base or requested via opt-in with age affirmation are not “advertising” (§36-2859(M)).
  • Youth prevention / education funding — statute purposes confirmed, programmes Verify: justice reinvestment programmes under §36-2863(G)(1) include “public and behavioral health, including evidence-based and evidence-informed substance use prevention and treatment and substance use early intervention services” — so Smart and Safe Fund justice-reinvestment money (35% to county public health departments, 35% to ADHS for nonprofit grants, 30% to ADHS for important public health issues, §36-2863(C)) may fund youth prevention, but no specific youth programme, grant recipient, or amount was located in this session — Verify via the §36-147 annual expenditure report to the Joint Legislative Budget Committee (which must include expenditures by programme and a list of grants distributed by ADHS). Separately, §36-2817(E)(5) ($4,000,000 for expungement-outreach grants) and (E)(7) ($1,000,000 for education/community-outreach grants) appear in a FindLaw mirror — Verify against the azleg §36-2817 text before publication. Sources: azleg.gov, §36-2863; FindLaw, §36-2817 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

12.7 Expungement — §36-2862 (directly opened from azleg.gov this session)

Source: azleg.gov, §36-2862 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

  • Who qualifies (A): beginning 12 July 2021, an individual arrested for, charged with, adjudicated or convicted by trial or plea of, or sentenced for, any of the following offences based on or arising out of conduct occurring before the effective date of the section may petition the court to have the record of that arrest, charge, adjudication, conviction, or sentence expunged: (1) possessing, consuming, or transporting 2.5 oz or less of marijuana, of which not more than 12.5 g was concentrate; (2) possessing, transporting, cultivating, or processing not more than 6 marijuana plants at the individual’s primary residence for personal use; (3) possessing, using, or transporting paraphernalia relating to cultivation, manufacture, processing, or consumption of marijuana.
  • Court process (B): the court shall notify the prosecuting agency and allow it to respond within 30 days; the court may hold a hearing on request of either party or if it concludes there are genuine disputes of fact about eligibility; the court shall grant the petition unless the prosecuting agency establishes by clear and convincing evidence that the petitioner is not eligible; the court shall issue a signed order or minute entry granting or denying the petition with findings of fact and conclusions of law.
  • Effect if granted (C): the order/minute entry shall (a) vacate the judgment of adjudication/conviction if any; (b) state that it expunges any record of arrest, charge, conviction, adjudication, and sentence; (c) state that civil rights, including the right to possess firearms, are restored, unless the petitioner is otherwise ineligible for restoration on grounds other than a §36-2862(A) offence; (d) require the clerk to notify DPS, the prosecuting agency, and the arresting agency if applicable; (e) require the clerk to seal all records relating to the expunged matter, accessible only to the individual or the individual’s attorney. DPS shall seal and separate the expunged record, inform all appropriate state and federal law enforcement agencies, and — unless the petitioner is indigent — may charge a reasonable fee set by the DPS director to research and correct the criminal history record. Arresting/prosecuting agencies shall mark their files/records as expunged and not make them available as public records to anyone except the individual or their attorney.
  • Further effects (D)–(G),(I): an expunged matter may not be used in a subsequent prosecution by a prosecuting agency or court for any purpose (D); the individual may state they have never been arrested/charged/adjudicated/convicted/sentenced for the expunged crime (E); if denied, the petitioner may file a direct appeal under §13-4033(A)(3) (F); on motion, the court shall dismiss with prejudice any pending complaint/information/indictment based on a §36-2862(A) offence (including conduct before the chapter’s effective date), and the individual may thereafter petition to expunge the arrest/charge records — such a motion could be filed before 12 July 2021 (G); the Supreme Court may adopt implementing rules and sponsor public service announcements/notifications for potentially eligible individuals (H); a prosecuting agency may petition on behalf of any individual it prosecuted, and the Attorney General may petition on behalf of any individual (I).
  • Case note (prior session, Verify citation): State v. Santillanes (Ariz. Ct. App. 2022, via FindLaw) — superior court granted expungement of a facilitation-to-sell/transport conviction; the State appealed arguing the offence exceeded the 2.5 oz limit; the court held the State has no statutory authority to appeal a grant of expungement but may seek special-action review; no published §36-2862 cases had been found at that time. Source: FindLaw, Santillanes — accessed 31 Oct 2026 (prior-session source; citation/date Verify).
  • Proposed, not law: HB 2801 (57th Legislature, 2nd Regular Session, 2026, Rep. Volk) would amend §36-2862, including changing “conduct occurring before the effective date of this section” to conduct before 30 Nov 2020 and adding a new subsection J. Source: HB 2801 (proposed) — accessed 31 Oct 2026. Do not present HB 2801 as current law; confirm its final status on azleg.gov.
  • Statistics — official statewide 2025–2026 total: Unavailable. No official statewide total was located. Dated secondary county figures only (do not present as current statewide totals): Maricopa County Superior Court had granted 3,643 expungement petitions by late Aug 2021 (Arizona Agenda, expungement resource — accessed 31 Oct 2026); the Maricopa County Attorney’s Office had filed 10,607 expungement petitions for closed cases by early 2022 (High There, Maricopa expungements — accessed 31 Oct 2026); a secondary legal guide claims Maricopa alone had processed over 26,822 petitions as of Feb 2024 (LegalMatch, Arizona marijuana possession — accessed 31 Oct 2026) — Verify; no filing fee is charged in Maricopa County per secondary guides (Verify county by county). No filing deadline/sunset exists in §36-2862 as opened.

12.8 Social equity and justice reinvestment in consumer terms

Justice reinvestment fund (§36-2863, directly opened). Source: azleg.gov, §36-2863 — accessed 31 Oct 2026. The fund consists of monies deposited under §36-2856 plus interest, is continuously appropriated, does not revert to the general fund, is exempt from §35-190 lapsing, and is administered by the State Treasurer. After administrative costs, on or before 30 June and 31 Dec each year the Treasurer transfers the excess: 35% to county public health departments (by decennial-census population) to provide justice reinvestment programmes or distribute grants to qualified nonprofits for such programmes in that county; 35% to ADHS to distribute grants to qualified nonprofits providing justice reinvestment programmes statewide; 30% to ADHS to address important public health issues. Grants are exempt from Title 41, Ch. 23, and each grantee must file an annual report detailing use of granted monies. “Justice reinvestment programs” means initiatives focusing on: (1) public/behavioral health including evidence-based/informed substance-use prevention, treatment, and early intervention; (2) restorative justice, jail diversion, workforce development, industry-specific technical assistance/mentoring for economically disadvantaged persons in communities disproportionately impacted by high arrest/incarceration rates; (3) addressing underlying causes of crime, reducing drug-related arrests, reducing the prison population; (4) creating/developing technology and programmes to assist restoration of civil rights and expungement of criminal records.

Grant recipients: Verify / not located. No published list of §36-2863 grant recipients was located in this session. The accountability route is §36-147: ADHS must submit an annual expenditure report to the Joint Legislative Budget Committee by 1 July each year, including expenditures by programme and a list of grants distributed (per the azleg §36-147 text in search results — azleg.gov, §36-147 — accessed 31 Oct 2026).

Social equity licences (consumer-facing): 26 additional establishment licences under §36-2854(A)(1)(f), lottery 8 Apr 2022 (Run 1); social-equity licensees must document community-benefit policies (hiring/interning or gross-profit donations to unaffiliated nonprofits) under R9-18-308(A)(2)(g) (see §11.13). Operating count officially unavailable (Run 1); no ADHS fronting enforcement order found (Run 1). A 2026 introduced measure (SCR 1047) proposing an AG marijuana enforcement fund and receivership for social-equity licences is proposed only, not law — do not present as current law (LegiScan, SCR 1047 introduced — accessed 31 Oct 2026).

12.9 Public health: warnings, poison control, adverse events

  • Label warning (verbatim): see §11.7 (R9-18-310(A)(2)(f)).
  • Retail sign warnings (verbatim): see §11.3 (R9-18-308(A)(15)(c)) — pregnancy/breastfeeding danger warning plus Department of Child Safety reporting-risk warning.
  • Advertising warning (verbatim, §36-2859(D)): “Do not use marijuana if you are under twenty-one years of age or pregnant. Keep marijuana out of reach of children.” All printed warnings must occupy at least 10% of the advertising area in black font on a white background, except billboard warnings must be at least 10% of the largest type used. Source: azleg.gov, §36-2859 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • QR pregnancy warning (verbatim, §36-2854.01(7)): “using marijuana during pregnancy could cause birth defects or other health issues to your unborn child.” Source: azleg.gov, §36-2854.01 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.
  • Poison control / adverse-event reporting: no ADHS adverse-event reporting rule for consumers or licensees was located in this session — Verify / none found. The only poison-control contact located is the number printed in ADHS’s 10 Feb 2026 recall bulletin: Arizona Poison and Drug Information Center, 1-800-222-1222 (ADHS bulletin, 10 Feb 2026 — accessed 31 Oct 2026). Present it only as the number given in that bulletin, not as a general adverse-event reporting system.

12.10 Consumer complaints and checking a product

  • How to complain (general ADHS licensing process, official ADHS document): complaints received by ADHS are reviewed for jurisdiction (if ADHS lacks jurisdiction, the complainant is notified and the complaint closed; if another agency has jurisdiction and is unaware, ADHS may refer it); assigned a priority and due date within 1–2 business days; due between 2 business days and the next on-site visit depending on priority; complainants are contacted before the investigation; investigations are unannounced; citations are documented in a Statement of Deficiencies sent within 30 business days after investigation; enforcement referrals go to the Enforcement Team. Source: ADHS complaint investigation process (PDF) — accessed 31 Oct 2026. This is ADHS’s licensing-wide process, not a marijuana-specific rule — Verify whether a marijuana-specific complaint portal/form exists for adult-use establishments and testing facilities.
  • Medical dispensary complaints (secondary, Verify): a secondary guide states complaints about an operating nonprofit medical dispensary may be filed by anyone with knowledge/concerns about a suspected AMMA/rules violation via an ADHS complaint form, and that per R9-17-309(C) complaints may not be filed anonymously (Scales Galore, medical marijuana selling guide — accessed 31 Oct 2026). The ADHS form URL and the adult-use equivalent were not opened — Verify.
  • Checking a batch: (1) read the batch number on the product label; (2) compare it against ADHS GovDelivery recall bulletins (fields: Licence Holder/Cultivator, Brand, Product, Batch, Product Type, Contaminant — dispensary names deliberately excluded, see §11.12); (3) scan the R9-18-310 QR code for the product webpage, distribution chain, and linked final testing report; (4) ask the retail agent for the R9-18-311 testing results, which must be made available if requested (R9-18-309(A)(2)); (5) contact the dispensary/establishment of purchase with questions (per ADHS recall bulletins).
  • Unlicensed sellers: consumers should verify a seller holds an ADHS establishment licence/dispensary registration — ADHS publishes a current list of all establishments and testing facilities by name and licence number (§36-2854(L)). Source: azleg.gov, §36-2854 — accessed 31 Oct 2026.

RESEARCH NOTES — covering both runs (Parts 0–12)

Research date for both runs: 31 October 2026. Run 1 (Parts 0–6) was commissioned 31 October 2026 and delivered that day as a standalone file; Run 2 (Parts 7–12) was commissioned when Tyler replied “go” on 31 October 2026. This complete file is Run 1 plus Run 2, with this research-notes section added at the end. The standalone Run 1 file is preserved unchanged at ~/workspace/your_files/arizona-cannabis-hemp-run1/arizona-cannabis-hemp-run1-parts-0-6.html; corrections found in Run 2 are listed here and in the Run 2 text, not silently edited into the Run 1 sections. Read the Run 1 sections subject to the corrections below.

Scope and method

  • What this file is: a single self-contained HTML document, Parts 0–12, organised by part with consistent field names, tables where they help, and source links and dates accessed next to facts. It uses only inline CSS; no external stylesheet, script, or image is required to read it offline. Links are to sources, not embedded content.
  • What it is not: a legal opinion, a licence register, a price list, or a property listing service. It does not list every licence holder (Part 5 explains why no complete public downloadable register exists), does not present a broker’s “fully permitted” claim as a verified permit (Part 8), and does not present a proposed or failed bill as law.
  • Federal baseline (user-given, applied in both runs, not re-researched): state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); all adult-use remains Schedule I; 280E generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity, adult-use fully subject; hemp PL 119-37 §781 effective 12 November 2026 but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026, after which the full total-THC definition and 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply; USDA DEA-lab testing enforcement delayed to 31 December 2026; federal medical appropriations rider through 11 December 2026; no federal cannabis banking law. Any source contradicting this baseline is flagged in the text; none was adopted over it.
  • Sourcing hierarchy applied: official sources first (azleg.gov statutes and bill/session-law pages, Secretary of State AAC/AAR, ADHS, AZDA, ADOR, AG opinions, Secretary of State ballot records, Arizona courts, official tribal sources, official city/county codes). Laws are quoted only from current official statute/regulation text where directly opened; where a quotation came from a mirror, a search result, or a secondary summary, the text says so and marks it Verify. Secondary sources are used only for non-law facts (market figures, polling, events, broker listings) with publication dates, preferring the most recent. Every fact carries a source link and date accessed in its section; a fact without a link in this file is a cross-reference to an earlier section, not a new unsourced claim.
  • Direct-opening limits (both runs): direct fetches of azleg.gov/ars/36/…htm failed in the Run 1 Parts 0–1 session; quotations there came from azleg viewdocument search results, azleg bill/session-law PDFs, and Justia/FindLaw/Cornell mirrors, and are marked Verify against the current official compilation. In Run 2, §36-2854, §36-2851, §36-2853, §36-2859, §36-2860, §36-2862, §36-2863, §36-2854.01, §36-2855, §28-1381, §36-601.01, §36-2813 and the USDA approved-plans list were directly opened, and R9-18-308/309/310/311/312/312.01/313/314/315/316/317/405/408/410 and R3-4-1002–1014 were directly opened via Cornell/Justia mirrors. Items that were only found in search in Run 2 are labelled “search result only” in Parts 9–10 and in the relevant Part 11–12 notes. A search-only item must not be quoted as directly read.
  • Same-name discipline: Glendale AZ vs CA, Peoria AZ vs IL, Mesa AZ vs CO, Kingman AZ vs KS, Payson AZ vs UT, Winslow AZ vs ME, Douglas AZ vs WY, Florence, Yuma AZ vs CO, Prescott, Nogales AZ vs Nogales, Sonora, San Luis AZ vs San Luis Río Colorado were kept distinct; City of Maricopa and Maricopa County are different jurisdictions and are treated as such in Part 3 and Part 8. Chino Valley and Camp Verde properties in Part 8 are in Yavapai County, Arizona.

Conflicts carried forward — do not resolve silently

The following conflicts were present in Run 1 and are not resolved by Run 2. They are listed here so a reader does not have to hunt through twelve parts to find them. Where a Run 2 section re-states a conflict, the Run 2 wording governs for that section; the underlying conflict remains open.

  1. Labs: 8 (ADHS July 2026 report) vs 7 (CannBus Q3 2026). Unresolved. Verify from a dated AZCareCheck extraction. (Parts 4–6, 9(a)(10).)
  2. Social-equity operating count: officially unavailable. 26 licences awarded; no current holder file; outcome tallies in secondary reporting conflict (AZCIR July 2023 11 corporate-owned / 7 investor-held / 8 original winners retaining equity; ~Oct 2023 13 opened, 1 independent, plus a 4+13+10=27 split that exceeds 26; Feb 2024 hearing claim 24/26 fully controlled). Do not sum or extrapolate. No ADHS fronting enforcement order found 2024–2026 — “not found”, not proof none exists. (Parts 4, 9, 12.8.)
  3. Rural licences: 13 (contemporaneous BDSA/MJBizDaily) vs 12 (IndicaOnline). Run 2 uses 13 and flags 12 as rejected, but the ADHS licence list was not opened to settle it. (Parts 4, 9.)
  4. Pharmacy denominator / licence ceiling: unavailable. No official registered-pharmacy count was opened; the secondary “~143 + 26 + 12” ceiling is rejected (wrong rural figure, no denominator). (Parts 4, 9(a)(7).)
  5. Annual sales series gaps: $40–107m between ADOR-derived secondary series (CannBus 2023 $1,462.0m / 2024 $1,357.4m / 2025 $1,224.0m vs ArizonaStateCannabis 2023 $1,376.4m / 2024 $1,250.5m / 2025 $1,184.3m; Headset POS 2024 $1,303.6m / 2025 $1,210.5m). ADOR PDF tables were not text-extracted; a direct ADOR September 2026 by-Period-Covered PDF pull is still required before publishing any 2026 monthly sales table. Aug–Sep 2026 period-covered sales not found. (Part 6.)
  6. Flower price: $4.03 vs $5.94 vs $10.28/g (Marijuana Herald / Headset Sep 2026 / Headset via CannBus Aug 2026). Direction of price compression is well-supported; level is not. Do not average. (Part 6.)
  7. HB 2179 effective date and vote: AUDIT RESOLVED. Session-law wording: “effective from and after June 30, 2026” (azleg summaries paraphrase as 1 July 2026). Three-fourths vote satisfied: House 51-3-6, Senate 28-1-1. (Parts 1, 9.)

  9. Social-equity application fee for the 2021–2022 round: $4,000 (ADHS checklist) vs $5,000 (some secondary sources). $4,000 adopted for that round; $5,000 flagged Verify. (Part 4.)
  10. Phoenix combined TPT: AUDIT RESOLVED to 9.1% current (8.6% pre-1 July 2025). Verify against ADOR (two-level retail threshold, Prop 104, Jan 2026 change noted). Other combined local rates in Part 3 are secondary and marked Verify. (Parts 2–3.)
  11. ArizonaStateCannabis patient table mixes total cardholders with qualifying patients and differs from ADHS by 186 in Jul 2026 — do not cite it as a patient series. CannBus “−37.4% Dec 2022→Aug 2026” does not reproduce (actual −46.7%). (Part 6.)

What Run 2 resolved (closed items — do not re-open without new evidence)

  • No-Metrc position — re-confirmed against R9-18-314 (§11.1).
  • Delivery $10,000 trip cap — confirmed in R9-18-312.01(C)(3) (§11.10).
  • Inventory retention 5 years (inventory documentation) and 30-calendar-day audit interval (§11.4, §11.16).
  • Advertising penalty split — $20,000 for non-establishments/dispensaries, discipline for establishments/dispensaries (§11.15).
  • RCH Wellness disposition — affirmed on standing, not a merits ruling on 500-ft timing (§9(b) case 6).
  • Cave/Sherri Dunn dismissal — granted from the bench, Oct 2025 (§9(b) case 8).
  • USDA tribal hemp plans — three, not two (§10(a)(6)).
  • SRPMIC dates and GRIC permit regime — corrected/qualified (§10(a)(1)–(2)).
  • Smoke-Free Act scope, open-container (none found), lounge licence class (none exists) — §12.3–12.4.
  • Hemp licence architecture in full — five types, research treatment, term/renewal, sampling, hot-crop band, corrective actions, notifications, seed rules, import/export notification (§7).
  • Operations architecture in full — security, physical plant, sanitation, inventory, waste, testing process, packaging/labelling, edibles food-establishment rule, trip plans, delivery, inspections/enforcement, recalls, employee rules, penalties, record retention (§11).
  • Consumer architecture in full — purchase limits, consumption, driving, landlord/tenant, schools/youth, expungement (§36-2862, directly opened), justice reinvestment, warnings, complaints/batch checks, quick-reference table (§12).
  • Part 8 verdict — zero properties meet the strict verified standard; 9 conditional, 2 excluded (§8.0). This is a verification finding, not a claim that no cannabis-zoned property exists.

Items deliberately NOT in this file (and why)

  • No complete licence-holder list. Part 5 establishes that the only complete current official named-holder source is AZCareCheck (search-only, daily, adult-use + labs only, no bulk download documented) and the only complete official count source is the ADHS Monthly Report (PDF, counts only). A holder list built from commercial directories would not be an official list and would misstate status.
  • No “verified” Part 8 property. Broker claims, “fully permitted” listing copy, and “green zoned” marketing language are not verifications. Part 8 grades every candidate CONDITIONAL or EXCLUDED and states the exact check that would change the grade.
  • No tribal dispensary list. No tribe was found operating a tribal-licensed commercial dispensary, and no commercial-directory tribal listing was adopted. Tribal hemp plans are not marijuana programmes and are not presented as such.
  • No averaged prices, summed tallies, or inferred counts. Where sources conflict (flower price, annual sales, social-equity outcomes, lab counts), the file keeps the conflicting figures side by side with flags rather than averaging, summing, or picking silently. Where a count cannot be derived (dual overlap, pharmacy ceiling, social-equity operating count), the file says Unavailable rather than deriving it.
  • No proposed bill as law. SB 1641 (2026 producers), SB 1725, SB 1128, SB 1476 (vetoed), SB 1556 (2025 hemp beverages), SB 1702, SB 1186 (2024, §3-320), HB 2801 (§36-2862), HB 2904 (landlord), SCR 1047 (social-equity AG fund), SB 1262 (2024 predatory agreements — failed 3/4 vote), SB 1299 (2025 Safe Community Enforcement Fund diversion — failed 3/4 vote), and SB 1713/SB 1057 (status Verify) are all labelled proposed, dead, vetoed, or failed as stated in their sections. The 2026 repeal initiative I-04-2026 was abandoned and is not on the 3 November 2026 ballot.
  • No personal contact details. Official public business contacts only (e.g., AZDA programme hotline and intake email in Part 7); no personal phone numbers or emails of individuals are reproduced, per the commissioning rules.

End of complete Arizona Cannabis & Hemp Research, Parts 0–12. Research date 31 October 2026. This file is self-contained: it uses only inline CSS and links out for sources; no external stylesheet, script, or image is required to read it offline. Corrections to this file should be made as dated amendments that name what they replace, not as silent edits — the corrections table above is the model.
